Executive Summary

Madagascar's third Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC 3.0) sets out an ambitious pathway towards a resilient, inclusive and low-carbon future for the period 2025-2035. It is aligned with the General State Programme, the national and sectoral strategies related to Climate Change, as well as with the commitments of the Paris Agreement and the Sustainable Development Goals.

A net carbon sink at global level (–108 800 Kt CO₂eq in 2023), the country nonetheless bears the full brunt of climate impacts. NDC 3.0 therefore sets out a twofold ambition: to preserve and strengthen this sequestration function while building resilience commensurate with the risks.

Total estimated cost: 13,86 billion USD over 2025-2035, broken down as follows:

  • Mitigation: 5,33 billion USD (38,5 %)

  • Adaptation: 7,98 billion USD (57,5 %)

  • Gender, Youth and Social Inclusion (GIS): 0,30 billion USD (2,2 %)

  • Coordination and Governance: 0,25 billion USD (1,8 %)

The measures will deliver a 34,0 % reduction in non-AFAT emissions relative to the reference scenario in 2035, i.e. 14 600 Kt CO₂eq avoided, while maintaining a net negative balance.

A- Mitigation targets by sector

Table 1: Mitigation targets by sector

Sector

Flagship targets for 2035

Energy

A 35 % reduction below BAU (923 kt CO₂eq avoided) through the deployment of 254 MW of large hydropower, 167 MW of solar and 8MW of onshore wind, as well as the installation of 1,4 million improved cookstoves and biogas systems.

Transport

A 25 % reduction below BAU (1 098 kt CO₂eq avoided) through the introduction of 6 000 electric buses, 10 000 cars, 20 000 electric motorcycles and tuk-tuks, bus rapid transit (BRT) systems, electric rail lines and the development of soft mobility.

Waste

Transformation into a genuine carbon sink, with a reduction of 10 900 Kt CO₂eq relative to BAU in 2035, through composting, recycling, energy recovery, anaerobic digestion and controlled incineration.

Industry

Near-complete decarbonisation with a decrease of 354 kt CO₂eq (63 % relative to BAU) through clinker substitution, conversion to gas, the use of high-efficiency motors and the adoption of low-carbon bricks.

Agriculture

A reduction of 951 kt CO₂eq (3,4 % below BAU) through the widespread adoption of rice cultivation systems with low emissions of methane (SRA, SDA, SRI), conservation agriculture and the deployment of rural biogas. Can we add something on livestock (enteric fermentation and manure management)

Forests/AFAT

Additional sequestration of 1 907 kt CO₂eq in 2030 and 2035 years (relative to BAU) through reforestation programmes, reduced deforestation, the development of agroforestry and assisted natural regeneration.

Status of Short-Lived Climate Pollutants (PCCDV)

In the scenario with mitigation measures, methane (CH₄) emissions will stand at 1 140 Gg in 2035, a reduction of 488 Gg relative to the reference scenario. Hydrofluorocarbon (HFC) emissions will reach 363 Gg in the same year, down by 69 Gg relative to BAU. As for black carbon (BC), its emissions remain almost stable (18 Gg in 2035) owing to the persistence of agricultural and forest fires, which are the dominant source and are difficult to reduce in the short term. These efforts contribute not only to mitigating global warming, but also to improving air quality and public health.

B- Adaptation targets by sector

Adaptation protects development gains and strengthens food security, health, infrastructure and ecosystems, while ensuring a just transition.

Table 2: Adaptation targets by sector

Sector

Flagship adaptation measures

Health, Water, Sanitation & Hygiene

1000 climate-resilient health facilities, health-climate early warning platform, 25 000 drinking water points, improved sanitation for 1,5 million households.

Water & GIRE

7 operational basin committees, 40 hydrometric stations, revised Water Code, 50 storage and regulation structures.

Risks, Disasters & Insurance

National risk financing strategy, contingency Fund increased to 20 billion MGA, climate insurance for 250 000 beneficiaries, national loss and damage register.

Agriculture & Food security

500 000 ha under sustainable practices (agroecology, conservation), 700 000 ha with adapted varieties, 3 000 km of rural tracks rehabilitated, 200 000 farmers insured.

Education

1 500 climate-resilient and accessible schools: school continuity plans in 100 % of CISCO climate-resilient schools; curricula integrating climate-environment/biodiversity and Disaster Risk Management 100 000 girls whose access to education and school retention is supported/year..

Social protection Adaptive safety nets for 120 000 households, climate-sensitive social registry, food reserves in 600 communes.

Infrastructure & Spatial Planning

Mandatory climate-resilient standards for all new public investment, urban drainage in Antananarivo and pilot cities, hydropower and road resilience.

Forests & Biodiversity

Restoration of 1,6 million hectares of terrestrial ecosystems, 16 000 ha of mangroves restored, 120 000 agroforestry holdings, protected areas strengthened (428 134 ha).

Coastal & Port Areas

Integrated coastal zone management plans, protection of the port of Toamasina, maritime early warning, restoration of natural defences (mangroves, dunes, reefs).

Loss and Damage

Madagascar is suffering growing climate losses that exceed its adaptation capacities. The country is committed to documenting, preventing and financing these losses through a structured national framework.

Table 3: National loss and damage framework

Area

Flagship measures

National loss register

Establishment of a digital register placed under the institutional governance of the ministry in charge of the environment / BNCCREDD+, with operational data collection coordinated by the BNGRC and statistical validation supported by INSTAT, covering 70 % of major events by 2030 and 100 % by 2035, interconnected with the systems of the BNGRC, the CPGU, the MEF and INSTAT. Data disaggregated by sex, age and disability.
Assessment and reporting

Carrying out standardised post-disaster assessments (PDNA/DAI) after each major event; publication of a consolidated annual report on economic and non-economic losses, insurance gaps and policy recommendations.

Financial preparedness and insurance

Strengthening of the national contingency Fund (20 billion MGA), extension of sovereign multi-hazard coverage (ARC, CERC, REPAIR) and deployment of climate insurance for 250 000 beneficiaries (crop farmers, livestock keepers, fishers).

Access to the international P&P Fund

Preparation and submission of at least 3 applications to the international Fund by 2030 and 6 by 2035, targeting as a priority the Grand Sud (drought) and coastal areas (erosion, flooding). Applications coordinated by the ministry in charge of the environment / BNCCREDD+ in collaboration with the ministry in charge of finance and the relevant sectoral institutions.

Non-economic losses Development

of a national doctrine for the recognition and quantification of intangible losses: long-term displacement, loss of cultural heritage, disappearance of endemic species, erosion of fisheries and social capital.

Strengthening of local capacities

Training of regions, communes, DREDD, decentralised BNGRC services and the relevant sectoral services in the collection, reporting and use of data relating to loss and damage in the collection and reporting of loss data; integration of dedicated modules into local contingency plans (pilot phase in 6 regions from 2026-2028, then national roll-out).

Estimated cost of annual losses: 100 million USD on average, with major episodes representing 2 to 4 % of GDP (e.g. Gezani: 2 billion USD). These figures do not include non-economic losses, which remain massive but insufficiently documented.

C- Cross-cutting priorities

Gender equality, the inclusion of young people and vulnerable persons, and a just transition are conditions for success.

NDC 3.0 recognises children, adolescents and young people, women, migrants and persons with disabilities as rights holders and as groups particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change, but also as essential actors in the transition towards resilient and equitable development. It is based on a human rights and child rights approach, in line with the Convention on the Rights of the Child and with General Comment No. 26 on children's rights and the environment, in order to ensure that the commitments on mitigation, adaptation, disaster risk reduction, climate finance and monitoring and evaluation contribute directly to the health, nutrition, education, protection and participation of every child and to their right to a clean, healthy and sustainable environment. It likewise recognises women as pillars of community resilience and of natural resource management, calling for their systematic inclusion in decision-making processes, land tenure security and access to climate finance mechanisms. NDC 3.0 incorporates disaggregated indicators, targeted measures for the economic empowerment of women and young people, and formal participation of these groups in climate governance.

GIS (Gender, Youth and Social Inclusion) targets by sector

Table 4: GIS (Gender, Youth and Social Inclusion) targets by sector

Sector

Priority GIS measures

Agriculture & Food systems

  • Land tenure security, with 40 % of certificate holders being women by 2035

  • At least 50 % women beneficiaries in climate financing mechanisms and insurance schemes

  • 35 000 women and young people supported through incubators and entrepreneurship training

Education

  • School continuity plans reducing post-disaster dropout by 80 %

  • Curricula integrating climate, environment and water-hygiene in 100 % of schools

  • Scholarships and school canteens for 200 000 girls by 2035

  • Orientation of 100 training streams towards green and blue jobs

Water, Sanitation & Hygiene (WASH)

  • Access to drinking water within less than 500 m for vulnerable households

  • Gender-balanced WASH committees (at least 40 % women)

  • 90 % of schools equipped with menstrual hygiene management facilities

  • Sanitation facilities that are resilient and accessible to persons with disabilities

Health

  • Mobile health services and continuity of sexual and reproductive health care in crisis situations

  • Training of 20 000 health workers on climate-gender dimensions and on gender-based violence

  • Integration of first aid and disability sensitivity into the health response

Forests & Biodiversity

  • 40 % women and young people in local protected area governance structures

  • 4 000 beneficiaries of alternative income-generating activities (agroforestry, beekeeping…)

  • Support for securing use rights and for inclusive community agreements

Coastal & Maritime Areas

  • Participatory risk mapping incorporating the local knowledge of women and young people

  • Formalisation of 25 000 jobs in artisanal fisheries and fisheries value chains

  • 7 000 artisanal fishers trained and equipped with appropriate early warning tools

  • Marine environmental education in 500 000 primary schools

Trade, Industry & SMEs

  • 50 % of SMEs receiving climate finance led by women

  • 40 000 young and women entrepreneurs supported in the circular economy and clean technologies

  • Administrative simplification targeting 50 % of women-led SMEs formalised

Energy

  • 40 % of households using clean cooking solutions, reducing the fuel collection burden on women

  • 35 000 green jobs created (solar maintenance, manufacture of improved cookstoves)

  • 50 % women and young people trained in renewable energy occupations

Spatial Planning & Infrastructure

  • 100 % of new public investments applying universal accessibility standards

  • 70 % of territorial planning documents integrating climate risks and hazard zoning

  • 50 000 green jobs generated by low-carbon and resilient infrastructure

Risk & Disaster Management

  • 85 % of vulnerable populations covered by multi-format early warning systems (local languages, accessibility)

  • 80 % of evacuation centres equipped with safe spaces and separate sanitation facilities

  • 60 % of adaptive social safety net beneficiaries are women heads of household

  • VBG prevention protocols integrated into the emergency response

D- Financial roadmap

The financing strategy combines the mobilisation of international funds, innovative instruments and national effort, drawing on the lessons of NDC 2.0.

Key principles:

  • Ambition-realism balance: 57,5 % of needs allocated to adaptation, reflecting the country's extreme vulnerability.

  • Clear national effort: unconditional financing of 250 million USD, of which 2.5 million USD contributed through the Green Budget (LFR 2026) and 247.5 million USD from private sector investment and national NGOs.

  • Innovative instruments: thematic sovereign bonds (blue and green), market mechanisms under Article 6, blue carbon from seagrass beds and mangroves, Public-Private Partnerships in energy, waste and irrigation.

  • Phasing: pipeline of bankable projects structured in waves (pilots, scaling up, mass deployment).
