F- Governance

Under the authority of the Ministry in charge of the Environment and of BNCCREDD+, implementation mobilises the sectoral ministries, BNGRC, CPGU, DGM, local authorities, the private sector and civil society. The revised CIME (2024) and MECIE (2025) decrees require climate risk to be taken into account in all public investments.

G- MRV and Transparency

The Measurement, Reporting and Verification (MRV) system builds on the first Biennial Transparency Report (BTR1) submitted in 2026. The GACMO tool, sectoral inventories and indicators disaggregated by sex, age and disability ensure the reliability of monitoring and access to international carbon financing.

Madagascar's NDC 3.0 is both a climate plan and a lever for sustainable development. By mobilising public and private investment, protecting its unique ecosystems and placing people at the centre of action, the country is turning its vulnerability into climate leadership, for an equitable, resilient and low-carbon future.

Detailed analysis

1. Introduction

Madagascar occupies a singular position in the global landscape of the fight against climate change. With its 9 million hectares of natural forests and its 400 000 hectares of mangroves, among the most extensive in Africa (FAO, 2022), Madagascar stands out as one of the few countries in the world to record a net negative carbon balance. In 2023, the country's non-AFAT emissions stood at 35 918 kt CO₂eq (IGES, MEDD), while natural sequestration provided by forest ecosystems reached −143 789 kt CO₂eq, thereby generating a net sink status of −108 779 kt CO₂eq. This atypical situation makes Madagascar a genuine global carbon sink, contributing significantly and selflessly to the mitigation of global warming. Yet this carbon sink status, although undisputed, must not mask the seriousness of the situation under business as usual. According to the updated projections of the GACMO model, the reference scenario (Business-as-Usual) reveals deeply worrying trends. Non-AFAT emissions projected to 2035 would reach 42 900 kt CO₂eq, an increase of 19,4 % on the 2023 level. More worrying still, the deforestation rate remains high, estimated at between 0,4 % and 0,5 % per year (MEDD, 2022), directly threatening the country's sequestration capacity. Between 2001 and 2022, Madagascar lost more than 4 million hectares of forest cover (Global Forest Watch, 2023), the equivalent of almost half the area of Switzerland. Pressures on Madagascar's natural heritage are intensifying under the combined effect of several factors. Energy dependence on fuelwood affects more than 90 % of households (INSTAT, 2022), resulting in an estimated annual consumption of 10 million cubic metres of wood. Shifting slash-and-burn agriculture, practised on around 1,5 million hectares, generates considerable emissions while degrading soils. Rapid urbanisation, with an urban growth rate of 4,5 % per year (INSTAT, 2023), increases pressure on peri-urban ecosystems and waste management services. If they are not reversed, these dynamics could turn Madagascar from a carbon sink into a net source of emissions in the coming decades. Paradoxically, while Madagascar contributes positively to the global climate balance, the country bears the full brunt of the devastating impacts of climate change. The country ranks 7th in the world on the Climate Risk Index (Germanwatch, 2023). Between 2000 and 2022, climate disasters caused economic losses estimated at more than 3 billion USD (World Bank, 2023). Successive cyclones, most recently cyclone Gezani in February 2026, which affected more than 600 000 people (BNGRC, 2026), recurrent droughts in the Grand Sud affecting more than 1,5 million people (WFP, 2023), and sea level rise threatening coastal areas, are a cruel illustration of this climate injustice. Madagascar, responsible for less than 0,01 % of historical global greenhouse gas emissions, pays a disproportionate price for climate disruption. This extreme vulnerability demands adaptation action commensurate with the dangers. NDC 3.0 places adaptation at the heart of its ambition, recognising that the resilience of populations and ecosystems is inseparable from the fight against emissions. Climate projections for Madagascar point to an intensification of extreme events: a temperature rise of 1,5 to 2,5 °C by 2050, changes in rainfall patterns, and an increase in the frequency and intensity of cyclones (Météo Madagascar, 2024). The most exposed sectors – agriculture, water resources, coastal areas, public health – will be the focus of adaptation efforts, with massive investment in resilient infrastructure, integrated water management, early warning systems and adaptive social protection. Strengthening the country's adaptive capacity is not only a humanitarian necessity: it is also a condition for preserving development gains and for maintaining the carbon sink function of ecosystems, in a logic of synergy between adaptation and mitigation. Climate action cannot be fully effective or fair without the systematic integration of the gender, youth and social inclusion dimensions. In Madagascar, women account for more than 70 % of agricultural production and are the primary custodians of natural resources, yet they remain largely excluded from decision-making processes and from land tenure security (FAO, 2023). Young people under 25 represent more than 60 % of the population, constituting a pool of innovation and green entrepreneurship that is still insufficiently mobilised. Persons with disabilities, women-headed households and isolated communities in the Grand Sud are disproportionately affected by climate shocks. NDC 3.0 therefore incorporates a resolutely inclusive approach, with targeted measures for the economic empowerment of women, the education of girls, the creation of green jobs for young people, universal access to basic services and citizen participation. This commitment to equity is not an optional extra, but a condition for the sustainability and national ownership of the low-carbon transition. Madagascar's climate ambition forms part of the broader framework of its right to development. With a GDP per capita of 515 USD in 2023 (World Bank, 2024) and more than 75 % of the population living in multidimensional poverty (INSTAT/UNDP, 2023), the country faces considerable challenges. Rural electrification covers only 15 % of the population (JIRAMA, 2023), limiting economic opportunities and perpetuating dependence on fuelwood. Chronic food insecurity affects nearly 1,3 million people (WFP, 2023), leaving populations vulnerable to climate shocks. These realities call for an integrated approach in which climate action contributes directly to improving living conditions. Madagascar fully asserts its right to industrialise and to develop its infrastructure in order to escape poverty. The country nonetheless makes the strategic choice of a low-carbon and resilient industrialisation, which is not a brake but a major asset for its transition. The construction of roads, ports, industrial zones and power plants will from now on follow resilience and energy efficiency standards, thereby avoiding technological lock-in to obsolete and highly emitting equipment. This vision makes it possible to decouple economic growth from the rise in greenhouse gas emissions, by making green industry a driver of job creation, energy sovereignty and a reduction in the fossil fuel import bill. The country has considerable assets for carrying through this green industrialisation: a solar potential of 2 800 hours of sunshine per year, an exploitable hydropower potential of 7 800 MW of which less than 3 % is currently harnessed (JIRAMA/MEH, 2022), biomass and agricultural waste resources, and innovative agricultural practices such as the System of Rice Intensification (SRI), which reduces methane emissions by 30 to 50 % while increasing yields (FAO/WWF, 2021). If they are mobilised on a large scale, these potentials can enable a transition towards low-carbon development while meeting the legitimate needs of the population. It is in this sense that green industrialisation becomes a pillar of climate ambition: it makes it possible to produce goods and services locally, to substitute for costly imports, to secure energy supply, and to create thousands of decent jobs, while maintaining and strengthening the country's carbon sink status. The third Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC 3.0) that Madagascar is submitting in 2026 embodies this renewed ambition. It reflects the conviction that preserving the status of global carbon sink, green industrialisation and socio-economic development can and must reinforce one another. The historic sequestration effort that Madagascar has borne for decades already constitutes, in itself, a major contribution to the global mitigation effort. But the country refuses to stand still: faced with the seriousness of the situation under business as usual, faced with the threats weighing on its natural heritage, faced with the devastating impacts of climate change on its population, Madagascar chooses to be even more ambitious. Ambition is also raised through the more explicit integration of short-lived climate pollutants (PCCDV), in particular methane and black carbon, whose rapid reduction delivers immediate climate benefits while contributing to improved air quality, public health and living conditions. This ambition, based on evidence, anchored in national realities and carried by all the country's driving forces, constitutes Madagascar's renewed commitment to present and future generations. "Madagascar does not choose between development and climate: it is betting on an industrial development that is low-carbon, resilient and just, in which the fight against climate change becomes an accelerator of shared prosperity."

2. Process and stakeholder engagement

2.1 Gap analysis At the midpoint of the 2030 deadline, the assessment of the implementation of Madagascar's revised Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC 2.0) shows encouraging sectoral progress, while also revealing structural gaps that will have to be closed in order to achieve the objectives of the Paris Agreement. NDC 2.0, submitted in 2022, targets a 28 % reduction in greenhouse gas emissions by 2030, i.e. 48 403 Kt eq. CO₂, for a total financing need estimated at 24,4 billion USD, covering mitigation, adaptation, coordination, institutional strengthening and loss and damage. Tangible progress has been recorded in the mobilisation of resources. Of the 18,9 billion USD required (excluding coordination) for mitigation and adaptation actions alone, 7,25 billion USD has been secured, i.e. a coverage rate of 38 %. Mitigation shows a more favourable dynamic, with 45 % of needs already financed (3,3 billion USD), while adaptation, although supported by 2,85 billion USD, does not exceed 24 % of estimated needs. This asymmetry partly reflects the predominance of funding from multilateral and bilateral institutions, often directed towards development projects with climate co-benefits, while the dedicated mechanisms of the UNFCCC remain under-used. Moreover, the contribution of the national budget and of the private sector remains marginal, limiting the domestic anchoring of efforts and the durability of investments. The governance of NDC 2.0 rests on coordination by the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MEDD) through the National Coordination Bureau for Climate Change and REDD+ (BNCCREDD+). Focal points have been designated in the sectoral ministries and allocations of IT equipment have strengthened local monitoring capacities. However, ownership of NDC 2.0 by all stakeholders still needs to be improved. Too often, projects are designed and implemented without an explicit link to the objectives of the NDC, which prevents a systematic assessment of their contribution to national targets. This disconnect undermines the pooling of efforts, the complementarity of interventions and the measurement of the overall effectiveness of climate actions. The monitoring and evaluation mechanism, although equipped with an implementation plan (PMO) with indicators, struggles to provide a consolidated, real-time picture of progress. The absence of systematic mapping of project achievements to emission reduction or adaptation indicators is a major obstacle. As a result, BNCCREDD+ does not have all the data needed to assess on a continuous basis the country's trajectory against the 2030 commitments, which reduces the capacity to make strategic adjustments during the cycle. Analysis of geographical and sectoral relevance shows disparities. For mitigation, actions are concentrated around areas with high reduction potential (Centre, Highlands, East), leaving the southern regions less well covered in number and in volume of financing. The distribution of adaptation projects, by contrast, is better correlated with exposure to climate risks, with a notable concentration of initiatives in the South. This coherence is nonetheless weakened by the influence of the interests of promoters and donors, which can lead to a sub-optimal allocation of resources towards areas with strong institutional anchoring rather than towards all vulnerable communities on the basis of strict needs criteria. Ultimately, the main gaps to be closed for NDC 3.0 lie in: (i) ownership and the absence of systematic reference to the NDC when projects are designed, (ii) (a massive financing shortfall, particularly for adaptation and coordination arrangements, (iii) the under-use of the financial mechanisms of the UNFCCC and of the carbon market, (iv) the weakness of the monitoring and evaluation system for measuring actual emission reductions and adaptation gains, and (v) (v) the need to better align geographical and sectoral allocation with national priorities, while ensuring an equitable redistribution of benefits. NDC 3.0 must therefore incorporate these lessons in order to strengthen governance, broaden partnership with the private sector and local authorities, and make the NDC a genuine steering tool for national climate action.

2.1.1 Analysis of the national situation regarding mitigation Madagascar is among the lowest greenhouse gas emitting countries on the planet, contributing only 0,09 % of global emissions (Madagascar NDC 2.0, 2022). With emissions of barely 0,14 tonne of CO₂ per capita (World Bank, 2024), the country is far below regional and international averages. This reality cannot, however, mask a worrying dynamic: the structure of national emissions, dominated by the agriculture, energy and waste sectors (MEDD, 2023), reflects an economy still heavily dependent on natural resources and biomass, while deforestation, the main driver of emissions in the UTCATF sector, continues to erode the country's principal carbon sink. Overall emissions have admittedly followed a downward trend, with an average decline of about 0,53 megatonne per year over the past decade, i.e. a reduction of about 0,84 % per year (National GHG Inventory, 2020). This trajectory nonetheless remains fragile and highly dependent on effectively controlling deforestation and on the energy transition. The energy sector is at the heart of the mitigation efforts undertaken by the country. With a national electrification rate of only 36 % -72 % in urban areas and barely 11 % in rural areas (Ministry of Energy and Hydrocarbons, 2023)- Madagascar faces a twofold imperative: to increase access to electricity while decarbonising its energy mix, which is today largely dominated by traditional biomass and fossil fuels. Nearly 45 % of electricity production currently comes from renewable sources, mainly hydropower, whose potential is estimated at 7 800 MW (NPE 2015-2030), to which are added some 2 000 MW of exploitable wind potential and sunshine exceeding 2 000 kWh/m²/year, favourable to solar photovoltaics (IRENA, 2022). The country has set itself the ambitious objective of raising the share of renewable energy to 85 % by 2030 and of reaching an electricity access rate of 80 % (NPE 2015-2030). The implementation of the New Energy Policy (NPE) 2015-2030 aims to structure this transition, but investments remain well below needs, and dependence on diesel generators remains a costly and emitting reality for remote localities (World Bank, 2023). In addition, the deployment of clean cooking solutions constitutes a major strategic mitigation lever directly linked to the priorities of the National Energy Plan: more than 90 % of Malagasy households depend on fuelwood, generating significant emissions of methane and black carbon. The transition to improved cookstoves and clean cooking solutions reduces this dependence while generating a threefold co-benefit: mitigation of PCCDV, improvement of indoor air quality and reduction of respiratory illnesses, and easing of the wood collection burden, which falls mainly on women and children. Furthermore, Madagascar is now a signatory to the Kigali Amendment to the Montreal Protocol, opening the way to the progressive reduction of hydrofluorocarbons (HFC) in the cold chain, air conditioning and refrigeration sectors, with joint benefits for the climate and for energy efficiency. Madagascar's forest cover, the second pillar of national mitigation, continues to face alarming pressures. Between 2001 and 2025, the country lost 5,4 million hectares of tree cover, a decrease of 32 % since the year 2000 (Global Forest Watch, 2025). In 2025 alone, 260 000 hectares of natural forest disappeared, generating the equivalent of 130 million tonnes of CO₂ emissions (Global Forest Watch, 2025). This rate of deforestation, fuelled by fires -6,2 million hectares of land burned in 2024, forests and savannahs combined (Météo Madagascar, 2024)-, slash-and-burn agricultural practices (tavy) and the illegal exploitation of precious timber, largely cancels out the potential gains of reforestation projects, whose target of 75 000 hectares planted per year (MEDD, 2023) remains modest in the face of the scale of the losses, estimated at around 100 000 hectares of forest destroyed each year (ONE, 2022). The implementation of the national REDD+ strategy (MEDD, 2021), coupled with initiatives such as the "Sustainable Landscapes in Eastern Madagascar" project supported by the Green Climate Fund (GCF, 2023), aims to reverse this trend through an integrated approach of conservation, restoration and agroforestry. In the agricultural sector, the shift towards low-carbon agriculture with a high sequestration capacity is advancing through the dissemination of climate-smart practices, agroecology and agroforestry, in particular under the sustainable agriculture promotion programmes deployed in the country's 23 regions (Ministry of Agriculture and Livestock, 2023). Improved cattle rearing, integrated agricultural models and sustainable land management are identified mitigation levers (NDC 2.0, 2022), although their scaling up remains hampered by financing and coordination difficulties. Lastly, waste management, a methane-emitting sector, has benefited from recent progress, notably Decree No. 2025-080 which sets the rules for environmental and social assessment and aims to ensure the sustainable management of solid waste (Official Journal, 2025), which should encourage the emergence of standardised landfills and appropriate treatment systems. It should be stressed, however, that the incineration of municipal waste, a practice still widespread owing to the lack of adequate collection and treatment infrastructure, on the one hand, and the uncontrolled burning of post-harvest agricultural residues, on the other, constitute two major and underestimated sources of PCCDV in Madagascar: black carbon from incomplete combustion, and methane generated by anaerobic decomposition in illegal dumps. These emissions, diffuse and scattered across the whole territory, largely escape conventional monitoring systems, which justifies a specific effort of quantification and integration into national inventories. Beyond conventional greenhouse gases, NDC 3.0 strengthens its ambition by explicitly integrating PCCDV, methane (CH₄), black carbon and hydrofluorocarbons (HFC), whose short-term radiative forcing and co-benefits for public health justify particular attention. While methane and HFC are greenhouse gases in the strict sense, black carbon is a carbonaceous aerosol resulting from the incomplete combustion of biomass, whose positive radiative forcing is among the highest of anthropogenic climate forcings. Faced with the climate emergency and the specific features of Malagasy emission systems, the analysis of PCCDV proves indispensable for an ambitious and credible NDC: it makes it possible to target rapid emission reductions where the climate and health gains are most immediate. With this in view, for everything concerning the technical analyses relating to PCCDV, in particular methane, black carbon and HFC, LLandDev has made its contribution with the support of the Climate and Clean Air Coalition. The sources identified in Madagascar cover three broad groups: (i) biomass fires, agricultural burning, municipal waste incineration and domestic uses of biomass, the main vectors of black carbon; (ii) flooded rice cultivation, livestock, and the management of organic waste and wastewater, which concentrate methane emissions; (iii) refrigeration and air conditioning equipment, sources of HFC growing rapidly with urbanisation. The spatial analysis also highlighted mangrove ecosystem areas as sources of methane, often little taken into account in conventional inventories. Although mangroves play a major role in carbon sequestration and climate mitigation, they also generate natural CH₄ emissions, to which are added those linked to deforestation and degradation in these areas. NDC 3.0 mobilises these levers through actions on clean cooking, waste management, sustainable agriculture and the control of industrial emissions, in addition to Madagascar's commitment to the Kigali Amendment. Despite these efforts, the financing shortfall remains the main bottleneck. Of the 7,29 billion USD needed for the mitigation actions of NDC 2.0, only 3,30 billion USD (45 %) had been mobilised at mid-term (NDC 2.0 Assessment, 2025), essentially through multilateral and bilateral channels, while contributions from the national budget and the private sector remain marginal (MEDD, 2025). The ongoing preparation of NDC 3.0 aims to correct these weaknesses by prioritising realistic and directly bankable mitigation project pipelines, in particular through thematic sovereign bonds, in order to accelerate the country's low-carbon transition while preserving its strategic ecosystems. 2.1.2 Adaptation and resilience gaps The assessment of Madagascar's NDC 2.0 and the sectoral analyses carried out as part of the preparation of NDC 3.0 reveal five categories of structural gaps that determine the ambition and feasibility of adaptation commitments. - Financing gaps: Adaptation financing needs for the period 2026–2035 are estimated at 16,43 billion USD, of which 13,81 billion USD for the technical sectors (agriculture, water, health, infrastructure, biodiversity, coastal zones), 1,97 billion USD for urban, social and human resilience, and 650 million USD for governance, data and early warning systems1. These amounts contrast sharply with the resources currently mobilised. Annual losses caused by climate-related disasters are estimated at around 100 million USD, with major events accounting for 2 to 4 % of GDP - a particularly heavy share for an economy whose GDP per capita stood at 545 USD in 20242. The national insurance gap is estimated at 157 million USD, and uncovered agricultural losses amounted to 46,9 million USD in 20233. The majority of the losses incurred by households, producers, MPMEs and local authorities remains uncovered by insurance or financial protection mechanisms, which heightens vulnerability and puts public finances under pressure after each shock. - Institutional and governance gaps: Intersectoral coordination between the ministries responsible for Water, Agriculture, Energy, Mines, Health and the Environment remains insufficient, despite the existence of consultation frameworks (CCC DEAH). The operational integration of the climate-health link into public policies remains incomplete. Disaster management capacities at local level are uneven: limited emergency equipment, absence of updated contingency plans in several districts, and still insufficient integration of disaster risk reduction into land-use planning4. In addition, climate-resilient technical standards are lacking in public procurement: infrastructure investments do not systematically incorporate climate vulnerability criteria or life-cycle costs. - Gaps in information and early warning systems: The multi-hazard early warning system remains insufficiently developed. Although the General Directorate of Meteorology (DGM) produces climate bulletins, their dissemination to vulnerable communities and their translation into anticipatory action remain limited by shortfalls in intersectoral coordination, operational protocols, dedicated financing and local capacities5. Only 3 automatic hydrological stations are operational out of the 9 identified as necessary for minimum monitoring of strategic basins6. The fragmentation of sectoral information systems (health, agriculture, water, education) is a major obstacle to the identification of cross-cutting vulnerabilities and to the prioritisation of adaptation investments.

- Gaps in access to essential services: Access to basic services remains highly insufficient: only 54,4 % of the population has a basic drinking water service and 12,3 % a basic sanitation service7. The ratio of health personnel per 10 000 inhabitants remains below WHO standards8. Between 2019 and 2023, 91 health facilities were damaged and 14 completely destroyed following the passage of climate hazards, with the Analanjirofo and Atsimo-Atsinanana regions recording the highest proportions of affected structures9. In the education sector, 10 700 schools were destroyed or damaged by cyclones over the period 2015-2025, disrupting the schooling of more than 1,2 million pupils10. Gaps in gender, youth and social inclusion mainstreaming: The assessment of the implementation of NDC 2.0 highlighted insufficient disaggregation of data by sex, age and disability status in sectoral adaptation indicators, as well as uneven mainstreaming of the gender approach in agricultural and climate resilience policies11. It also highlighted a lack of appropriate monitoring mechanisms and the absence of dedicated budget lines. These gaps reduce the capacity to measure the differentiated impacts of climate change. They limit the targeting of interventions towards the most vulnerable groups, women, children, adolescents, young people, persons with disabilities, displaced persons and migrants, coastal communities, agro-pastoralists of the Grand Sud, and affect the accountability of public policies. The participation of young people, women and vulnerable persons in climate decision-making and planning processes also remains insufficiently formalised, whereas they represent a strategic lever for the dissemination of adaptation practices within communities. 2.1.3 Governance, MRV and financing frameworks; adaptation component Climate governance architecture Adaptation governance in Madagascar rests on a progressively strengthened institutional architecture. The ministry in charge of the environment, through BNCCREDD+, provides central coordination. The Inter-ministerial Committee on the Environment (CIME), reorganised by Decree No. 2024-1808, constitutes the framework for inter-ministerial consultation on climate policies. BNGRC and CPGU ensure the coordination of risks and disasters. DGM is recognised as the national warning authority for hydrometeorological hazards12. This architecture exists. It must, however, be further operationalised: sectoral climate focal points in the technical ministries remain insufficiently formalised, the reporting of information to BNCCREDD+ remains irregular, and coordination between the central and decentralised levels is still fragmentary Adaptation MRV system The system for measurement, reporting and verification of adaptation is under construction. The CBIT project, implemented by BNCCREDD+ with Conservation International Madagascar, has made it possible to assess existing institutional arrangements, to develop emission factors specific to the Malagasy context, and to initiate the establishment of a national system for the collection, processing and transfer of sectoral data13. These achievements provide a basis, but the system is not yet fully operational for adaptation. The first Biennial Transparency Report (BTR1) of Madagascar, submitted in 2026 in accordance with the requirements of Article 13 of the Paris Agreement, marks a decisive step: it establishes the transparency baseline on which NDC 3.0 relies to document future progress14. The experience of other countries that have submitted their BTR1, including Bangladesh, which explicitly links its MRV to an integrated system for tracking mitigation, adaptation and financial flows, shows that the effectiveness of this system depends on an interoperable data architecture and a dedicated technical unit, two elements that Madagascar has still to consolidate15. The lessons learned from 47 countries supported by the UNDP NDC Support Programme confirm that integrated climate governance and climate finance are the two areas where capacity deficits are the most detrimental to implementation16. Financing frameworks The adaptation financing landscape has been substantially enriched since 2024. The Country Platform for Climate Finance, launched in November 2024 and co-led by MEDD and MEF with the support of the World Bank, the Global Center for Adaptation and UNICEF, constitutes the national framework for the coordination and mobilisation of climate finance17. The SFC, currently being finalised, is anchored to it. It organises the mobilisation of public, private and innovative financing around four principles: social inclusion, creation of sustainable jobs, structural transformation and environmental sustainability18. The IMF's FRD Facility provides additional structural support by making its disbursements conditional on specific climate reforms such as the revision of the Water Code, the selection of public investments on climate criteria, and the establishment of a national disaster insurance scheme19. Finally, the national workshop of 15 and 16 April 2025 validated the methodology of Madagascar's first green budget, which will be annexed to the 2026 Finance Act and will allow systematic tagging of climate expenditure in the State budget20. These advances are real. They must now translate into effective, traceable financial flows aligned with the adaptation priorities of this NDC.

2.2 Consultations

The process of preparing Madagascar's NDC 3.0 was based on a participatory and multisectoral approach aimed at strengthening national ownership of climate commitments, improving coherence between sectoral policies and integrating the needs of the populations most vulnerable to the effects of climate change. The consultations were conducted between 2025 and 2026 under the coordination of the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MEDD), through BNCCREDD+, with the support of UNDP and of the technical and financial partners engaged in the NDC process. The consultation arrangement mobilised the sectoral ministries concerned with adaptation and mitigation. Decentralised territorial authorities, civil society organisations, community-based organisations, women's organisations, youth associations, the representatives of the private sector, academic institutions and the technical and financial partners were also associated with the work in order to guarantee an inclusive and territorialised approach to climate planning. The consultations drew on several complementary modalities:

  • National sectoral validation workshops;

  • Inter-ministerial technical consultations;

  • Thematic working groups;

  • Regional consultations;

  • Bilateral interviews with technical institutions;

  • Targeted community consultations in areas highly exposed to climate hazards;

  • Workshops dedicated to youth, gender and social inclusion.

Particular attention was paid to the integration of gender and social inclusion issues into the consultation process. The assessment of the implementation of NDC 2.0 had indeed highlighted still limited participation of vulnerable groups in climate governance mechanisms as well as weak consideration of disaggregated data in sectoral adaptation policies. In order to address these limitations, specific consultations were organised with women's organisations and youth representatives. Five interregional youth consultations were in particular conducted by UNICEF as part of the preparation of NDC 3.0 in order to identify the priority actions of younger generations in terms of climate resilience, green employment, climate education and participation in environmental governance. The recommendations resulting from these consultations helped to strengthen the measures relating to just transition, green entrepreneurship, resilient food systems and vocational training linked to adaptation and mitigation occupations. The consultation process also made it possible to identify several cross-cutting priorities shared by the stakeholders consulted:

  • The strengthening of early warning systems;

  • The improvement of access to essential services;

  • The financing of adaptation at local level;

  • The development of resilient infrastructure;

  • Support to the livelihoods of vulnerable households;

  • The protection of ecosystems;

  • The need to strengthen institutional capacities and intersectoral coordination mechanisms.

Finally, the consultations confirmed the need to strengthen monitoring and evaluation and climate transparency mechanisms, in particular through the development of indicators disaggregated by sex, age and disability status, the improvement of sectoral climate data systems, the strengthening of the capacities of national and territorial actors involved in the implementation of NDC 3.0 and better coordination among actors. This NDC thus reflects a process of progressive co-construction bringing together public institutions, territories, communities and technical and financial partners, with the objective of strengthening the coherence, inclusiveness and operationalisation of Madagascar's climate commitments In line with one of the major innovations of this cycle compared with NDC 2.0, the "food systems" approach, systemic and integrated, which goes beyond the agricultural sector alone to link production, water, soils, nutrition, processing, markets and producer protection, has structured part of the consultations. Several consultation workshops with food systems stakeholders were thus organised in 2025 in order to identify the relevant adaptation and mitigation measures and to feed their integration into this NDC.

2.3 Alignment with national policies

NDC 3.0 is strictly aligned with the existing national strategic frameworks, in particular the General State Programme (PGE), which sets out the country's short-, medium- and long-term development priorities, as well as the National Climate Change Strategy setting the framework for climate action. It also incorporates the PNEDD (2030), the specific climate change target of the SPANB, the SNRPF (2030), the NPE 2015 and the Clean Cooking policy. It is furthermore articulated with the national REDD+ Strategy as well as with the sectoral adaptation and mitigation plans developed under the SLTC. This overall coherence ensures the integration of climate action into development policies, thereby facilitating the mobilisation of national and international resources. In addition, there is a national roadmap to support the transformation of food systems towards the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals of the 2030 Agenda (2022): stemming from the 2021 United Nations Food Systems Summit, it guides the sustainable transformation of the country's food systems and constitutes a reference framework for the agricultural, nutritional and food security measures of this NDC.

3. Update of the national context and risk assessment

3.1 Sectoral vulnerability

Madagascar is one of the countries most exposed to climate hazards on the African continent. Tropical cyclones, recurrent floods, prolonged droughts, heatwaves, coastal erosion and sea level rise combine over a territory of extreme bioclimatic contrasts, from the eastern coast, which receives up to 3 700 mm of annual rainfall, to the far South, which receives less than 40021. These hazards strike an economy whose GDP per capita stood at 545 USD in 2024 and where 79,7 % of the population lived below the extreme poverty line22. Madagascar's climate vulnerability is therefore as much structural as physical. Health, water, sanitation and hygiene The Malagasy health system is structurally fragile in the face of climate hazards. Between 2019 and 2023, 91 health facilities were damaged by cyclones or floods and 14 completely destroyed, with the Analanjirofo and Atsimo-Atsinanana regions recording the highest proportions23. Malaria is endemic in several regions and its transmission area is likely to expand with warming. Diarrhoeal diseases are the leading cause of child morbidity after floods. Heatwaves exacerbate chronic, cardiovascular and neurological diseases. The cold chain is vulnerable to power cuts; the supply of medical inputs is regularly disrupted as soon as a road link is cut, that is to say with every significant cyclone. In terms of access to water and sanitation, only 54,4 % of the population has a basic drinking water service and 12,3 % a basic sanitation service24. Water resources The major strategic catchments (Ikopa, Betsiboka, Mangoky, Tsiribihina, Onilahy) are subject to increasing hydrological variability and to increasingly conflicting use pressures between irrigated agriculture, hydropower generation, drinking water supply and industrial activities25. In the Grand Sud, water points are drying up, groundwater tables are falling, collection distances are lengthening, and it is always women and girls who bear this burden. Only 3 automatic hydrological stations are operational out of the 9 needed for minimum monitoring of priority basins26. The degradation of upstream catchments due to deforestation and accelerated erosion aggravates the silting of reservoirs and the irregularity of flows. Risks and disasters, financing and insurance Annual losses linked to climate disasters are estimated at 100 million USD on average, with major events accounting for 2 to 4 % of GDP27. Cyclones Fytia (2026) and Gezani caused losses estimated at 475 million and 2 billion USD respectively. The vast majority of these losses remains uncovered: the national insurance gap is estimated at 157 million USD, and uncovered agricultural losses at 46,9 million USD in 202328. The national contingency fund remains undercapitalised. The existing sovereign coverage mechanisms (African Risk Capacity, REPAIR, World Bank contingent lines) provide a useful but fragmented basis, insufficiently articulated for a rapid and predictable response. Agriculture, nutrition and food security

The sector employs 70 to 75 % of the working population and remains overwhelmingly rain-fed. Rice occupies 1,3 million hectares with an average yield of 2,7 t/ha that is heavily dependent on rainfall29. Of the 1,086 million hectares of irrigated perimeters, 30 to 40 % are only partially functional, with silted canals, degraded structures, and chronically insufficient maintenance30. The repeated droughts in the Grand Sud between 2015 and 2022 affected more than 1,3 million people; in 2024, more than 1,2 million were in acute food crisis31. The IPCC estimates that an increase of 1 °C can reduce cereal yields by 5 to 10 % in the absence of adaptation32. The growing disruption of cropping calendars, due among other things to the late onset of rains, mid-season dry spells and intense concentrations of rainfall, complicates sowing decisions and reduces production windows. It should be noted that individuals severely affected by climate events who do not recover their crop and livestock losses within 12 months are more likely to migrate. Education 57 % of Malagasy schools have a high probability of exposure to cyclones, representing 4,85 million pupils. 50 % are highly exposed to drought; 24 % to flooding33. Over the period 2015–2025, 10 700 establishments were damaged or destroyed, disrupting the schooling of more than 1,2 million pupils. The East coast concentrates almost all the establishments with a very high cyclone probability; the Grand Sud is dominated by drought exposure; the alluvial plains and low-lying urban areas concentrate flood-prone schools. Each prolonged interruption increases the risk of dropping out, particularly among girls whose families are impoverished by the shock. And school buildings made of light materials, falafa or unstabilised bricks, without cyclone-resistant standards, are rebuilt only to be destroyed again. Human mobility The cumulative effects of droughts, cyclones, land degradation, food insecurity and loss of livelihoods contribute to the increase in climate-related human mobility, in particular seasonal internal migration, forced displacement and movements towards urban centres and coastal areas34. These dynamics exert growing pressure on basic social services, infrastructure, natural resources and local social cohesion mechanisms, particularly in host areas that are already weakened. These dynamics are already measurable. According to the World Bank35, around 285 800 people were forcibly displaced in 2022 following rapid-onset climate events such as tropical cyclones and floods, and 68 000 of them were still internally displaced as at 31 December 2022. The displacement hotspots are concentrated on the east coast, the most exposed to cyclones, while the Grand Sud experiences departures linked to drought and the erosion of livelihoods. Displacement following sudden-onset hazards is most often temporary, whereas that associated with slow-onset hazards, in particular salinisation and progressive land degradation, tends to become permanent and difficult to reverse. Beyond economic losses, these mobilities are accompanied by non-economic losses such as the weakening of community ties, the loss of local knowledge and attachment to place, which remain insufficiently documented.

Social protection and child protection

Recurrent climate crises put local livelihood systems under strain and generate repeated needs for food assistance and emergency transfers, in particular in the Androy, Anosy and Atsimo-Andrefana regions and along the cyclone corridors of the East coast36. The existing social safety net programmes cover only a fraction of vulnerable households in relation to growing climate needs. The articulation between early warning, the triggering of transfers and humanitarian response remains insufficiently operationalised. Infrastructure and land-use planning Nearly 80 % of the Malagasy road network is unpaved, which makes it particularly vulnerable to intense rainfall, floods and landslides. Cyclones Batsirai and Emnati (2022) generated 170 million USD of damage in the transport sector alone37. The cutting of a road link isolating an entire region is not an exceptional event, it is an annual recurrence. The Antananarivo plain is exposed to recurrent flooding aggravated by unplanned urbanisation and the chronic inadequacy of stormwater drainage. The hydropower system, which provides a significant share of the national electricity mix, is vulnerable to rainfall variability and to the progressive silting of reservoirs linked to the deforestation of upstream catchments. Accelerated urbanisation and population movements linked to climate hazards increase the pressure on urban infrastructure, social services and the occupation of at-risk areas, in particular in informal settlements exposed to floods and landslides. Forests, biodiversity and protected areas Madagascar has lost around 44 % of its forest cover since 1950, at a current rate of 80 000 to 100 000 ha per year38. This decline weakens national water regulation, accelerates hillside erosion and feeds the silting of downstream hydraulic structures. The humid forests of the East are exposed to cyclones and to changes in rainfall patterns; the dry forests of the West and the spiny formations of the South are subject to rising temperatures, droughts and fires. Mangroves, covering a total of around 236 400 ha on the western coasts, play a crucial role of coastal protection, fish nursery and blue carbon sequestration, but are subject to cutting, conversion and erosion pressures that progressively reduce their extent and effectiveness39. Coral reefs and seagrass beds, among the most extensive in the Indian Ocean, are threatened by warming, bleaching and acidification. Coastal, maritime and port areas Sea level is expected to rise by 11 cm by 2030, 22 cm by 2050 and 43 cm by 2080 compared with the 2000 level40. Along a coastline of 4 828 km, the implications are immediate: progressive salinisation of shallow aquifers and coastal rice fields, erosion of the shoreline, more frequent and more extensive marine flooding during cyclone episodes. The port of Toamasina, which concentrates 90 % of national container traffic and more than 80 % of the country's foreign trade, is directly exposed. Cyclone Gamane (2024) affected 535 000 people in 33 flooded communes41. Coastal data such as altimetry, bathymetry and tide gauge series remain insufficiently available to allow appropriate spatial planning and protection. With regard to sea level rise and hydrometeorological hazards in coastal/maritime zones, the climate displacement hotspots are concentrated on the east coast, where most sudden-onset disasters strike, in particular cyclones.

In 2022, approximately 285 800 people were forcibly displaced as a result of rapid-onset climate events (tropical cyclones, floods). Coastal erosion, marine submersion and the gradual salinization of land and groundwater are likely to increase population displacement and future needs in terms of planned relocation, resilient territorial planning and protection of exposed coastal communities.

3.2 Gender equality, social inclusion and conflict sensitivity

3.2.1 Gender and social inclusion In Madagascar, the effects of climate change affect populations differently according to gender, age, socio-economic situation, place of residence, disability and access to productive resources and essential services. Women, children, young people, persons with disabilities and poor rural households are among the groups most exposed to the effects of droughts, cyclones, floods, degradation of natural resources and food insecurity. This differentiated vulnerability results from the interaction between climate hazards and persistent structural inequalities in access to land, education, financing, infrastructure, basic social services and participation in decision-making. Madagascar remains highly exposed to climate risks. Approximately 80,5 % of the population lives in rural areas and depends directly on natural resources and climate-sensitive sectors, in particular agriculture, fisheries and forests. The national poverty rate is estimated at nearly 75 %, while the country ranks among the states most vulnerable to climate change according to several international indices. Women account for approximately 73 % of the agricultural workforce but hold only a small proportion of secured agricultural land, estimated at around 15 % of formal land titles. This situation limits their access to credit, agricultural inputs, insurance mechanisms and adaptation investments. Young people, for their part, represent a majority share of the national population, with nearly 60 % of the population under 25 years of age, in a context marked by high unemployment and strong economic vulnerability. Climate impacts amplify these existing vulnerabilities. Prolonged droughts in the South reduce water availability, aggravate food insecurity and increase the domestic burden on women and girls linked to the collection of water and fuelwood.Recurrent cyclones and floods affect school, health, water and transport infrastructure, with direct consequences for the continuity of essential services, maternal health, children's education and household livelihoods. Extreme climate events also increase the risks of displacement, gender-based violence, school dropout among girls and economic precariousness of the most vulnerable populations. These impacts also directly affect children's rights to health, nutrition, drinking water, sanitation, education, protection, participation and a clean, healthy and sustainable environment. In response to these challenges, the NDC 3.0 recognizes that the integration of gender and social inclusion is an essential condition for the effectiveness and sustainability of climate action. The approach adopted aims to systematically integrate the gender and social inclusion dimensions into all policies, investments and governance mechanisms related to adaptation, mitigation and just transition. This orientation follows on from Madagascar's international commitments under the Paris Agreement, the Sustainable Development Goals, the Lima Work Programme on Gender and the UNFCCC Gender Action Plan, and is consistent with the National Gender Policy, the National Gender and Climate Change Strategy and the orientations of the National Adaptation Plan. The NDC 3.0 thus provides for the strengthening of gender-sensitive adaptation and climate transition mechanisms, in particular:

  • the development of inclusive climate finance mechanisms;

  • securing women's land tenure;

  • support for green entrepreneurship by women and young people;

  • the integration of the specific needs of women and girls into the WASH, health, energy and education sectors;

  • the participation of women and young people in local climate governance arrangements;

  • the development of accessible and resilient infrastructure;

  • the strengthening of adaptive social protection in the face of climate shocks.

The NDC 3.0 also ensures that mitigation, adaptation, disaster risk reduction, climate finance and monitoring and evaluation commitments contribute to strengthening the resilience of children, adolescents and young people, as well as of the essential social services on which they depend. The priority sectors of the NDC incorporate specific GIS entry points. In the agricultural sector, the measures aim in particular to strengthen women's access to resilient inputs, climate services, financing mechanisms and agricultural value chains. In the education and health sectors, the emphasis is on the continuity of essential services, the integration of climate education, sexual and reproductive health and the protection of vulnerable groups in disaster contexts. In the WASH and energy sectors, the proposed measures aim to reduce inequalities in access to basic services and to lessen the domestic burdens borne by women and girls. The sectors related to infrastructure, risk management and coastal zones incorporate approaches based on community participation, universal accessibility and protection of exposed populations. The NDC 3.0 also provides for a strengthening of monitoring and evaluation arrangements that are sensitive to gender and social inclusion. Indicators disaggregated by sex, age and disability status will be integrated into the sectoral MRV arrangements in order to improve the measurement of the differentiated impacts of climate policies and to strengthen accountability for the actions implemented. The development of national gender-sensitive climate data systems is thus a cross-cutting priority for improving planning, the targeting of investments and the monitoring of climate and social results. The process of preparing the NDC 3.0 also paid particular attention to the participation of women, young people, community organizations and vulnerable groups. Specific consultations were conducted with women's organizations, youth associations, representatives of persons with disabilities and communities exposed to climate risks in order to identify action priorities and the specific needs of the territories. Lastly, the NDC 3.0 recognizes that women, children, adolescents, young people, people on the move and local communities must not be considered solely as vulnerable groups, but also as central actors in resilience and the climate transition. Their knowledge of the territories, their natural resource management practices, their capacity for innovation and their role in community dynamics are essential levers for strengthening the resilience of socio-ecological systems and supporting more inclusive, sustainable and resilient development in the face of climate change. 3.2.2 Just climate transition Adaptation cannot be socially neutral. In Madagascar, where 79,7 % of the population lives below the extreme poverty line and where climate change strikes first at those who have contributed least to it, the question of climate justice is not rhetorical, it is operational42. The present integrates the principle of just transition as a condition of coherence and effectiveness of all of its adaptation commitments, in accordance with the decisions of COP 22 and 23 and the recommendations of the sixth IPCC report43. Agro-pastoralists constitute the most exposed group. They represent 70 to 75 % of the working population, derive most of their income from rainfed crops without any safety net, and bear the full brunt of droughts, floods and cyclone hazards without access to insurance or to formal compensation mechanisms44. An adaptation policy that improves average yields without reaching the most vulnerable smallholders, in this case those of the Grand Sud, of remote areas, households without land title, is not a just adaptation policy. The erosion of livelihoods is a determining factor of departure. According to the World Bank, the depletion of natural resources, in particular the collection of wild tubers and of wood intended for charcoal, together with the loss of agricultural income, are among the main determinants of mobility. Displaced households were harder hit than others: approximately 97 % of them suffered a decline in their agricultural production, compared with 92,8 % of non-displaced households; 88,2 % lost livestock, compared with 66,4 %; and 86,5 % saw their capacity to buy food decrease, compared with 79,3 %. Severely affected households that do not manage to rebuild their crop and livestock losses within twelve months are the most likely to migrate, which underlines the importance of a rapid response in terms of social protection and livelihood recovery. Women bear a disproportionate and invisible climate burden. They carry out the water-fetching chore, which becomes considerably longer in the dry season or during climate hazards, manage food stocks during the lean season, absorb the weight of unpaid domestic work when men migrate, and have structurally more limited access to productive resources, land and decision-making45. The International Labour Organization estimates that productivity losses linked to heat stress could reach 15 to 20 % in sub-Saharan Africa during prolonged heatwaves, a loss that first affects women agricultural workers exposed in the fields46. This NDC adopts as a principle that every adaptation measure must be assessed in the light of its differentiated effects by gender. Young people represent both the group most exposed over the long term and the most underused strategic lever. They make up the majority of the Malagasy population, absorb the shocks of climate-related school dropout, move into the urban informal economy, often in flood-prone areas, and yet carry a capacity for innovation, community engagement and transformation of practices that remains insufficiently mobilized47. Five inter-regional youth consultations were conducted by UNICEF and Aika Alliance as part of the NDC 3.0 process; their recommendations feed directly into the sectoral priorities of this document. Youth and the diaspora also constitute an adaptation lever that is still under-mobilized. Remittances, the sharing of know-how and technologies, and the engagement of the diaspora can support the resilience efforts of communities of origin, provided that they are recognized and supported by appropriate arrangements. Children bear the full brunt of the impacts of climate change because they are the most vulnerable population. Being dependent, they present specific vulnerabilities in the face of climate and environmental shocks, in particular with regard to nutrition, health, access to education and protection. They are the first affected by malnutrition, with more than half of children aged 6 to 59 months suffering from anaemia and 37.3% of children suffering from stunted growth due to chronic malnutrition. They are also the ones who experience interruptions in schooling and must then work in domestic jobs to support their families. This justifies priority attention in public policies and programmatic interventions. The communities of the Grand Sud deserve particular attention. Recent studies by the World Bank and the International Organization for Migration (IOM) indicate that Madagascar could experience the highest levels of climate-related internal migration in Africa by 2050 in the absence of sufficient action, with the Grand Sud as the main source of forced departures48. These forms of mobility take contrasting forms. While some result from household adaptation strategies in the face of climate pressures, others take the form of forced displacement linked to the erosion of livelihoods and the multiplication of extreme hazards. Mobility can therefore constitute a mechanism of resilience as much as a sign of insufficient adaptation, and calls for a differentiated public response. This NDC undertakes to prevent forced displacement through investment in in situ resilience, and to support unavoidable mobility through vocational training, social protection and inclusive territorial planning in receiving areas, in order to turn it into dignified life trajectories rather than descents into urban poverty. Operationally, just transition translates into four cross-cutting requirements: priority targeting of multi-hazard areas and of the poorest households in all investment programmes; the integration of indicators disaggregated by sex, age and disability into the MRV system; the effective participation of women, young people and vulnerable groups in climate governance bodies at all levels; and the deliberate generation of social co-benefits such as green jobs, access to services and the reduction of inequalities in the very design of adaptation measures49. A transition that leaves the most vulnerable behind is not a transition. It is a transfer of the problem.

3.3 Policy coherence with the National Adaptation Plan and other frameworks

Madagascar's NDC is not developed in an institutional vacuum. It forms part of a coherent ecosystem of national policies and international commitments that the adaptation measures operationalize, deepen and finance. Demonstrating this alignment is a requirement of Article 7 of the Paris Agreement and a condition of credibility vis-a-vis technical and financial partners. National adaptation plan (PNA, 2021) The PNA constitutes our strategic reference framework for adaptation. Developed by the MEDD through the BNCCREDD+ on the basis of consultations in the 23 regions, it is structured around three axes: strengthening governance, implementing a programme of priority sectoral actions, and financing adaptation, and it identifies the same priority sectors as those addressed in this NDC: agriculture-livestock-fisheries, water resources, public health, biodiversity and forestry, coastal zones, infrastructure, risk management50. This NDC is the operational translation of the PNA into measurable commitments. The two documents must be read together. National climate finance strategy (SFC, 2025) Co-led by the MEDD and the MEF with the support of the World Bank, the Global Center for Adaptation and UNICEF, the SFC was developed within the framework of the Country Platform for Climate Finance launched in November 202451. It defines the modalities for mobilizing public, private and innovative resources such as blended finance, thematic bonds and impact funds, and establishes the basis for Madagascar's first green budget, the methodology for classifying climate expenditure of which was validated on 15 and 16 April 2025 with a view to the 2026 Finance Act52. This NDC and the SFC are mutually dependent: one sets the investment priorities, the other organizes their financing. IMF Resilience and Sustainability Facility (FRD, 2024) This agreement supports the integration of Madagascar's climate programme into public finance management. Its structural reforms include the revision of the Water Code, the establishment of a national disaster insurance scheme, and the selection of public investments on climate criteria53. These reforms directly overlap with the water governance, risk financing and climate-resilient public procurement measures that we set out in this NDC. Strengthened regulatory framework (2024, 2025) Decree No. 2024-1808 on the organization of the Interministerial Committee on the Environment (CIME) and Decree No. 2025-080 revising the MECIE framework, which integrates climate risk into the environmental and social assessment of all public projects, constitute the regulatory foundation of the adaptation governance carried by this NDC54. National REDD+ strategy and First Biennial Transparency Report (BTR1, 2026) The ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) measures proposed in this NDC follow on from the national REDD+ Strategy coordinated by the BNCCREDD+, whose adaptation co-benefits they amplify55. The BTR1 submitted in 2026 constitutes the transparency baseline for documenting future progress. National biodiversity strategy (SPANB 2026-2030) and LT-LEDS The measures of the biodiversity component retained in this NDC are directly aligned with the financial needs of the SPANB 2026-2030 and with the AFR100 commitment to restore 4 million hectares of land and forest landscapes by 203056. The long-term low-carbon development strategy (LT-LEDS) also sets the national trajectory towards carbon neutrality by 2050: adaptation measures such as the restoration of watersheds, the agroecological transition and the reduction of tavy contribute directly to it through carbon sequestration and the reduction of land degradation, thereby creating substantial synergies between adaptation and mitigation57. International frameworks The adaptation commitments of this NDC respond to the Global Goal on Adaptation (GCA) defined in Article 7 of the Paris Agreement and specified at COP 28 around seven priority themes: water, food security, health, ecosystems and biodiversity, infrastructure, poverty reduction and cultural heritage. The nine adaptation sectors of the NDC 3.0 cover all of these themes58. The sectoral objectives adopted are also aligned with the four priorities of the Sendai Framework for disaster risk reduction (2015-2030), with the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), with the Addis Ababa Action Agenda on Financing for Development, and with the African Union's Agenda 2063. This coherence is not formal. The objectives set in this NDC are calibrated on the existing targets of these frameworks, the MRV indicators are harmonized with their respective reporting systems, and the financial envelopes identified draw on the mechanisms that they mobilize. Madagascar therefore intends to make its NDC 3.0 a document of effective strategic alignment, and not one more declaration of intent.

4. Mitigation Contribution

4.1 Introduction

The third Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC 3.0) establishes an ambitious framework for climate action between 2025 and 2035, consistent with the objective of the Paris Agreement of holding the increase in global temperature well below 2°C and pursuing efforts to limit it to 1,5°C. This contribution forms part of a trajectory of continuous improvement, drawing on the lessons of the NDC 2.0 and incorporating the recommendations of the Enhanced Transparency Framework.

The NDC 2.0 marked a significant step towards the integration of climate action into national planning. The process strengthened institutional coordination through the National Coordination Bureau on Climate Change and the Reduction of Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (BNCCREDD+) of the Ministry of Environment and Sustainable Development (MEDD). Notable progress was recorded in the development of monitoring and evaluation systems and in the alignment of sectoral policies with climate objectives.

The NDC 3.0 adopts a more robust approach, evidence-based and finance-ready, anchored in a and a clear link between mitigation, adaptation and the investment pipeline, and reinforced by the explicit integration of PCCDV as a lever of additional ambition. This contribution benefits from a broadened consultative process, involving all sectoral ministries, local authorities, the private sector, civil society and the technical and financial partners.

4.2 Reference projections (BAU)

The Business-as-Usual (BAU) reference scenario describes the evolution of emissions in the absence of any additional mitigation measure after 2023. It incorporates demographic trends (annual growth of 3.8 %), economic trends (average annual GDP growth of 4 %) and the spontaneous improvement of technological efficiency, in accordance with the requirements of the Enhanced Transparency Framework.

The updated projections, derived from the GACMO model, result in total gross emissions (excluding AFAT) of 39 023 kt CO₂eq in 2030 and 42 900 kt CO₂eq in 2035, an increase of 19,4 % compared with 2023 (35 918 kt CO₂eq). This moderate growth reflects the persistence of agricultural emissions (approximately 25 222 kt in 2030) and the underlying upward trend in the energy and waste sectors.

Compared with the PCCDV projections, the reference scenario (BAU) indicates a continuous increase in CH₄ emissions, rising from 914 Gg in 2025 to 1 252 Gg in 2030 and then to 1 628 Gg in 2035. HFC emissions also follow a gradual dynamic, rising from 306 Gg in 2025 to 397 Gg in 2030, and then to 432 Gg in 2035. Conversely, black carbon emissions remain relatively stable, rising from 16 Gg in 2025 to 18 Gg in 2035. It should be specified that these values mainly reflect agricultural and forest fires as well as domestic energy uses captured by aerosol measurement instruments; since the domestic combustion of fuelwood and charcoal is not fully accounted for by the current projection model, actual black carbon emissions are likely to be higher than the values presented.

4.4 Sectoral analysis of the mitigation measures

(i) Energy

The energy sector relies on a mix of large-scale renewable power plants, decentralised solutions and household energy efficiency. The measures selected are consistent with the Energy Sector Development Policy (PDMC).

Total reduction for the Energy sector (excluding transport): 962 kt CO₂eq in 2030 and 923 kt CO₂eq in 2035.

Detailed explanation:

The reduction is calculated as the difference between the BAU scenario (2 217 kt in 2030; 2 655 kt in 2035) and the scenario with measures (1 255 kt in 2030; 1 732 kt in 2035). The large-scale deployment of hydropower (254 MW) and solar (167 MW cumulative) is the main lever, complemented by the dissemination of 1,4 million improved cookstoves. These options make it possible to reduce emissions by nearly 35 % relative to BAU in 2035, while strengthening energy security and reducing pressure on woody resources.

Sources: GACMO analysis 2026, IGES data 2023.

Total reduction for the Transport sector: –191 kt CO₂eq in 2030 and 1 098 kt CO₂eq in 2035.

Detailed explanation:

The reduction is obtained as the difference between BAU (4 133 kt in 2030; 4 343 kt in 2035) and the scenario with measures (4 324 kt in 2030; 3 245 kt in 2035). The slight increase in 2030 (–191 kt) reflects a transitional phase in which the large investments in infrastructure (BRT, electric rail, charging points) and the initial deployment of electric fleets have not yet fully offset the growth in demand. From 2035, the combined effect of the scaling up of electric vehicles (10 000 cars, 6 000 buses, 20 000 two-wheelers) and of modal shift makes it possible to achieve a reduction of 1 098 kt, i.e. 25 % below BAU.

Sources: GACMO analysis 2026, IGES data 2023; calibration INSTAT, ATT, DGSR, CIM.

.

The waste sector is undergoing a profound transformation: it shifts from a net source of emissions to a carbon sink in 2035 thanks to material and energy recovery, anaerobic digestion and the substitution of fossil fuels.

Total reduction for the Waste sector: 5 846 kt CO₂eq in 2030 and 10 880 kt CO₂eq in 2035.

Detailed explanation:

The reduction is calculated as the difference between BAU (6 806 kt in 2030; 7 153 kt in 2035) and the scenario with measures (960 kt in 2030; –3 727 kt in 2035). In 2035, the sector becomes negative (–3 727 kt), which is explained by the cumulative effect of landfill gas capture, anaerobic digestion, composting and RDF production, which avoids the use of fossil energy. These measures make it possible to turn a source of emissions into a sector contributing to net mitigation.

Sources: GACMO analysis 2026, IGES data 2023.

Total reduction for the Industry sector: 59 kt CO₂eq in 2030 and 354 kt CO₂eq in 2035.

NOTE ON INDUSTRY: Madagascar has a major asset for combining industrial development with the fight against climate change: its considerable potential in renewable energy (solar, hydropower, biomass) and its agricultural and forest resources. Launching a green industrialisation is a strategic opportunity to create local value added, generate decent jobs, reduce dependence on imports and strengthen economic sovereignty, while maintaining the country's carbon sink status. Several green industry sectors are particularly promising for Madagascar: - Sustainable agro-industry: local processing of agricultural products (rice, fruit, spices, essential oils, cassava, sugar cane) using energy-efficient processes and residual biomass to produce energy (biogas, biochar, briquettes). This sector makes it possible to reduce post-harvest losses, increase producers' incomes and create jobs in rural areas. - Low-carbon construction materials industry: production of low-temperature fired clay bricks, compressed earth blocks, cements with a low clinker content (using volcanic ash or slag), and insulating materials based on plant fibres (hemp, bamboo, straw). These locally produced materials reduce the carbon footprint of the building sector and create a new industrial sector that generates jobs. - Renewable energy and energy efficiency: local manufacture and assembly of solar panels, solar water heaters, improved cookstoves, small wind turbines and biogas systems. This industry makes it possible to meet growing domestic demand while exporting to the Indian Ocean region. - Circular economy and waste recovery: plastics recycling, industrial composting, production of refuse derived fuel (RDF) from municipal waste, and anaerobic digestion of organic waste. These sectors reduce pollution, create jobs in collection, sorting and processing, and lower methane emissions from landfills. - Pharmaceutical and cosmetic industry based on endemic plants: sustainable use of Madagascar's unique biodiversity for the production of extracts, essential oils and high value-added active ingredients, with specifications guaranteeing the preservation of resources and the equitable sharing of benefits with local communities. - Eco-responsible textiles and crafts: production of natural fibres (raffia, sisal, organic cotton) and plant-based dyes, using water- and energy-efficient processes, meeting the requirements of international sustainable fashion markets. Green industrialisation will not take place at the expense of ecosystems: it will be based on sustainable resource management, strict environmental standards (revised MECIE) and tax incentives for low-carbon investments. It thus constitutes a lever for inclusive economic development, generating jobs for young people and women and reducing poverty, while contributing to the mitigation and adaptation objectives of the NDC 3.0.

Total additional sequestration for the Forest sector: 1 907 kt CO₂eq in 2030 and 1 907 kt CO₂eq in 2035.

Detailed explanation:

The additional sequestration corresponds to the difference between the BAU sink (–117 380 kt in 2030; –98 227 kt in 2035) and the sink with measures (–119 287 kt in 2030; –100 133kt in 2035). Increasing the restored area to 300 000 ha every 5 years makes it possible to strengthen carbon sinks significantly, while the adjustments to avoided deforestation (25 000 ha every 5 years) and silvopastoralism (25 000 ha every 5 years) reflect a more realistic target better suited to local capacities. These 1 907 five-yearly are added to the sectoral reductions, bringing the total improvement in the balance to nearly 15,8 Mt CO₂eq in 2035.
