4.5 Governance and MRV of the mitigation measures

The governance of mitigation requires strong technical interaction with the emitting sectors (Energy, Transport, Agriculture, AFAT, Waste, Industry).

  • Technical Coordination: The BNCC-REDD+ acts as the technical secretariat for the MRV. It centralises the sectoral GHG inventories.

  • Sectoral Accountability: To be practical, the arrangement relies on Data Management Units (UGD) within the key ministries (Energy, Agriculture, Transport).

  • Legal Framework: Use of Decree No. 2024-1808 (CIME) to make the annual transmission of activity data by private and public operators to the MEDD mandatory.

  • Bottleneck to be removed: The shift from "ad hoc" data collection (for one-off reports) to a continuous, institutionalised flow.

For the NDC MRV to be operational, it must be structured around three interconnected components:

Table 19: Components of the MRV system

Component

Practical Function

Reference Tool

M - Measurement

Calculation of emissions by sector and monitoring of progress indicators (e.g. MW of renewable energy installed).

GACMO and PROSPECTS+ tools for measures and tracking.

R - Reporting

Compilation of data for the national GHG inventories and the biennial reports.

BTR1 (2026): Establishes the reference trajectory (Baseline) and the progress achieved.

V - Verification

Internal quality control (QA/QC) by a unit of national experts and external review by the UNFCCC secretariat.

IPCC guidelines (2006) to ensure transparency and accuracy.

4.6 Mitigation conclusion

The scenario with mitigation measures achieves a significant reduction in gross emissions excluding AFAT compared with the reference scenario (BAU). In 2030, emissions excluding AFAT fall from 39 023 kt CO₂eq (BAU) to 31 946 kt CO₂eq (with measures), a reduction of 7 077 kt CO₂eq. In 2035, they fall from 42 900 kt CO₂eq (BAU) to 28 306 kt CO₂eq (with measures), a reduction of 14 594 kt CO₂eq.

At the same time, the forest sector (AFAT) strengthens its role as a carbon sink. The additional sequestration, calculated as the difference between the BAU sink and the sink with measures, amounts to 1 907 kt CO₂eq in 2030 and 1 907 kt CO₂eq in 2035. These gains are added to the emission reductions achieved in the other sectors.

The net emissions balance, which includes the whole AFAT sector (forests and other land uses), is as follows:

Table 20: Net AFAT emissions balance

Year

Net BAU balance (kt CO₂eq)

Net balance with measures (kt CO₂eq)

Total improvement (kt CO₂eq)

2030

−78 357

−87 341

8 983

2035

−55 322

−72 561

17 239

This total improvement is the sum of the emission reductions excluding AFAT (7 077 kt in 2030 and 14 594 kt in 2035) and the additional forest sequestration (1 907 kt at each horizon). The net balance thus remains negative over the whole 2025-2035 period, confirming that Madagascar retains its status as a net carbon sink.

The implementation of these mitigation measures requires conditional financing of 4 883,7 million USD (i.e. approximately 4,88 billion USD), broken down by period as follows:

  • 1 561,6 million USD for the period 2030-2035;

  • 3 459,9 million USD for the period 2035-2040.

  • In addition to these amounts, there is an unconditional contribution of 150 million USD, comprising.

Finally, the marginal abatement cost analysis (MACC) confirms the economic profitability of the majority of the proposed measures. This profitability, combined with the robustness of the reduction and sequestration trajectories, fully justifies the inclusion of these measures in the priority portfolio of the NDC 3.0.

5. Contribution to adaptation and strengthening of resilience

5.1 Sectors

The priority adaptation sectors of the present are defined on the basis of the dominant climate risks, sectoral consultations, national priorities and the resilience needs identified in the adaptation report. They cover essential social systems, productive sectors, physical infrastructure, ecosystems and financial risk management mechanisms. Human mobility dynamics linked to the effects of climate change, in particular displacement, internal migration and pressures on host areas, also constitute a cross-cutting dimension of resilience and adaptation that requires intersectoral consideration. The sectors selected are the following: Sector 1 - Health, water, sanitation and hygiene (integrated). This sector addresses in an integrated manner the resilience of the health system to climate hazards, access to drinking water, sanitation and the prevention of climate-sensitive diseases. The integration reflects the functional interdependence between public health, water quality and hygiene conditions in the Malagasy context. Sector 2 - Water and integrated water resources management (GIRE). This sector covers the governance of strategic watersheds, the securing of water infrastructure, the regulation of competing uses in periods of water stress and the strengthening of the national hydrometeorological network. Sector 3 - Risks, disasters, financing and insurance. This sector covers the instruments for financial preparedness for climate shocks: contingency funds, multi-hazard sovereign coverage, inclusive climate insurance, a national loss and damage registry, and rapid trigger protocols. It does not cover sectoral reconstruction investments, which are dealt with in the sectors concerned. The national loss and damage registry is intended to document both economic and non-economic losses, including those associated with population displacement. Sector 4 - Agriculture, nutrition and food security. This sector covers the resilience of agricultural, pastoral, fisheries and aquaculture production systems, the dissemination of climate-smart agriculture (AIC) practices, the strengthening of agricultural value chains and the food security of vulnerable households. Sector 5 - Education. This sector covers the physical resilience of school infrastructure, the continuity of teaching in situations of climate crisis, the integration of climate education into curricula and the protection of vulnerable pupils. Sector 6 - Social protection and child protection. This sector covers adaptive social safety nets triggered by climate thresholds, the protection of the households most exposed to shocks and the resilience of community subsistence systems, including displaced households, migrant populations affected by climate hazards and host communities facing increased pressure on resources and basic social services. Sector 7 - Infrastructure and spatial planning. This sector addresses the resilience of roads, bridges, public buildings, energy networks and urban drainage systems, as well as climate-resilient construction standards in public procurement and territorial planning. Sector 8 - Forests, biodiversity and protected areas. This sector covers ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) approaches: forest restoration, protection of mangroves and marine ecosystems, agroforestry, community governance of natural resources and biodiversity monitoring. Sector 9 - Coastal, maritime and port areas. This sector covers the protection of the coastline against sea-level rise and coastal erosion, integrated spatial planning of port cities, maritime warning systems and the resilience of strategic port infrastructure In short, these sectors are not independent of one another. Health depends directly on the resilience of water, sanitation, food and infrastructure. Agriculture depends on water management, soil restoration, climate services and rural tracks. Education depends on the resilience of school buildings, social protection, school WASH and the continuity of teaching in situations of crisis. Coastal areas depend at once on territorial planning, biodiversity, fisheries, port infrastructure, social protection and warning systems. The NDC 3.0 therefore adopts a sectoral logic, but with intersectoral implementation. 5.1.1 Sectoral measures Sector 1 – Health, water, sanitation and hygiene Public health is directly affected by climate hazards through the increase in climate-sensitive diseases, the disruption of access to care, damage to health facilities, the contamination of water points and the weakening of hygiene services in times of crisis. The priorities concern the anticipation of health risks, the continuity of services, the strengthening of infrastructure and the improvement of the environmental determinants of health. The One Health approach reinforces this orientation by linking human health, animal health and environmental health in the prevention of climate, health and ecosystem risks59.

These measures place public health at the heart of adaptation by linking climate alerts, epidemiological surveillance, WASH services and continuity of care. They make it possible to reduce morbidity linked to floods, droughts, cyclones and heatwaves, while strengthening local capacities. Integrating hygiene, sanitation and water quality into the health sector is essential to avoid a fragmented response to waterborne and vector-borne diseases. The measures will have to be monitored through indicators disaggregated by sex, age, disability, district and type of hazard. The integrated health-climate early warning system is the central measure for this sector. It makes it possible to anticipate epidemic peaks before they occur, by coupling DHIS2 health surveillance data with DGM weather forecasts. It is a no-regret measure: it simultaneously strengthens the quality of routine care and resilience to shocks. The rehabilitation of health facilities to cyclone-resistant standards addresses a documented vulnerability, a facility that is flooded or whose cold chain has been destroyed can no longer function precisely at the moment when it is most needed. On the water side, the target of 25 000 water points by 2035 responds both to a fundamental right and to a basic public health requirement, in a country where access to safe drinking water remains the exception for a large part of the rural population. Community WASH committees, with a requirement that 50 % of their members be women, are the guarantee of the sustainability of physical investments: infrastructure without an identified local manager deteriorates within a few years. Sector 2 – Water and integrated water resources management Water is a central determinant of adaptation. Rainfall variability, droughts, floods, degradation of catchment areas, silting of structures and conflicts over use simultaneously affect drinking water, agriculture, energy, health, sanitation and ecosystems. The measures selected aim to strengthen basin governance, improve hydrological information, secure infrastructure and anticipate trade-offs during periods of water stress60.

Water-related measures must be regarded as a cross-sectoral foundation. They determine food security, health, hygiene, energy, the prevention of conflicts over use and the resilience of ecosystems. The priority challenge is not only to increase water supply, but to govern the resource better, to protect catchment areas and to keep services operating during climate shocks. Indeed, IWRM represents the institutional architecture without which all hydraulic investments remain fragile. Basin committees with clear allocation protocols make it possible to settle conflicts over use before they degenerate into social tensions, a reality already perceptible in several basins during the dry season. The hydrometeorological monitoring network is an indispensable basic infrastructure. With only 3 automatic stations operational out of the 9 required, Madagascar does not yet have sufficient knowledge tools for the proactive management of its water resources. The implementation and strengthening of the Water Code, undertaken as part of the reforms supported by the IMF's FRD, must translate concretely into clarified use rights and enforceable catchment protection zones. The rehabilitation of irrigated schemes, 30 to 40 % of which are currently only partially functional, represents the investment with the highest return in terms of food security: producing more with the water available, without increasing pressure on already strained resources. These targets relating to stations, dams, water points and populations served will nevertheless have to be consolidated through national inventories and municipal registers. Sector 3 – Risks, disasters, financing and insurance Climate risk management cannot be limited to post-disaster response. Recurrent economic losses, damage to infrastructure, low insurance coverage, pressure on public finances and non-economic losses call for an architecture of pre-arranged financing, risk transfer and monitoring of loss and damage. The measures below cover only the additional financial and institutional functions, in order to avoid any double counting with sectoral physical investments61.

These measures follow a logic of their own: it is not a matter of financing either services or infrastructure, it is a matter of financing the capacity to respond financially to shocks before they occur. The logic of layered financing, that is to say the national budget for frequent events, contingent lines for intermediate shocks, sovereign insurance for severe episodes and a loss and damage window for irreversible ones, is an architecture of financial resilience that Madagascar must put in place methodically. Inclusive climate insurance remains the most critical missing link: farmers, livestock keepers and fishers face climate shocks without any formal insurance safety net. Accessible, partially subsidized index-based products will make it possible to protect the most exposed households without placing the entire risk on public finances. The loss and damage register, which is a moral and political obligation given Madagascar's vulnerability, is the basis for any credible advocacy with international loss and damage financing mechanisms. Sector 4 – Agriculture, nutrition and food security

Agriculture, livestock, fisheries and food systems are among the sectors most sensitive to climate variability. Late rains, dry spells in the middle of the rainy season, cyclones, floods, violent winds, erosion, lavakisation, silting of valley bottoms and post-harvest losses directly affect rural incomes and food security. The approach adopted covers the entire food system, from productive water and soils through to markets, storage, nutrition, local processing and financial protection for producers62.

This agricultural portfolio reflects an important shift: agricultural adaptation is no longer treated solely as a production issue, but as a transformation of the food system. Integrating soils, water, seeds, nutrition, storage, tracks, fisheries, aquaculture and insurance makes it possible to reduce losses, increase the resilience of incomes and support food self-sufficiency. To this end, three entry points are decisive. Irrigation first: moving from an agriculture that is 80 % dependent on rainfall to systems that control water is the most important structural break. Rehabilitated schemes and new developments are the backbone of the programme. Climate services next: delivering seasonal forecasts and sowing advice to 1 million producers by 2035, via SMS, radio and farmer relays, is a measure with a very low cost per beneficiary and a very high impact on reducing vulnerability. Finally, the reduction of post-harvest losses: losses are estimated at 20-30 % nationally for rice, a considerable loss in a country structurally exposed to food insecurity. Building community warehouses, disseminating solar drying and opening up producing areas through 3 000 km of rural tracks will make it possible to secure what has already been produced. The rice yield target, which is to move from 2,7 to 3,8 t/ha by 2035, is ambitious but realistic provided that investments in irrigation, improved seeds and climate services are effectively deployed in a coordinated manner. The measures must nevertheless be prioritized by production basin and articulated with territorial planning, in order to avoid the dispersion of investments. Sector 5 – Education Education is an adaptation sector in its own right. Cyclones, floods, droughts and heatwaves damage schools, interrupt learning, worsen school dropout and increase inequalities, in particular for girls, children with disabilities, displaced children and pupils living in rural or isolated areas. The measures aim to protect infrastructure, guarantee educational continuity and integrate climate skills into learning pathways63. During the 2022 cyclone season, 3 478 schools were severely affected, and 1 740 in 2023.

60 % of schools covered in 2030; 100 % in 2035

Post-cyclone, post-flood and post-drought areas

MEN, MSP, , specialised NGOs

Integrated Education-Climate system

SIGE-climate interoperability, school days lost, exposure of schools, dashboards, annual reports

National system operational in 2030; real-time management in 2035

National; DREN/CISCO; public decision-makers

MEN, DGM, BNGRC, CPGU, INSTAT, MEDD

Educational measures respond to three objectives: preserving access to school, maintaining the quality of learning and integrating climate into educational governance. They must be conceived as an investment in human capital, and not merely as a reconstruction expense. School resilience reduces learning losses, protects children, supports households and prepares a generation capable of acting in the face of climate risks. The programme rests on two complementary pillars: the first is preventive, building and rehabilitating schools that withstand cyclonic winds and whose platforms do not flood; the second is operational, never losing a week of school unnecessarily, thanks to tested continuity plans, radio education in remote areas and evacuation protocols known to all school stakeholders. The target of 1,5 million beneficiaries of educational social protection in 2030 reflects a concrete reality: climate shocks impoverish families, and a child whose household has lost everything does not return to school without explicit support. Sector 6 – Social protection and child protection Climate shocks increase poverty, food vulnerability, temporary and even permanent displacement, interruptions in schooling, the risk of violence and the loss of household income. Social protection systems must therefore be able to anticipate, absorb and accompany climate shocks. The measures selected aim to link early warning, social registries, transfers, school canteens, community arrangements and recovery mechanisms64.

Adaptive social protection makes it possible to reduce the shift from climate shock to humanitarian crisis. It must be linked to early warning, registries, financial mechanisms and basic social services. It is also a lever for equity, since women, children, older persons, persons with disabilities and poor households bear a disproportionate share of the burden of climate crises. This adaptive social protection, based on a logic of anticipation, is not yet fully institutionalised in Madagascar. Today, transfers mostly take place after the disaster, once losses have already occurred. Anticipatory triggering protocols, which are activated as soon as a weather forecast crosses a predefined threshold, make it possible to distribute assistance before drought destroys the harvest or a cyclone devastates housing. The experience of the MIONJO programme in the Grand Sud and of the humanitarian arrangements of WFP, UNICEF and IOM shows that this articulation is technically feasible in Madagascar. This NDC commits to institutionalising it through a formalised national framework, targeting as a priority multi-hazard municipalities and the poorest households. Sector 7 – Infrastructure and land-use planning Physical infrastructure underpins national resilience. Roads, bridges, urban networks, hydraulic works, public buildings, energy infrastructure and community facilities suffer recurrent damage during cyclones, floods, landslides and droughts. The measures aim to integrate climate risk into planning, standards, public investment, maintenance operations and urban development65.

Adaptation of infrastructure directly reduces the economic and social losses caused by disasters. It makes it possible to maintain access to markets, health care, school, water and energy. The measures must be integrated into public investment management so that every new infrastructure project in exposed areas is subject to a climate assessment, appropriate design and a maintenance plan. Public procurement reform, through the integration of climate criteria into all DAO above a given threshold, is the most important systemic measure in this sector: it ensures that future public investments withstand future hazards, and not the weather conditions of the past. Urban drainage of the Antananarivo plain is an urgent priority: every cyclone season floods entire neighbourhoods, contaminates groundwater, destroys property and paralyses a capital that concentrates a disproportionate share of national economic activity. Sector 8 – Forests, biodiversity and protected areas Madagascar is home to one of the world's biodiversity hotspots, with more than 80 % of plant and animal species being endemic. Deforestation is continuing at a rate of 80 000 to 100 000 ha per year66. This degradation weakens national water regulation, accelerates slope erosion and feeds the siltation of hydraulic works downstream. Ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) approaches are a high-return lever: they restore ecosystem functions while generating climate (mitigation), ecosystem, food and economic co-benefits for rural communities.

The restoration of 500 000 ha of ecosystems by 2030 is an ambitious but realistic target, consistent with the AFR100 commitment and with the restoration programmes already under way in Madagascar, the 2024-2025 results of the PLAE-PARE programme attesting to the pace already set67. Local governance, through GELOSE contracts, dina and management committees among others, is the condition for sustainability: a forest replanted without a community that takes ownership of its management will be deforested within the following decade. Success will depend on land tenure security, local governance, sustainable financing of protected areas and the inclusion of neighbouring communities, in particular women and young people. This ambition is part of the Kunming-Montreal Global Biodiversity Framework, whose target 3 ("30x30") aims for the effective conservation of at least 30 % of terrestrial and marine areas by 2030.

Sector 9 – Coastal, marine and port areas The Malagasy coastline concentrates populations, infrastructure, port activities, fishery resources, mangroves, reefs and seagrass beds that are highly exposed to sea-level rise, erosion, salinisation, cyclones, swell and strong winds. The measures aim to anticipate risks, protect critical assets, restore natural defences and strengthen coastal early warning and planning capacities68.

The Malagasy coastline combines several vulnerabilities: high population density, strategic port infrastructure, fragile ecosystems, communities dependent on small-scale fishing, and coastal data that are still insufficient for rigorous planning. Multi-hazard mapping is the absolute priority: a coastline cannot be effectively protected without knowledge of its bathymetry, its altimetry and its erosion trends. The combination of nature-based solutions such as the restoration of mangroves, dunes, reefs and seagrass beds with works targeted on the most strategic assets constitutes the most efficient approach. Heavy engineering works are reserved for sites of very high national economic value, foremost among them the port of Toamasina, whose continuity of operation is a condition of the economic competitiveness of the country as a whole. Coastal planning must also limit the increase in future exposure. An integrated approach to disaster risk reduction at the coastal level can also limit the damage to populations and their "migration outcome".

5.2 Cross-cutting adaptation measures

Sectoral measures will only be able to produce their effects if they are supported by solid cross-cutting functions. The adaptation report identifies four structuring levers: governance and institutional coordination, climate finance and climate-sensitive budgeting, the MRV system and transparency, and participation, inclusion and communication. These levers are consistent with the National Adaptation Plan, which already emphasises governance, sectoral implementation and the mobilisation of financing. The first cross-cutting priority is the improvement of adaptation governance. The NDC will have to consolidate coordination between the ministry in charge of the environment, the BNCCREDD+, the sectoral ministries, the BNGRC, CPGU, the Directorate General of Meteorology, INSTAT, the local authorities, the technical and financial partners, civil society organisations, economic actors, youth organisations, women's organisations and local communities, as well as the national mechanisms for supporting and financing sustainable territorial development.. The NDC will have to consolidate coordination between the ministry in charge of the environment, the BNCCREDD+, the sectoral ministries, the BNGRC, CPGU, the Directorate General of Meteorology, INSTAT, the local authorities, the technical and financial partners, civil society organisations, economic actors, youth organisations, women's organisations and local communities. The second priority concerns data, climate services and early warning systems. Madagascar already has national capacities, in particular within the DGM, the BNGRC, CPGU, certain sectoral systems and emerging climate and environmental data platforms. However, the data remain fragmented, unevenly disaggregated, sometimes poorly interoperable and insufficiently used in local public decision-making. Strengthening information systems, territorial observatories, sectoral databases and digital platforms is therefore an essential condition for adaptation. The strengthening of national data systems could also incorporate the collection, analysis and monitoring of data relating to displacement, migration and human mobility linked to climate change, in order to improve territorial planning, anticipatory responses and adaptation policies. Early warning systems should also ensure that they reach displaced and migrant populations, whose access to preventive information often remains limited, in order to prevent and better manage population displacement. The third priority is the integration of climate risk into public investment. Every infrastructure, land-use planning, health, education, water, agriculture or territorial development project should incorporate a climate risk analysis, resilience criteria, appropriate technical standards and monitoring mechanisms applicable to local public investment. This development is consistent with the reforms under way on public investment management, the integration of climate and gender criteria, and the development of climate budget tagging. Every infrastructure, land-use planning, health, education, water, agriculture or territorial development project should incorporate a climate risk analysis, resilience criteria, appropriate technical standards and monitoring mechanisms. This development is consistent with the reforms under way on public investment management, the integration of climate and gender criteria, and the development of climate budget tagging. The fourth priority is social and territorial inclusion. Adaptation measures must target as a priority the most vulnerable populations, in particular women, children, young people, older persons, persons with disabilities, poor households, displaced and migrant populations and those in any other situation of mobility, small producers, small-scale fishers and communities dependent on natural resources.

Particular attention could also be paid to host communities facing growing pressure on social services, infrastructure and natural resources in areas exposed to displacement and migration linked to the effects of climate change. A preventive approach to social cohesion and territorial resilience deserves to be prioritized in areas of high climate vulnerability and human mobility, in order to prevent tensions over resources and services between displaced populations, migrants and host communities. Consultation, local governance, monitoring and financing mechanisms will have to include these groups not only as beneficiaries, but also as actors of adaptation. Non-formal education arrangements and information, education and communication (IEC) activities targeting communities also strengthen this inclusion and the dissemination of adaptation practices. Adaptation measures will also have to promote territorial targeting based on climate vulnerability, exposure to risks and local capacities. Table of measures

5.3 Policy and institutional measures

The implementation of adaptation does not depend on sectoral investments alone. It requires regulatory, normative and institutional reforms whose structuring effect goes beyond that of any individual project. The following policy measures are necessary conditions for the effective implementation of this NDC. Measure 1 - Systematic integration of climate risk into public procurement: Any public contract exceeding 5 million USD will have to include a climate vulnerability analysis and integrate resilience criteria into the terms of reference. This obligation, undertaken as part of the IMF's FRD reforms, must be formalized in the revised Public Procurement Code and applied across all technical ministries. Measure 2 - Adoption and application of climate-resilient technical standards: Technical guidelines by sub-sector, such as road drainage sized for extreme rainfall, cyclone-resistant standards for public buildings, elevation requirements in flood-prone areas and rules for water infrastructure, must be developed, validated and made enforceable. Ten priority standards are expected by 2030 and twenty by 2035. Measure 3 – Continued operationalization of the Water Code, and analysis of revision needs: Undertaken as part of the IMF's FRD reforms, this exercise must lead to clarified use rights, water catchment protection zones delimited by order, allocation protocols for periods of water stress, and mechanisms to regulate abstraction in stressed basins. It must result in a structural reform proposal whose effects will benefit all water-consuming sectors. Measure 4 - Integration of climate risk into spatial planning: Territorial planning instruments, in particular the National Spatial Planning Scheme (SNAT, 30-year vision), the SRAT, PSM, PUDi, PUDé, SAC, SAIC and PALOSS, must incorporate hazard mapping and managed retreat requirements, in particular in coastal zones, floodplains and landslide-prone areas.. The revised MECIE framework (Decree No. 2025-080) provides the regulatory lever for this integration for all public investment projects.

Measure 5 - Climate budget tagging and first green budget - The methodology for classifying climate expenditure, validated at the national workshop of April 2025 and intended to feed into Madagascar's first green budget annexed to the 2026 Finance Act (LFI 2026), must be systematized across all ministries and progressively extended to revenues and decentralized investments. This tagging is the condition for the traceability of the national adaptation effort and for the credibility of the progress reports submitted to the UNFCCC. Measure 6 - Formalization of sectoral climate focal points: Sectoral climate focal points must be formally designated and provided with resources in each technical ministry concerned. They ensure the flow of information to BNCCREDD+ and consistency between sectoral planning and the commitments of this NDC. Measure 7 - Formal inclusion of youth in climate governance: Climate governance mechanisms at national and territorial levels must formally include representatives of youth organizations, recognizing their strategic role in disseminating adaptation practices, in social innovation and in the long-term sustainability of climate policies. Measure 8 - Application of the revised MECIE decree (No. 2025-080): The revised MECIE framework integrates climate risk into the environmental and social assessment of all public investment projects above a defined threshold. Its effective application, with sufficient review capacity at the level of deconcentrated services, is a condition for the quality and sustainability of public investments in a context of growing climate risks.

5.4 Gender-sensitive adaptation indicators

The NDC 3.0 adopts the principle of systematic disaggregation of adaptation indicators by sex, age and disability status, in line with the recommendations of the UNFCCC Gender Action Plan and with the national commitments made under the National Adaptation Plan69. These indicators are monitored annually in the national MRV dashboard. The indicators below constitute a synthetic matrix of gender-sensitive indicators. They do not replace the detailed sectoral indicators presented in the sectors' MRV systems. They serve as markers of equity, targeting and accountability, in order to verify that adaptation measures do not only produce physical or institutional results, but also contribute to reducing inequalities in the face of climate risks. The baselines will be established during the start-up phase of the NDC 3.0 investment plan, by 2026 at the latest, on the basis of programme registers, disaggregated household surveys and sectoral databases. The integration of gender-sensitive budget markers into the climate expenditure classification system will make it possible to track, in parallel, the share of adaptation investments specifically targeting women and girls. These indicators reflect a reality: if adaptation measures do not target women, girls, the en and the most marginalized groups in an explicit and measurable way, they risk reproducing or amplifying existing inequalities. An adaptation policy that improves average agricultural yields without reaching women farmers without land title in the Grand Sud, or that builds water points without involving women in their management, is neither an equitable nor a sustainable adaptation policy.

5.5 Loss and damage

Madagascar faces growing climate-related loss and damage that exceeds available adaptation capacities. Average annual losses linked to climate disasters are estimated at 100 million USD, with major episodes such as Cyclone Gezani (2 billion USD) and Fytia (475 million USD)70. According to the estimates of the NDC 2.0, the economic cost of climate-related loss and damage could reach 2,8 billion USD by 2030. These figures do not capture the full extent of the losses incurred. Non-economic losses, human lives, long-term displacement, irreversible degradation of cultural heritage, loss of endemic species, collective trauma and erosion of social capital in affected communities are real and massive, but insufficiently documented and recognized. Madagascar contributes less than 0,01 % of cumulative global greenhouse gas emissions. The disproportion between national responsibility for climate change and the losses incurred is among the most pronounced in the world. The Ministry in charge of the Environment provides political leadership and national coordination of the agenda on climate-related loss and damage. Through BNCCREDD+, it ensures the coherence of this agenda with the NDC, the National Adaptation Plan, sectoral policies and Madagascar's international commitments. BNCCREDD+ provides the technical secretariat of the national coordination mechanism for loss and damage, in collaboration with MEF, BNGRC, CPGU, INSTAT, the sectoral ministries and the territorial authorities Madagascar's commitments The Republic of Madagascar commits, under this NDC and in line with the mandate of the international Fund for responding to Loss and Damage established at COP 28, to the following actions.

  • National governance of loss and damage. A national coordination mechanism will be established under the authority of the ministry in charge of the Environment and technically led by BNCCREDD+. It will provide strategic guidance, interministerial coordination, validation of national priorities, monitoring of NDC commitments and articulation with international financing mechanisms

  • National register of losses, damages and harm. A national register will be set up, covering 70 % of major events by 2030 and 100 % by 2035. It will be linked with the information systems of BNGRC, CPGU, the ministry in charge of finance, INSTAT and the sectoral ministries, and will be interoperable with the MRV system of the NDC 3.0 and with future BTRs. Institutional governance and the integration of the register into the national climate transparency system will be ensured by the ministry in charge of the environment, through BNCCREDD+. BNGRC will coordinate the operational collection of post-disaster data, while INSTAT will support their standardization and statistical validation. Its establishment is an institutional priority for the period 2026-2028. The data collected will be disaggregated by sex, age, income level and disability status, in line with the Sendai Framework, in order to better analyse the differentiated effects of disasters.

  • National annual report on loss and damage. A consolidated annual report will be published, including direct and indirect economic losses, documented non-economic losses, analysis of insurance coverage gaps, and policy recommendations to reduce residual losses. This report will feed into international stocktaking exercises and climate negotiations. The report will be coordinated and consolidated by BNCCREDD+, on the basis of data validated and transmitted by BNGRC, CPGU, INSTAT, the sectoral ministries and the territorial authorities. It will be submitted for validation by the national coordination mechanism before publication

  • Access to the international Fund for responding to Loss and Damage. Madagascar commits to preparing and submitting at least 3 applications to the international Fund by 2030 and 6 by 2035, targeting as a priority the communities of the Grand Sud exposed to chronic drought and the coastal areas experiencing irreversible erosion or submersion. The ministry in charge of the environment, through BNCCREDD+, will coordinate the identification, prioritization and preparation of the programmes and projects to be submitted to the Fund for responding to Loss and Damage and to other relevant international mechanisms. To this end, BNCCREDD+ will maintain a national project portfolio and ensure its consistency with national climate priorities. The strengthening of national and regional capacities to prepare applications and mobilize this Fund, as well as the involvement of international financial institutions and the private sector, constitute a priority.

  • Complementarity and additionality of financing. BNCCREDD+, in collaboration with the ministry in charge of finance, BNGRC and CPGU, will carry out the analysis of the complementarity of loss and damage interventions with humanitarian financing, sovereign insurance mechanisms, risk financing instruments, adaptation programmes and reconstruction operations. This analysis will aim to avoid double financing and to demonstrate the added value of the resources mobilized for loss and damage

  • National doctrine on non-economic loss and damage. A national methodology for recognizing and quantifying non-economic losses will be developed, covering in particular long-term displacement linked to cyclones and drought, the degradation or loss of cultural and heritage spaces, the irreversible loss of endemic species, and the erosion of the fisheries capital of coastal communities. The development of this doctrine will be coordinated by the ministry in charge of the environment, through BNCCREDD+, with the participation of research institutions, sectoral ministries, territorial authorities, affected communities and civil society organizations.

  • Under the technical coordination of BNCCREDD+, loss and damage will be covered by harmonized indicators in the national MRV system. Loss and damage will be covered by harmonized indicators in the national MRV system, with standardized collection protocols and time series that are comparable across events and across years, in line with the requirements of the BTRs.

Institutional framework and division of responsibilities MEDD provides political leadership and national strategic guidance on climate-related loss and damage. BNCCREDD+, as the technical structure of MEDD responsible for coordinating climate policies, provides the technical secretariat of the national mechanism, the consolidation of data, the monitoring of indicators, reporting under the NDC and the BTRs, as well as the coordination of the preparation of international financing requests. BNGRC ensures the operational coordination of post-disaster assessments and the collection of field data. CPGU contributes to the strategic analysis of risks and to the integration of loss and damage into public policies. INSTAT guarantees the methodological and statistical consistency of the data, while MEF ensures budget programming, mobilization, traceability and financial monitoring of resources. The sectoral ministries and the territorial authorities ensure the assessment and monitoring of losses falling within their areas of competence.

6. GIS contribution

6.1 Agriculture and food systems

The agricultural sector concentrates a large share of climate and social vulnerabilities in Madagascar. Women represent a significant share of the agricultural workforce but have limited access to land, financing, resilient inputs, climate services and insurance mechanisms. Rural youth are also highly exposed to economic precariousness, to income losses linked to climate hazards and to difficulties in entering agricultural value chains. Droughts, cyclones, floods and land degradation directly affect food security, agricultural incomes and the livelihood systems of rural households. In this context, the integration of gender and social inclusion is an essential lever for strengthening the resilience of agricultural and food systems, improving equitable access to productive resources and supporting more sustainable and inclusive agricultural models.

The integration of gender and social inclusion into agricultural policies thus contributes to improving the resilience of food systems. It also makes it possible to strengthen local value chains and to support a more sustainable and inclusive agricultural transition.

6.2 Education

Climate hazards strongly affect the continuity of education services in Madagascar, in particular in areas exposed to cyclones, floods and prolonged droughts. Interruptions of schooling linked to climate disasters particularly affect girls and children, who are more exposed to the risks of dropping out of school, increased domestic work, early marriage and gender-based violence. School infrastructure also remains insufficiently adapted to climate risks and to the specific needs of vulnerable groups. In this context, the integration of gender and social inclusion into education policies appears essential to strengthen the resilience of the education system and to guarantee equitable access to education in a climate context.

6.3 Water, sanitation and hygiene (WASH)

Climate vulnerabilities accentuate inequalities in access to drinking water, sanitation and hygiene services, in both urban and rural areas, particularly in zones highly exposed to droughts and floods. Women and girls bear a disproportionate burden linked to water collection and are particularly affected by the absence of suitable sanitation infrastructure, especially in times of climate crisis. Persons with disabilities also continue to face significant difficulties in accessing WASH infrastructure. In this context, NDC 3.0 aims to strengthen resilient, accessible and inclusive WASH services.

6.4 Health

Climate change strongly affects health systems in Madagascar through the increase in waterborne, vector-borne and respiratory diseases, the disruption of care services and the worsening of the health vulnerabilities of exposed populations. Women, children, older persons and isolated populations are particularly vulnerable to the health effects of climate disasters. Climate hazards, in particular cyclones, floods, droughts and heavy rainfall, directly affect the functioning of the health system. They cause the destruction of health infrastructure, the contamination of water points, breaks in the supply of medicines and a disruption of essential health services, in particular in rural and remote areas.

6.5 Forests, biodiversity and ecosystems

Forest ecosystems and natural resources play a central role in the livelihoods and the pharmacopoeia of Madagascar's rural populations. Women and local communities depend heavily on forest resources for food, domestic energy, health and income, while being particularly affected by ecosystem degradation and the growing scarcity of natural resources. Natural resource management arrangements remain characterised by limited participation of women and vulnerable groups, reducing the effectiveness of local governance mechanisms and the sustainability of interventions. Indeed, women are often the guardians of traditional knowledge for the identification, cultivation, harvesting and valorisation of natural resources. Informal gold mining (orpaillage) is a significant cause of forest loss in Madagascar, including close to or inside protected areas. The clearing of sites, the disturbance of banks and watercourses, and mercury pollution durably degrade forest ecosystems. Explicit recognition of this factor, and its integration into forest protection and restoration measures, is necessary for a coherent strategy.

6.6 Coastal and maritime zones

Coastal communities are among those most exposed to the effects of climate change in Madagascar. Coastal erosion, cyclones, sea level rise and the degradation of fishery resources directly affect people's livelihoods, in particular in artisanal fishing. Women play an important role in fishery value chains but remain weakly integrated into the governance and financing mechanisms of the sector.⁷ Gradual sea level rise, recurrent cyclones and storms are all factors driving the displacement of coastal populations, who are obliged to resettle further inland.

SMEs and local economic activities remain highly vulnerable to climate impacts, in particular because of weak infrastructure, limited access to financing and supply chain disruptions. Women entrepreneurs and young people face additional constraints in accessing financing, markets and clean technologies.

6.8 Energy

The energy sector remains marked by low electricity coverage and heavy dependence on fuelwood and charcoal, particularly in rural areas. This situation disproportionately affects women and girls, who are primarily responsible for wood collection and for domestic activities linked to cooking. Climate impacts, in particular droughts, cyclones and the degradation of forest resources, accentuate the energy vulnerabilities of households and limit access to essential services. In this context, NDC 3.0 aims to promote an inclusive, resilient and low-carbon energy transition. Nevertheless, the energy transition, in Madagascar as at the global scale, rests on a growing demand for so-called transition minerals (nickel, cobalt, graphite, rare earths, ilmenite). The country is among the potential suppliers of these resources, which creates a direct link between low-carbon energy objectives and the extractive sector. This connection calls for responsible governance of supply chains, ensuring that the deployment of renewable energy does not translate into new social and environmental pressures on mining areas

6.9 Territorial planning and infrastructure

Madagascar's infrastructure and territories remain highly exposed to the impacts of climate change, in particular cyclones, floods, coastal erosion and droughts. Populations living in precarious urban neighbourhoods, remote rural areas and coastal territories are particularly vulnerable to the destruction of infrastructure, the interruption of essential services and displacement linked to climate disasters. Women, children and persons with disabilities are more exposed to the risks linked to the insufficiency of accessible, safe and resilient infrastructure.

6.10 Risk and disaster management

Madagascar is among the countries most exposed to climate disasters in Africa, in particular to cyclones, floods, droughts and heavy rainfall episodes. The impacts of disasters disproportionately affect women, children, older persons, persons with disabilities and poor households, which have limited adaptive capacities and reduced access to protection and assistance mechanisms. The risks of gender-based violence also increase in contexts of displacement and climate emergency.
