7. Analysis of co-benefits

7.1 Analysis of co-benefits – mitigation measures

7.1.1 Sectoral co-benefits

7.1.1.1 Mitigation co-benefits and the blue economy sector The current absence of seagrass beds from NDC emission modelling tools, in particular the GACMO model, is explained by several methodological difficulties. Seagrass beds share their coastal space with mangroves, creating a risk of double counting of carbon if areas and flows are not rigorously delimited. Moreover, these ecosystems remain extremely vulnerable to physical disturbance: a single boat anchoring or an alteration of currents can resuspend sediments and release sequestered carbon, compromising the permanence of the stocks. This fragility, combined with the lack of spatial coverage data and of in situ measurements of sequestration, has so far prevented their integration into national greenhouse gas inventories. Consequently, the primary condition for seagrass beds to be counted as a reliable sink in NDC 3.0 lies in the implementation of strict protection measures, which alone can guarantee the durability of sequestration and, hence, the valorisation of blue carbon on voluntary markets. This is precisely the ambition of the initiatives under way in the DIANA and SOFIA regions. The Aro Fano project (2024-2030) and the regional ReSea programme have laid the first building blocks of a systematic accounting of national blue carbon. The Aro Fano project provides for an assessment of the spatial coverage of seagrass beds and of their carbon storage capacity, not only in its intervention zones in the North and North-West, but also across all the coastal zones of Madagascar. The first estimates available for the Sahamalaza and Nosy Hara National Parks alone report at least 7 907 hectares of seagrass beds in good ecological condition. The key to these measures is to ensure the strict protection of seagrass beds in order to preserve their health and thereby avoid disturbing the sediments, which are important carbon sinks. By 2035, at least 7 907 hectares of seagrass beds located in the intervention sites of the Aro Fano Project will continue to benefit from effective protection measures, guaranteeing their good ecological condition and the absence of significant disturbance. This 2035 target constitutes the permanence horizon from which blue carbon credits may be issued and sold with a high level of confidence. The mitigation effect of this protection can be quantified. Assuming an average sequestration rate of 6 to 8 tonnes of CO₂ per hectare per year for seagrass beds in good health, the 7 907 hectares under strict protection could generate an annual sequestration flow of the order of 47 000 to 63 000 tonnes of CO₂ equivalent. The development of a community blue carbon pilot project, covering approximately 1 000 hectares of seagrass beds and mangroves within one of the project's sites, gives concrete form to the ambition of monetising this potential. The sale of blue carbon credits, whose prices on voluntary markets generally lie between 10 and 20 dollars per tonne, could generate annual revenues estimated at between 470 000 and 1,26 million dollars for the 7 907 hectares alone, with an additional premium if the biodiversity and community resilience co-benefits are certified. These revenues, directly reinjected into the conservation of the sites and local development, will finance surveillance activities, ecological monitoring and the installation of soft barriers (geo-sacs) to combat coastal erosion, thus ensuring the physical protection of the sediments and the durability of the carbon sink. Beyond the figures, the integration of these ecosystems into NDC 3.0 responds to a logic of integral co-benefit. Strict seagrass protection measures are not limited to carbon sequestration; they also protect the nesting habitats of sea turtles, maintain nursery functions for artisanal fisheries and strengthen the resilience of coastlines in the face of climate hazards. By setting a quantified target of 7 907 hectares protected by 2035, supported by a standardised monitoring framework (protocols of the MPA networks of Madagascar National Parks), Madagascar equips itself with a measurable, verifiable objective and directly financeable through market mechanisms. This pathway, grounded in science, strict protection and economic valuation, illustrates how blue carbon can contribute realistically and additionally to national climate ambitions, while closing the financing gap through innovative instruments such as blue carbon credits and blue bonds.

7.1.1.2 Mitigation co-benefits and the Water sector

Beyond wastewater and waste management, already incorporated into the GACMO model, Madagascar's water sector holds significant but still largely under-exploited mitigation potential. This potential rests on four interconnected pillars: reducing emissions associated with drinking water supply, controlling methane emissions from irrigated rice cultivation, decarbonizing agricultural pumping, and protecting watersheds through forest restoration. Taken together, these levers, which relate to both the quantitative and the qualitative management of water resources, offer Madagascar the opportunity to make the Water sector a full-fledged climate contributor in its NDC 3.0, while generating major health, food and ecosystem co-benefits.

- The mitigation lever most directly linked to the Water sector concerns access to drinking water in rural areas. In Madagascar, 64 % of the rural population has no access to an improved water source, which forces households to boil river water with fuelwood or charcoal in order to make it drinkable (World Bank, 2023). This practice, at the intersection of the water, forest and energy sectors, generates a twofold pressure: a health pressure, with waterborne diseases that contribute to child malnutrition, and a climate pressure, with CO₂ emissions from biomass combustion and an acceleration of deforestation (SaniTap, 2022). The H2O4CO2 project, implemented by SaniTap in the Anôsy and Analanjirofo regions, has demonstrated the viability of this lever at large scale: by restoring more than 2 000 non-functional rural water points and ensuring their maintenance over a period of 10 to 15 years, the project avoids the emission of 240 000 tonnes of CO₂ equivalent per year while providing access to drinking water for 650 000 people (SaniTap, 2022). Certified Gold Standard, this mechanism generates voluntary carbon credits whose sale provides sustainable financing for the upkeep of the infrastructure (SaniTap, 2022).

The national potential is considerable. Of the approximately 10 000 water points recorded in Madagascar, around 40 % are completely out of service and a further 45 % are significantly damaged and require maintenance (SaniTap, 2022). The MadAvance project, which is pursuing the extension of this approach, aims to rehabilitate 4 300 pumps in order to serve 662 000 people and reduce 242 000 tCO₂e per year (MadAvance, 2025). If all of the country's defective water points were rehabilitated, the total pool of avoided emissions could exceed 500 000 tCO₂e per year, assuming a linear relationship between the number of functional water points and the population no longer resorting to boiling water (estimate based on the ratios of the H2O 4 CO2 project). This mechanism, strictly linked to the supply of drinking water, unambiguously falls under the Water sector and not the Waste sector, and deserves to be integrated as such into the NDC 3.0, with quantified rehabilitation targets and avoided-emission pathways.

- Deforestation and land degradation, which are advancing at an alarming rate of nearly 100 000 hectares per year (ONE, 2022), do not only threaten biodiversity and REDD+ objectives: they also compromise the function of hydrological regulation of watersheds, with cascading consequences for the water supply of towns, irrigated areas and hydropower plants downstream. The conversion of forests into agricultural land through slash-and-burn emits greenhouse gases and directly threatens the carbon storage function of the forest as well as the viability of watershed services (UNESCO/Biocom Project, 2025). This finding places integrated water resources management (GIRE) at the heart of the interface between mitigation and adaptation.

- Forest landscape restoration (RPF), established as a national priority under the AFR100 initiative, explicitly links these two objectives: restored ecosystems simultaneously improve food security, water supply, conservation and carbon sequestration (Panorama Solutions, 2020). An assessment carried out under the REDD+ strategy estimates the carbon storage of restored forests in Madagascar at 148,4 tCO₂ per hectare (FAO, 2020). If the restoration of the priority watersheds identified in the national RPF strategy were targeted at water catchment areas that are critical for the supply of urban centres and irrigated areas, each hectare restored would contribute at once to carbon sequestration, groundwater recharge and the reduction of soil erosion. The Biocom project, led by UNESCO in northern Madagascar, illustrates this integrated approach: by protecting the forests of the Montagne des Français and of the Marojejy and Andohahela national parks against deforestation, the project simultaneously preserves the carbon storage function of the forest and the hydrological services of the watersheds (UNESCO/Biocom Project, 2025).

In accounting terms, while forest sequestration is already partly incorporated into the UTCATF sector of the NDC, the specific valuation of the carbon sequestered through the protection of water-related watersheds constitutes an additional co-benefit that can be monetized through blue carbon mechanisms extended to freshwater ecosystems, or through payments for environmental services (PSE) coupling carbon and water. The national GIRE strategy, which provides for strengthening governance and the integration of adaptation into sectoral planning (PSEau, 2023), offers the institutional framework to operationalize this synergy.

7.1.2 Socio-economic co-benefits

Mitigation projects in the drinking water and household energy sectors generate direct and immediate socio-economic gains for rural households. The rehabilitation of water points operating by manual or solar pumping spares women and children long hours of collecting fuelwood to boil water, time that can be reinvested in education, productive activities or rest (EcoHubX, 2025). Replacing wood-based cooking with improved cookstoves or biogas solutions reduces household fuel expenditure, limits exposure to toxic smoke that causes respiratory diseases, particularly among women and children (SSM Stove, 2024; MadAvance, 2025), and preserves forest cover, on which entire economic value chains such as beekeeping and the gathering of non-timber forest products depend. With less than 1 % of households having access to clean cooking solutions such as biogas, bioethanol or GPL, the potential for socio-health transformation remains immense (World Bank, National Energy Compact, 2024). In the agricultural domain, mitigation practices such as intermittent rice irrigation (SRI/AWD) or agroforestry produce significant socio-economic benefits beyond the reduction of methane emissions and carbon storage. Intermittent irrigation allows water savings of 25 to 50 % and increases rice yields by 25 to 100 % depending on implementation conditions, thereby improving food security and the incomes of rice farmers (ReliefWeb, 2015; Virginia Tech, 2010). Agroforestry, by diversifying crops and restoring soil fertility, offers producers additional sources of income (fruit, timber, fodder) while strengthening their food security and their resilience to climate shocks (Cœur de Forêt, 2023; Helvetas, 2023; GoodPlanet, 2023). Finally, the carbon financing mechanisms attached to these mitigation projects create direct income flows for local communities. The sale of carbon credits from the protection of seagrass beds and mangroves, such as Blue Ventures' Tahiry Honko project, generates resources that are channelled back into locally managed community funds to finance conservation and the development of income-generating activities (Blue Ventures, 2019; Panorama Solutions, 2023). Likewise, improved cookstove and water point rehabilitation projects create local green jobs — maintenance workers, stove-making artisans, nursery operators, ecoguards — often including vulnerable women (Tandavanala, 2023; MINAE, 2023). This circular carbon economy model makes it possible to financially empower the most vulnerable populations while sustaining climate results over time.

7.1.3. Co-benefits of reducing short-lived climate pollutants (PCCDV) Short-lived climate pollutants (PCCDV), notably methane (CH₄) and black carbon (BC), occupy a strategic place in Madagascar's climate policies owing to their strong short-term warming potential and their direct impacts on health, food security, soils and air quality. PCCDV reduction measures are of particular interest for Madagascar because of their rapid effect in slowing climate warming in the short term, an effect that complements longer-term CO₂ reduction efforts. As CH₄ and BC emissions in Madagascar are dominated by agricultural systems, livestock, waste and household biomass use. In this context, sustainable land management (GDT) covers an integrated set of practices for managing soils, water, biomass and crop residues aimed at maintaining land productivity while reducing the emissions associated with these systems. In addition, the valorization of urban organic waste through composting or anaerobic digestion constitutes an additional lever for reducing methane emissions from landfills, generating direct co-benefits for circular agriculture.

  • Reduction of agricultural methane

Irrigated rice cultivation is the main source of methane in the agricultural sector in Madagascar, with particularly high emission factors in the large rice-growing basins of the Highlands, Alaotra, Sofia and Marovoay. Under conditions of continuous flooding, the anaerobic soils of rice paddies favour the production of CH₄ through methanogenic fermentation. Intermittent irrigation practices (AWD/SRI), already presented in the Water section from the angle of water management, are considered here for their specific contribution to reducing agricultural methane emissions. They make it possible to reduce methane emissions by 30 to 70 % while improving agricultural yields and the resilience of rice systems to drought (Carrijo et al., 2017; Cornell University, 2021). Enteric fermentation in the ruminant herd is also an important source of agricultural methane in Madagascar. In traditional livestock systems, manure is rarely valorized in a structured way: it is often left on the ground in night pens/enclosures/stables or spread raw on plots, favouring methane emissions through anaerobic decomposition. However, aerobic composting of manure significantly reduces these emissions while producing an organic amendment that improves soil fertility (IPCC, 2006). The development of rural biodigesters is also a promising option making it possible to capture the methane from animal waste in order to produce biogas for household cooking. This simultaneously reduces CH₄ emissions and dependence on fuelwood. The valorization of urban organic waste then constitutes a double-dividend lever: by directing organic matter towards composting or anaerobic digestion channels rather than towards landfills, it reduces methane emissions while producing organic amendments that can be directly reused in agriculture, thereby strengthening the synergies between waste management and circular agriculture.

  • Reduction of black carbon linked to slash-and-burn practices and household energy

Reducing agricultural burning is a major additional co-benefit of GDT with regard to PCCDV. Tavy practices, land preparation fires and the burning of crop residues represent significant sources of black carbon in rural areas of Madagascar. Yet deforestation and slash-and-burn are advancing at an alarming rate of nearly 100 000 hectares per year (ONE, 2022), simultaneously threatening biodiversity, watersheds and air quality. The dissemination of agroecological practices, improved management of crop residues and the gradual reduction of the use of fire make it possible to lower atmospheric BC emissions, to preserve soil organic matter and to limit the risk of bush fires spreading to the surrounding forest massifs. The massive use of traditional biomass for household cooking remains a major source of black carbon and indoor air pollution. The deployment of improved cookstoves, household biogas and alternative fuels derived from crop residues makes it possible to significantly reduce BC emissions and to lower populations' exposure to toxic smoke, particularly among women and children.

  • Co-benefits of biochar and the valorization of crop residues

The development of biochar from crop residues represents a solution particularly well suited to the Malagasy context. Each year, rice-growing activities generate large quantities of rice husks that are often burned in the open air or abandoned in the fields, causing direct emissions of black carbon, methane and other atmospheric pollutants. Valorizing these residues thus makes it possible to transform a highly emitting agricultural waste into an energy and agronomic resource with added value. The controlled pyrolysis of rice husks, carried out in artisanal kilns, makes it possible to produce biochar, a stable, carbon-rich organic amendment. This technology offers a threefold climate benefit, comprising: the reduction of BC emissions linked to the open burning of residues, the reduction of methane from their anaerobic decomposition, and the contribution to durable carbon storage in soils over long time horizons. Biochar is recognized by the IPCC and several international voluntary standards as a solution with strong potential for durable carbon sequestration in agricultural systems (IPCC AR6, WGIII, 2022; Schmidt et al., 2021). In the degraded agricultural soils of the Highlands and of Boeny, biochar improves porosity, water retention and nutrient availability. Combined with manure compost in the form of compost-biochar, it helps to durably improve soil fertility while strengthening the stability of organic carbon. However, within this PCCDV section, these carbon storage benefits are considered additional co-benefits to the main objectives of reducing CH₄ and BC emissions. The energy valorization of rice husks offers a credible and sustainable alternative to the traditional charcoal used in rural households. Depending on the processing method, these crop residues can be converted into biochar for agronomic use or into ecological charcoal briquettes intended for household cooking. Used in improved cookstoves with optimized combustion, these briquettes sharply reduce emissions of black carbon and fine particles, while lowering energy consumption and easing the pressure exerted on forest resources.

The reduction of smoke and fine particle emissions directly improves public health, in particular that of women and children, who are the most exposed to indoor air pollution linked to the household use of biomass. These health co-benefits are substantial: reducing black carbon lowers the incidence of chronic respiratory diseases (asthma, bronchitis, lower respiratory tract infections), reduces hospitalizations and premature mortality linked to air pollution, and improves children's cognitive development. At the level of farming households, reducing residue burning also lowers exposure to smoke during fieldwork, directly benefiting rural populations. These co-benefits justify an integrated PCCDV- health-energy approach in the NDC 3.0, in connection with the actions of the health sector and the objectives of the National Health Policy. At the same time, the development of value chains around biochar, ecological briquettes and improved cookstoves stimulates the rural economy by generating green jobs in the collection of crop residues, pyrolysis, equipment manufacturing and the distribution of alternative fuels.

7.2 Analysis of co-benefits – adaptation measures

The adaptation measures selected in this NDC are not aimed exclusively at reducing vulnerability to climate risks. They generate substantial economic, social and environmental co-benefits, which strengthen their political legitimacy, their attractiveness to investors and their consistency with national development objectives. This section documents these co-benefits in a sectoral and cross-cutting manner, drawing on the data available for Madagascar and on comparative lessons from the international literature. The notion of co-benefit here refers to any positive effect of an adaptation measure on dimensions other than the direct reduction of climate vulnerability: job creation, food security, public health, gender equality, biodiversity, carbon sequestration, poverty reduction, social cohesion. These effects are not marginal. In the Malagasy context, where 79,7 % of the population lives below the extreme poverty line and where the country ranked 177th out of 193 in the 2023-2024 Human Development Index, adaptation measures are at the same time development investments with a strong social impact71. 7.2.1 Economic co-benefits Reduction of losses linked to climate disasters Average annual losses linked to climate disasters are estimated at 100 million USD in Madagascar, with episodes that can represent 2 to 4 % of GDP72. GFDRR analyses show, at the global scale, that one dollar invested in the resilience of infrastructure avoids on average 4 to 7 dollars of reconstruction costs and indirect economic losses73. Investments in urban drainage, in cyclone-resistant standards for public buildings, in road rehabilitation and in coastal protection works therefore produce net positive economic returns, measurable over the life cycle of the assets. In Madagascar specifically, if the roads and railways had been more resistant during the four tropical storms of 2022, the damage would have been reduced by 85 million USD according to IMF estimates74. This is the direct economic co-benefit of climate-resilient construction standards. Agricultural productivity gains The rehabilitation of 300 000 ha of irrigated areas by 2035, combined with the dissemination of improved varieties tolerant to drought and flooding, is projected to raise the average paddy rice yield from 2,7 to 3,8 t/ha. Across the 1,086 million hectares of agricultural area equipped for irrigation, even partial progress towards this objective represents a substantial increase in national production. The literature on climate-smart agriculture in sub-Saharan Africa shows productivity gains of between 15 and 40 % for systems that combine improved irrigation, adapted seed and climate services75. The reduction of post-harvest losses, estimated at 20-30 % for rice at the national level, constitutes an equivalent additional gain, without increasing pressure on natural resources. Creation of green and blue jobs The adaptation measures of this NDC constitute reservoirs of sustainable jobs for rural and coastal youth. The restoration of 500 000 ha of terrestrial ecosystems by 2030, the 3 000 km of rural tracks to be rehabilitated or built, the 1 500 km of urban drainage, the 10 000 water points to be created and the 500 agricultural storage warehouses to be built represent worksites mobilizing a considerable local workforce. Cash-for-work programmes with climate co-benefits integrated into adaptive social safety nets, in this case reforestation, the construction of anti-erosion terraces and the development of water collection basins, directly transform adaptation investment into income for the poorest households. In sub-Saharan Africa, landscape restoration programmes generate on average 3 to 5 local jobs per hectare restored during the initial implementation phases76. Applied to the target of 500 000 ha, this represents a potential of 1,5 to 2,5 million working days per year. The development of resilient aquaculture, sustainable fisheries and diversified agricultural value chains further generates value-added jobs in the processing and marketing chains, primarily benefiting women and young people. Valorization of natural capital and reduction of dependence on imports Malagasy mangroves, covering approximately 236 400 ha, provide coastal protection services whose economic value, estimated using replacement cost methods for equivalent grey infrastructure, can represent hundreds of millions USD over the lifetime of the ecosystems77. An analysis conducted under the World Bank's West Africa Coastal Areas (WACA) programme shows that healthy mangroves can reduce wave energy and coastal damage by up to 60-70 %, generating a protection value several times higher than the cost of their restoration and sustainable management78. Energy diversification towards solar micro-grids (150 micro-grids targeted by 2035) reduces dependence on imported fossil fuels and on variations in hydropower flows, two sources of chronic economic vulnerability for Madagascar. 7.2.2 Social co-benefits Food security and reduction of malnutrition Adaptation measures in the agriculture, water and nutrition sectors aim for a 40 % reduction in the prevalence of food insecurity in the targeted areas by 2035. In 2024, more than 1,2 million people were in acute food crisis in the Grand Sud and the South-East79. The combination of efficient irrigation, tolerant varieties, community food stocks and nutrition gardens targeting vulnerable households represents an integrated strategy whose co-benefits in terms of child nutrition are documented by several regional studies. The IPCC AR6 stresses that climate-smart agriculture measures combining crop diversification, improved soil fertility and water management simultaneously reduce exposure to food shocks and the rate of chronic malnutrition80. Public health The deployment of integrated health-climate early warning systems, the rehabilitation of health facilities and the extension of access to drinking water produce direct, measurable health co-benefits. The link between access to drinking water and the reduction of child morbidity is one of the best documented in public health: each additional percentage point of access to safely managed drinking water translates into a measurable reduction in cases of diarrhoea, acute respiratory infections and malnutrition among children under five81. In Madagascar, where only 22 % of the population benefits from a safely managed drinking water service, the objective of 80 % access to a basic service in 2035 represents a considerable public health gain, well beyond climate adaptation alone. The reduction of exposure to heatwaves through improved ventilation in buildings public also generates direct benefits in terms of labour productivity and thermal comfort, particularly in the coastal regions of the West and the South. Human capital and education The rehabilitation and construction of 1 500 schools to climate-resilient standards by 2035, combined with school continuity plans and post-shock educational social protection, will structurally reduce the number of class days lost during disasters. The ILO estimates that each additional year of schooling increases future individual income by 8 to 12 % in low-income countries82. Reducing school dropout linked to climate shocks, it should be recalled, particularly among girls whose dropout rates systematically increase after disasters, is therefore an investment in national human capital with a very high long-term social return. Mainstreaming climate, environmental and risk-reduction education content into primary and secondary curricula produces a generational co-benefit: young people trained in adaptation practices become vectors of dissemination within their communities, multiplying the impact of public policies well beyond the direct beneficiaries of the programmes alone. Reduction of gender inequalities Women bear a disproportionate climate burden in Madagascar: water fetching in the dry season, management of food stocks during the lean season, unpaid domestic work increased during shocks, limited access to productive and land resources83. The measures of this NDC that explicitly target women, such as the management of community water points with 50 % women in WASH committees, agricultural climate insurance targeting 50 % women beneficiaries by 2035, and agroforestry with 30 % of farms headed by women, are not administrative constraints. They are levers for transforming structural inequalities. CGIAR has shown that women invest a higher proportion of their income than men in children's food, health and education: targeting women in adaptation programmes therefore generates multiplier effects on household resilience that exceed the direct benefits of the measures themselves84. Social cohesion and reduction of use conflicts The establishment of watershed committees equipped with allocation protocols for periods of water stress, the formalisation of natural resource use rights through GELOSE contracts and dina, and participatory governance of coastal zones generate social cohesion co-benefits that are rarely accounted for in cost-benefit analyses of adaptation. Water use conflicts, between irrigating farmers, livestock keepers, hydropower producers and domestic users, are a source of social tension already documented in several Malagasy basins. The institutional mechanisms of GIRE, by providing legitimate arbitration frameworks, reduce these tensions and improve local resource governance, which is in itself a significant development co-benefit. 7.2.3 Environmental co-benefits Carbon sequestration The EbA measures of this NDC generate direct climate co-benefits in terms of mitigation. The restoration of 500 000 ha of terrestrial forest ecosystems by 2030 will allow carbon sequestration estimated at between 0,8 and 1,5 MtCO₂e, rising to 1,8-3,0 MtCO₂e by 2035. These estimates, based on the emission and sequestration factors adopted in Madagascar's national forest monitoring system (NFMS), are conservative in view of the biological potential of the targeted ecosystems85. The restoration of 6 000 ha of mangroves by 2035 contributes to blue carbon sequestration. Mangroves sequester up to 4 to 10 times more carbon per hectare than tropical terrestrial forests, and storage occurs mainly in the sediments, ensuring long-term permanence86. These mitigation co-benefits create financing opportunities through the mechanisms of Article 6 of the Paris Agreement, voluntary carbon markets and blue carbon credits, thereby strengthening the bankability of EbA measures and their attractiveness to private investors. Biodiversity conservation The restoration of 500 000 ha of terrestrial ecosystems, the protection of 6 000 ha of mangroves and the strengthened management of four priority protected areas (Nosy Hara, Sahamalaza, AMP Ankarea, AMP Ankivonjy) contribute directly to the objectives of the National Biodiversity Strategy (SPANB 2026-2030) and to the Kunming- Montréal Global Biodiversity Framework87. In a country where more than 80 % of species are endemic and where habitat fragmentation is the leading threat to biodiversity, ecosystem-based adaptation measures simultaneously reduce the climate vulnerability of communities and the loss of biodiversity. Lemurs, chameleons, amphibians, forest species dependent on humid microclimates and marine species associated with mangroves and reefs are among the groups that benefit directly from these measures. The restoration of ecological corridors also makes it possible to maintain connectivity between animal and plant populations, strengthening their capacity to adapt to climate change itself, which is an adaptation co-benefit for biodiversity that in turn benefits human adaptation. Hydrological regulation and soil protection The restoration of degraded watersheds, the reforestation of riparian woodlands and the widespread adoption of agroforestry on 120 000 farms by 2035 improve the regulation of the water cycle at basin scale. These measures reduce runoff, increase infiltration and groundwater recharge, stabilise flows in the dry season and reduce flood peaks during intense episodes. They also reduce the sediment load of rivers, extending the service life of hydropower reservoirs and irrigated perimeters, and improving water quality downstream. The link between deforestation and siltation is clearly documented in Madagascar: satellite data from Global Forest Watch show that the most degraded watersheds have siltation rates several times higher than basins under forest cover88. The 20 000 ha of reforestation in the upstream basins of the Andekaleka, Tsiazompaniry and Mandraka hydropower plants constitute an adaptation investment whose energy co-benefit is directly quantifiable in terms of hydropower generation maintained. Quality of coastal ecosystems and fishery resources The protection and restoration of mangroves, coral reefs and seagrass beds generate considerable fishery co-benefits for coastal communities. Mangroves act as nurseries for many species of fish and crustaceans: regional studies in East Africa show that mangrove area is positively correlated with the fishery yields of neighbouring coastal communities, with a multiplier coefficient estimated at between 1,5 and 3 depending on the species and fishing zones89. Coral reefs, whose nearshore seagrass beds along Madagascar's coasts are among the most extensive in the Indian Ocean, provide essential breeding areas for hundreds of species. Their protection preserves the productive base of artisanal fishing, which is the main source of animal protein and income for several hundred thousand Malagasy coastal households.

7.2.4 Co-benefits for systemic resilience Beyond sectoral co-benefits, the adaptation measures of this NDC generate systemic co-benefits that strengthen the country's overall resilience to climate, economic and social shocks. Reduction of macroeconomic vulnerability Investments in resilient infrastructure, risk management and climate insurance reduce the frequency and scale of emergency budget appeals that disrupt the programming of public expenditure and undermine the sustainability of public finances. The IMF has modelled Madagascar's public debt indicators under a pessimistic climate scenario, marked temperature increases and greater rainfall variability, and has shown that the impact on public debt by 2044 could be substantial in the absence of adaptation measures90. Adaptation investment is therefore, in part, an investment in long-term fiscal sustainability. Attractiveness for international climate finance A structured adaptation programme, with robust MRV indicators, a credible institutional framework and a pipeline of bankable projects, substantially increases Madagascar's attractiveness for international climate finance mechanisms. The lessons of the UNDP NDC Support Programme, drawn from 47 countries supported over the period 2017-2025, show that countries combining integrated climate governance, robust MRV and clear financial programming mobilise on average two to three times more climate finance than those whose institutional architecture is fragmented91. This NDC, by linking sectoral measures, monitoring indicators and an integrated financial framework, aims precisely to position Madagascar in this category. International legitimacy and advocacy for climate justice The rigorous documentation of adaptation co-benefits, combined with the establishment of the national loss and damage registry, strengthens Madagascar's capacity to advance credible, evidence-based advocacy for climate justice in international negotiations. A country that demonstrates that its adaptation measures generate measurable social, economic and environmental effects, and that simultaneously documents the losses those measures were unable to prevent, has a negotiating position grounded in data, the most solid form of argument in international forums.

8. Means of implementation and monitoring framework

8.1 Financial analysis and summary of total costs

The total cost of implementing Madagascar's third Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC 3.0) is estimated at 13,86 billion USD for the period 2025-2035. This budget is broken down into four main components, with a clear predominance of adaptation (57,5 %).

Of the 250 million USD in unconditional financing, only 2,5 million USD comes directly from the State budget, mobilised through the Green Budget of the 2026 Finance Act (LF 2026), mainly for regulatory reforms and support to coordination. This private investment is distributed across the four components of NDC 3.0 in accordance with the breakdown table below.

8.2.3 Mainstreaming of Gender, Youth and Social Inclusion (GIS)

A cross-cutting budget of 304,5 million USD is dedicated to the inclusion of women, young people and vulnerable groups in climate action. The main allocations are:

  • Land tenure security and inputs for women (26,5 million USD)

  • School grants and teacher training (20,5 million USD)

  • Menstrual hygiene and gender-balanced water committees (20,5 million USD)

  • Climate micro-insurance, incubation of green businesses for young people/women (19,0 million USD)

  • Behaviour change and youth leadership (27,5 million USD)

This GIS budget represents 2,2 % of the total cost of NDC 3.0, a ratio consistent with international standards, which recommend cross-cutting mainstreaming of at least 2 % of climate financing.

8.2.4 Coordination and Governance

The budget for coordination, governance and communication stands at 245 million USD, of which 25 million USD is unconditional (private contributions and the State's contribution through the Green Budget) and 220 million USD is conditional. It represents 1,8 % of the total cost. It is sharply lower than under NDC 2.0 (1,267 billion USD planned), thanks to capitalising on investments already made (equipment, information systems, focal points).

8.4.2 Economic benefits and co-benefits

Investment in NDC 3.0 generates economic, social and environmental benefits far exceeding its cost.

  • Return on investment (adaptation): Every dollar invested in resilient infrastructure avoids 4 to 7 dollars of future economic losses (World Bank, 2023). Thus, the 5,2 billion USD of water, health and spatial planning infrastructure could avoid between 20 and 35 billion USD of climate damage by 2050.

  • Value of avoided carbon: The 5,3 billion USD of conditional mitigation makes it possible to reduce GHG emissions by approximately X MtCO₂e cumulatively. On the basis of a social carbon price of 50 USD/tCO₂e, the present value of avoided emissions would exceed 10 billion USD.

  • Green job creation: Renewable energy, reforestation, agroforestry and waste management projects should create more than 150 000 direct and indirect jobs over the decade.

  • Savings on the trade balance: The substitution of fossil fuels by solar and hydropower would reduce imports of petroleum products by 1,2 to 1,8 billion USD per year by 2035 (estimate based on the energy transition plan).

  • Health and productivity: Access to drinking water and clean cooking solutions avoids millions of cases of waterborne and respiratory diseases, reducing pressure on the health system and improving household productivity (avoided cost estimated at 200-400 million USD/year).

8.4.3 Aggregate cost-benefit ratio

Combining avoided damage, the value of carbon, import savings and productivity gains, the cost/benefit ratio of NDC 3.0 is estimated at between 1:3 and 1:5 - every dollar invested yields between 3 and 5 dollars of net economic benefits for Madagascar.

8.5 Financial sustainability and financing mechanisms

Aware of the difficulty of mobilising 13,86 billion USD over 10 years, NDC 3.0 adopts a realistic, prioritised and innovative financing approach:

  • Pipeline of bankable projects: The budget is structured in three phases, making it possible to submit mature and directly financeable projects to donors.

  • Thematic sovereign bonds: Planned issuance of Sovereign Blue Bonds (200 million USD) and Green Bonds (500 million USD) targeting impact investors, along the lines of the Lemur Bonds.

  • Carbon market: Monetisation of emission reductions from rice farming, solar energy, waste and blue carbon; revenue potential of 20 to 50 million USD/year by 2035.

  • Multilateral and bilateral funds: Systematic targeting of the Green Climate Fund, the Global Environment Facility, the Adaptation Fund and bilateral cooperation.

  • Public-private partnerships (PPP): Structuring of PPPs for electric transport, waste-to-energy recovery, hydropower dams and large irrigated perimeters.

  • Domestic resources: Inclusion of climate budget lines in the Finance Act (unconditional component), tax incentives for renewable energy and improved cookstoves.

This combination of instruments ensures a diversification of sources and reduces dependence on a single window, in line with the lessons of NDC 2.0.

8.6 Technology and capacity-building needs

8.6.1 Context and strategic framework

The ambitious implementation of NDC 3.0 depends on increased access to and effective transfer of environmentally sound technologies, in accordance with the provisions of the Paris Agreement and the Enhanced Transparency Framework. Like many developing countries, Madagascar faces major technical, institutional and financial capacity challenges in deploying at scale the technological solutions required for mitigation and adaptation. The national Technology Needs Assessments (TNA) carried out since 2018 have made it possible to identify an initial portfolio of priority technologies, notably in the agriculture, energy and water sectors. However, the increased ambition of NDC 3.0 and the 2035 horizon require these needs to be updated and broadened in order to incorporate recent technological advances and the lessons learned from initial field experience (UNEP/DTU, 2018).

The technology needs for NDC 3.0 are organised around three complementary lines of action:

1. The deployment of mature and proven technologies for rapid scaling up (renewable energy, improved cookstoves, solar pumping);

2. The transfer and adaptation of emerging technologies with high potential for emission reduction or resilience-building (electric mobility, advanced biofuels, methane capture, climate-smart agriculture);

3. The strengthening of local capacities for innovation, ownership, maintenance and monitoring of these technologies, in order to ensure their durability and their anchoring in the country's socio-economic realities.

The table below provides an indicative and non-exhaustive list of priority technologies by sector, specifying their purpose (mitigation or adaptation), their level of maturity (Mature or Emerging) and the main associated needs in terms of transfer, training and investment.

8.6.3 Capacity building by sector

Capacity building in the public sector

The capacities of Malagasy public institutions will be strengthened in order to improve the planning, coordination, implementation and monitoring of climate policies at national, regional and local levels. Particular attention will be paid to the development of skills related to the Enhanced Transparency Framework (ETF), the preparation of Biennial Transparency Reports (BTR), the updating of national greenhouse gas inventories, the management of MRV systems as well as the collection, processing and analysis of climate, environmental and sectoral data.

Sectoral ministries, public agencies, technical establishments and research institutions will receive support aimed at strengthening the integration of climate change into public policies, sectoral strategies, budgetary frameworks and national development planning processes. National capacities in the economic assessment of climate measures, climate- and gender-responsive budgeting, impact analysis and the monitoring of co-benefits will be progressively consolidated.

At the territorial level, the capacities of the Regions, the Communes and the deconcentrated structures of the State will be strengthened for the preparation and implementation of local development plans integrating climate issues, the integration of climate risks into spatial planning, the management of local climate data and the development of community resilience mechanisms. The deployment of climate services, multi-hazard early warning systems and disaster management arrangements will be accompanied by training and skills transfer actions for the benefit of local actors.

National capacities for mobilising climate finance will also be strengthened. Training and technical assistance programmes will be implemented in order to improve the preparation of concept notes, funding proposals and investment files intended for international climate funds. National institutions will progressively develop expertise in the identification, formulation, structuring and negotiation of climate projects that meet the requirements of international financing mechanisms.

A national support mechanism for the origination and maturation of climate projects will be strengthened in order to build a continuous portfolio of bankable projects. Capacities in financial structuring, public-private partnerships, profitability analysis, risk management and blended finance will be developed in order to facilitate access to international resources and private investment.

In parallel, the technical and institutional capacities relating to carbon market mechanisms will be consolidated. The public actors concerned will receive support in the areas of accounting for emission reductions, the management of national registries, corresponding adjustments, the certification of carbon credits and the implementation of Article 6 of the Paris Agreement. Particular attention will be paid to the opportunities offered by the forestry, agriculture and sustainable land use sectors, given their strategic importance for Madagascar.

Capacity building in the private sector

The Malagasy private sector will be supported in order to strengthen its contribution to the transition towards resilient, inclusive and low-carbon development. Awareness-raising, training and technical assistance programmes will promote the integration of climate issues into corporate strategies, production processes and investment decisions.

The technical capacities of enterprises will be strengthened in the areas of renewable energy, energy efficiency, climate-smart agriculture, the sustainable management of natural resources, landscape restoration, the circular economy, waste recovery, sustainable aquaculture and low-carbon technologies. Enterprises will progressively have the skills needed to measure, report and verify their greenhouse gas emissions in accordance with international standards.

The development of capacities in climate finance will constitute a major focus of support to the private sector. Banks, financial institutions, insurance companies and investment funds will strengthen their skills in climate risk assessment, green financing, the structuring of innovative financial instruments and the mobilisation of concessional resources.

A particular effort will be devoted to the development of a national ecosystem for the preparation and structuring of climate projects. Enterprises, consulting firms, incubators, chambers of commerce and industry, professional organisations and producer organisations will receive support aimed at improving the preparation of concept notes, green business plans, pre-feasibility and feasibility studies as well as investment files meeting the criteria of international donors and private investors.

The capacities of economic actors will also be strengthened in order to promote their participation in carbon market mechanisms. Support programmes will enable project developers, cooperatives, farmers' organisations, natural resource managers and enterprises to acquire the skills needed for carbon certification, the generation of carbon credits and their valorisation on national and international markets.

With a view to sustainable economic development and the creation of green jobs, national capacities will be strengthened in the areas of manufacturing, assembly, installation, operation and maintenance of climate equipment. Vocational training centres, specialised enterprises, artisans and technicians will receive support aimed at developing local expertise in solar technologies, electricity mini-grids, clean cooking equipment, efficient irrigation systems, waste recovery infrastructure, nature-based solutions and climate monitoring equipment. This dynamic will contribute to strengthening the local content of climate investments, improving the durability of infrastructure and supporting the development of a job-creating green economy.

Cross-cutting capacity building

National capacities for the production, management and use of climate, environmental and socio-economic data will be progressively strengthened in order to improve the quality of results monitoring and to support evidence-based decision-making. Particular attention will be paid to the development of information systems incorporating data disaggregated by sex, age and disability status, in accordance with national commitments on social inclusion and gender equality.

Universities, research centres, training institutions and innovation centres will be mobilised in order to strengthen national capacities in applied research, innovation, technology transfer and the development of climate solutions adapted to the realities of Madagascar. Mechanisms for incubation, technology demonstration and the dissemination of green innovation will be progressively consolidated.

Particular attention will also be paid to strengthening knowledge and skills in the sectors most vulnerable to climate change, notably agriculture, livestock, fisheries, water resources, coastal areas, forests and biodiversity. Extension, technical advisory and good practice dissemination arrangements will be strengthened in order to improve the adoption of adaptation solutions by communities and economic actors.

Finally, all capacity-building actions will be implemented following an inclusive, gender-responsive approach based on the participation of communities. Women, young people, decentralised territorial authorities, community-based organisations, indigenous peoples where concerned and vulnerable groups will benefit from increased opportunities for access to the knowledge, technologies, financing and jobs generated by Madagascar's climate transition.

8.7 Innovative instruments

8.7.1 Barriers and innovative instruments – Mitigation

The implementation of the mitigation commitments of the NDC 3.0 faces several structural barriers. In financial terms, the conditional needs – 5,33 billion USD in total – remain out of all proportion to the flows currently mobilised. The unconditional national budget, although symbolically important, remains marginal (2,53 million USD) and the domestic private sector is still only intervening timidly, held back by the high perception of risk, weak fiscal incentives and the absence of pipelines of bankable projects ready for investment. Access to concessional windows (Green Climate Fund, Global Environment Facility) remains slowed by cumbersome procedures, while the preparation of competitive proposals requires technical and legal expertise that is still being consolidated within the sectoral ministries.

In institutional terms, the lack of coordination between the emitting sectors (energy, transport, agriculture, waste) limits an integrated approach to mitigation. The activity data required for greenhouse gas inventories are not yet reported in a continuous and standardised manner, despite the progress made in the MRV framework. The weak integration of mitigation objectives into sectoral public policies also hampers the large-scale deployment of the most beneficial measures, such as the electrification of transport fleets or the energy recovery of waste.

To overcome these bottlenecks, Madagascar is deploying a range of innovative financial instruments. Recourse to the mechanisms of Article 6 of the Paris Agreement will make it possible to monetise part of the emission reductions, in particular in the energy, waste and forest (REDD+) sectors, through bilateral agreements or the international crediting mechanism. The country is finalising its legal and operational framework on the organisation and supervision of the carbon market in Madagascar.

In parallel, thematic sovereign bonds (green bonds and blue bonds) will target impact investors, along the lines of the Lemur Bonds, in order to finance renewable energy infrastructure and the restoration of blue carbon ecosystems. The development of blue carbon pilot projects on seagrass beds (7 907 ha protected by 2035) illustrates how the strict protection of ecosystems can generate credits sold on voluntary markets, with annual revenues estimated at between 0,5 and 1,3 million USD for the north-western sites alone.

Public-Private Partnerships (PPPs) will be structured for large capital-intensive infrastructure (hydropower plants, solar parks with storage, waste recovery units), where risk sharing with international operators makes it possible to attract private capital without increasing sovereign debt.

Finally, the Country Platform for Climate Finance (November 2024) provides an unprecedented coordination framework for aligning multilateral donors, development banks and the private sector around the investment pipeline of the NDC 3.0. These instruments, combined with the scaling up of the national Green Budget, outline a financing trajectory that turns the identified barriers into levers for action.

8.7.1.1 implementation of Article 6 in Madagascar Madagascarensures that Article 6 is not regarded as a substitute for concessional financing, climate funds (GCF, GEF) and international public support

but as an additional instrument making it possible to strengthen the financial feasibility of conditional measures, to accelerate the deployment of low-carbon investments and to support the achievement of national mitigation objectives under conditions compatible with the environmental integrity and the climate sovereignty of the country.

ℹ The country platform for climate finance (November 2024, IMF/World Bank/Government) — the first in Africa via the Enhanced Cooperation Framework — aims to mobilise the private sector and to scale up green investments. Article 6 forms part of this broader framework of financial mobilisation.

8.7.1.3 Distinction between conditional and unconditional in relation to Article 6

For each sector, Madagascar will formally distinguish:

  • The unconditional share: reductions financed from national resources — non-transferable.

  • The conditional share: reductions that can be mobilised with international support (including Article 6) — potentially transferable subject to integrity and NDC compatibility.

This distinction must be formalised in the Climate Investment Plan of the NDC 3.0 and in the authorisations issued by the ANDMC.

8.7.1.5 Legal framework and authorisation Madagascar commits to adoptinga formalised national legal framework. The interministerial decree under preparation will consolidate Order No. 14683/2025 already in force and Decree No. 2025-626 (DRMCF) for forest carbon. This framework will comprise:

  • An interministerial decree defining the organization and supervision of the carbon market in Madagascar

  • An Article 6 participation strategy defining project eligibility criteria (additionality, permanence, no double counting, contribution to sustainable development); and including a mechanism for the annual review of authorizations, making it possible to adjust transferable volumes in line with changes in inventory trajectories and NDC commitments

  • A procedures manual defining the formal procedure for applying for and processing authorizations, with regulatory deadlines, published assessment criteria and avenues of appeal;

  • Standardized contractual clauses for Art. 6.2 bilateral agreements (minimum content: rights/obligations, adjustment mechanisms, benefit sharing, dispute resolution);

8.7.1.6 Implementation roadmap for Madagascar (2025–2035) The implementation of the mechanisms covered by Article 6 of NDC 3.0 is carried out according to a phased roadmap, structured in three phases. Madagascar reserves the right to adjust the volumes of transferable ITMOs at each NDC revision cycle. No transfer will be authorized to the detriment of the unconditional commitments of NDC 3.0.

8.7.2 Barriers and innovative instruments - Adaptation

The implementation of the adaptation commitments of this NDC comes up against structural barriers whose honest recognition is a condition for overcoming them. These barriers are not inevitable. They constitute the diagnosis to which innovative financing and governance instruments must provide operational responses. Financial barriers The financing gap is massive. Needs estimated at 7,98 billion USD over 2026- 2035 bear no comparison with the flows currently mobilized. The national budget available for adaptation remains very limited, climate budget tagging is not yet fully systematized and dependence on concessional external financing is structural. The weakness of insurance coverage aggravates this picture: the national insurance gap is estimated at 157 million USD, and the majority of the losses suffered by households, producers and local authorities remains uncovered92. The time required to prepare and process projects submitted to international climate funds constitutes an additional operational constraint, out of step with the urgency of adaptation needs on the ground. Institutional and governance barriers Intersectoral coordination remains insufficient despite the progress under way. Silos between the ministries responsible for water, agriculture, energy, health and the environment slow down the integrated approaches that adaptation requires. At the decentralized level, communes and districts lack the human, technical and financial resources to plan and implement adaptation measures autonomously. The absence of enforceable climate-resilient technical standards in construction and public procurement systematically exposes new investments to the same risks as existing assets, recreating with each cycle the vulnerability that adaptation seeks to reduce. Data and information barriers The hydrometeorological observation network is insufficient: only 3 automatic hydrological stations are operational out of the 9 required93. Sectoral information systems are not interoperable, which limits the ability to identify cross-cutting vulnerabilities and to prioritize interventions accurately. Coastal data remain insufficient for rigorous spatial planning of the coastline. The disaggregation of data by sex, age and disability status in the indicators for adaptation is still incomplete, reducing the ability to target measures towards the most vulnerable groups. Technical and capacity barriers Qualified human resources are lacking in several key areas: hydrology, hydraulic engineering, resilient urban planning, climate finance, agroecology, integrated coastal management. Adaptation technologies such as water-efficient irrigation, improved seeds, solar micro-grids and storage solutions remain of limited accessibility to small-scale producers and rural communities, both financially and logistically. The capacity to prepare bankable projects for submission to international climate funds requires technical and legal expertise that Madagascar does not yet fully possess in-house, lengthening timeframes and reducing the volume of financing mobilized. Land tenure and social barriers The overlapping of land norms and tenure statuses creates areas of friction that limit the effectiveness of reforestation programmes, payments for ecosystem services and agricultural investments. Without secure land tenure for plantations and restoration sites, communities have no incentive to invest over the long term in EbA measures. Moreover, the adoption of new practices (conservation agriculture, agroforestry, sustainable pasture management, etc.) presupposes behavioural changes that require time, sustained support and an institutional trust that past programmatic discontinuities have sometimes eroded. In the South, tensions linked to insecurity and cattle theft complicate local public action in the areas most exposed to climate shocks, precisely where adaptation needs are most urgent94.

Annex 4: Report on the integration of short-lived climate pollutants into Madagascar's NDC and BTR: promoting climate objectives, air quality and sustainable development

Integration of short-lived climate pollutants into Madagascar's NDC and BTR: promoting climate objectives, air quality and sustainable development

June 2026

List of acronyms
