Biennial Transparency Report CA-Markov: Cellular Automata-Markov – Markov cellular automaton CCAC: Climate and Clean Air Coalition – Coalition for Climate and Clean Air UNFCCC: United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change NDC: Nationally Determined Contribution Methane CH₄: CO: Carbon monoxide Carbon dioxide CO₂: EF: Emission Factor – Emission factor EDGAR: Emissions Database for Global Atmospheric Research FRP: Fire Radiative Power – Fire radiative power GACMO: Greenhouse Gas Abatement Cost Model GHG: Greenhouse Gas Gg: Gigagram GHSL: Global Human Settlement Layer IPCC: Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change GWP: Global Warming Potential HFC: Hydrofluorocarbons IIED: International Institute for Environment and Development JRC: Joint Research Center – Joint Research Centre of the European Commission LEAP: Long-range Energy Alternatives Planning System LST: Land Surface Temperature – Land surface temperature MAE: Mean Absolute Error – Mean absolute error MODIS: MODerate resolution Imaging Spectroradiometer MRV Measurement, Reporting and Verification MSI: MultiSpectral Imager MW: Megawatt NASA: National Aeronautics and Space Administration NBR: Normalized Burn Ratio – Normalized burn index NDVI: Normalized Difference Vegetation Index – Normalized vegetation index NIR: Near InfraRed Nitrogen dioxide NO₂: PCCDV/SLCP: Short-Lived Climate Pollutants (SLCPs) PFC: Perfluorocarbons UNEP: United Nations Environment Programme RF: Random Forest – Random forest RMSE: Root Mean Square Error – Root mean square error SRI: System of Rice Intensification SWIR: Short-Wave Infrared CAGR: Compound Annual Growth Rate TROPOMI: TROPOspheric Monitoring Instrument UTCATF: Land Use, Land-Use Change and Forestry VIIRS: Visible Infrared Imaging Radiometer Suite

Executive summary

Madagascar faces growing vulnerabilities linked to climate change, ecosystem degradation and air pollution. In this context, short-lived climate pollutants (PCCDV), notably methane (CH₄), black carbon (BC) and hydrofluorocarbons (HFC), represent a strategic lever for rapidly slowing global warming while generating immediate benefits for public health, air quality and agricultural production systems. Unlike CO₂, PCCDV have a relatively short atmospheric lifetime, making it possible to obtain rapid climate benefits over the coming decades. The main emission sources are associated with irrigated rice cultivation, livestock, urban waste, agricultural burning, vegetation fires, biomass combustion for household cooking, as well as the rapid expansion of refrigeration and air-conditioning uses. Although it is not accounted for as a greenhouse gas in conventional UNFCCC inventories, black carbon is a carbonaceous aerosol with a strong short-term warming potential and a major indicator of the health impacts of air pollution. This study constitutes the first integrated prospective spatialization analysis of PCCDV in Madagascar, combining satellite data, temporal modelling and national climate scenarios. The methodology is based on the articulation of remote sensing, geographic information systems (GIS) and statistical modelling. The models are built from satellite images and land cover data from Dynamic World, with 2019 as the reference year. The temporal projections use the compound annual growth rate (CAGR) in order to represent average trends in the evolution of emissions, while the ARIMA models incorporate non-linear fluctuations and temporal anomalies. Future land cover changes are simulated by means of Markov chains and CA-Markov models in order to better represent local spatial dynamics, particularly in areas undergoing rapid urbanization. Five prospective scenarios for 2030 and 2035 were developed: a baseline scenario (BAU), two mitigation scenarios aligned with the unconditional and conditional targets of NDC 3, a scenario incorporating UTCATF sector measures, and an accelerated urbanization scenario intended to assess the effects of strong urban growth on future emissions. The results were validated against EDGAR data and then adjusted with the national inventories from the BTR in order to ensure their consistency with national data and international climate reporting frameworks. Between 2019 and 2025, methane emissions increased from 753 Gg to 914 Gg, with major concentrations in the large rice-growing basins of the Highlands, Alaotra, Sofia and Marovoay, as well as in densely populated urban areas. The spatial analysis also highlighted mangrove ecosystem areas as sources of methane, often little taken into account in conventional inventories. Although mangroves play a major role in carbon sequestration and climate mitigation, they also generate natural CH₄ emissions, to which are added those linked to deforestation and degradation in these areas. Black carbon emissions remained relatively stable, fluctuating between 14,7 and 16,4 Gg, with however strong spatial variations linked to agricultural fires, tavy and vegetation fires. HFC emissions, for their part, rose notably, from 199 Gg to 306 Gg over the same period, driven by the expansion of refrigeration and air-conditioning equipment. The main HFC emission hotspots are concentrated in the large urban areas of Antananarivo, Antsirabe, Fianarantsoa and Toamasina. The projections highlight highly contrasting trajectories depending on the scenarios studied. In the baseline scenario (BAU), methane emissions reach 1 252 Gg in 2030, then 1 628 Gg in 2035. The mitigation scenarios bring these emissions down to 1 127 Gg in the unconditional scenario and to 1 015 Gg in the conditional scenario in 2030, and then to 1 368 Gg and 1 140 Gg respectively in 2035. Conversely, the accelerated urbanization scenario leads to a marked increase in CH₄ emissions, reaching 1 403 Gg in 2030 and 2 196 Gg in 2035.

With regard to HFCs, the BAU scenario projects emissions of 397 Gg in 2030 and 432 Gg in 2035. The mitigation scenarios lower these emissions to 340 Gg in the unconditional scenario and to 327 Gg in the conditional scenario in 2030, and then to 383 Gg and 363 Gg respectively in 2035. The accelerated urbanization scenario, by contrast, leads to a notable increase in emissions, reaching 407 Gg in 2030 and 543 Gg in 2035 as a result of the expansion of air-conditioning uses, the cold chain and urban infrastructure. Black carbon emissions fluctuate between 14,5 and 18,3 Gg depending on the year and the scenario, but remain highly sensitive to the dynamics of agricultural fires, slash-and-burn practices and household combustion using biomass. However, these estimates present an important methodological limitation, since they do not explicitly quantify "indoor" emissions from household cooking. This limitation is particularly significant in the Malagasy context, where more than 90 % of the population uses solid fuels for cooking, while the residential sector is estimated to account for nearly 8 % of the country's air pollution sources. Consequently, the results obtained may not fully reflect all the emissions and exposure levels associated with household uses of biomass, particularly in rural areas and indoor spaces. Several practices offer strong potential for rapidly reducing methane and black carbon emissions in the Malagasy context. Intermittent irrigation of rice fields, biodigesters, the reduction of agricultural burning, improved cookstoves and the valorization of agricultural residues in the form of biochar or ecological briquettes are among the most effective and most accessible interventions. The valorization of urban organic waste through composting or anaerobic digestion also constitutes an important complementary lever for reducing methane emissions from landfills. The protection and restoration of mangroves also constitute an important mitigation lever (UTCATF measure), by limiting the CH₄ emissions linked to the deforestation and degradation of these ecosystems, while preserving their function as a blue carbon sink. These measures also generate significant socio-economic and environmental co-benefits, notably improved food security, enhanced soil fertility, reduced indoor air pollution, as well as the creation of green jobs in rural areas, with significant positive effects for public health. In health terms, the reduction of PCCDV produces differentiated but complementary effects. The reduction of black carbon contributes directly to reducing the respiratory and cardiovascular diseases associated with indoor and outdoor air pollution. The reduction of methane emissions, for its part, limits the formation of tropospheric ozone, which is responsible for respiratory pathologies and for part of premature mortality. Finally, the reduction of hydrofluorocarbons (HFC) contributes indirectly to mitigating short-term global warming, which helps to limit the health impacts linked to heatwaves, vector-borne diseases and food insecurity. They thus constitute levers for action consistent with the national priorities of sustainable development, climate adaptation and energy transition. The study provides a robust scientific basis for the territorial targeting of mitigation policies, the monitoring of the NDC 3 targets and the mobilization of climate financing linked to PCCDV. The complementarity between spatialization and macro-sectoral approaches such as GACMO, EDGAR and the national inventories strengthens national capacities for climate planning, monitoring and reporting at the international level. Spatial approaches generally produce higher estimates than conventional sectoral models because of their ability to explicitly incorporate the distribution of fires, land cover changes and areas under strong anthropogenic pressure. The various models constitute complementary tools that make it possible to deepen the understanding of the territorial dynamics of emissions, to identify priority concentration areas (hotspots) and to strengthen the development of climate action plans as well as sectoral mitigation strategies in Madagascar.

Introduction

Faced with the growing urgency of the global climate crisis, the search for solutions capable of reconciling climate change mitigation, improved public health and sustainable socio-economic development is today a strategic priority. For developing countries, which are particularly vulnerable to climate impacts and have limited resources, it is essential to adopt integrated approaches that produce immediate benefits for populations while contributing to the global objectives of the Paris Agreement. From this perspective, short-lived climate pollutants (PCCDV), also known as Short-Lived Climate Pollutants (SLCPs), represent a particularly relevant lever for action. They mainly comprise black carbon (BC), methane (CH₄) and hydrofluorocarbons (HFC). Unlike carbon dioxide (CO₂), whose effects persist for several centuries in the atmosphere, PCCDV have relatively short lifetimes, ranging from a few days to about a dozen years. Reducing them thus makes it possible to obtain rapid benefits for the climate while simultaneously improving air quality and the health of populations. According to the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP), an ambitious reduction in PCCDV emissions could limit global warming by about 0,4 to 0,5 °C by 2050. Such action would also contribute to significantly reducing the health impacts linked to air pollution and to preserving agricultural productivity, particularly in the most vulnerable regions. For Madagascar, these issues are of particular importance. Although the country accounts for only 0,2 % of global greenhouse gas emissions, it is among the nations most exposed to the consequences of climate change. As a large developing island State and one of the least developed countries, Madagascar is confronted with a multiplication of extreme climate events, notably tropical cyclones, recurrent droughts, floods and the growing irregularity of rainfall. These phenomena directly affect the livelihoods of populations, weaken productive sectors and threaten the exceptional ecosystems that constitute one of the world's richest natural heritages. To these climate challenges are added environmental and health issues closely linked to air quality. The country's strong dependence on traditional biomass for household uses constitutes one of the main sources of black carbon emissions. Indeed, more than 90 % of the population still uses fuelwood or charcoal for cooking. This situation exposes millions of people daily to high levels of indoor air pollution, with significant consequences for health, particularly for women and children. In urban areas, rapid population growth, the increase in road traffic, inadequate waste management and biomass fires also contribute to the deterioration of air quality. Moreover, the agricultural and forestry sectors constitute significant sources of PCCDV. Flooded rice fields and extensive livestock farming generate significant methane emissions, while land-clearing fires, bush fires and deforestation simultaneously release black carbon and other air pollutants. In this context, the reduction of PCCDV represents a unique opportunity to generate multiple benefits. This approach is based on the concept of "triple benefit" (triple win), according to which mitigation measures simultaneously produce climate, health and socio-economic gains. By reducing PCCDV emissions, Madagascar can contribute to limiting short-term global warming, improving air quality, reducing the costs linked to respiratory diseases and strengthening agricultural productivity as well as the resilience of communities. Madagascar has reaffirmed its commitment to combating climate change through the ratification of the Paris Agreement and the submission of its Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC). The country is also engaged in the implementation of the Transparency Framework

Enhanced, which provides for the regular preparation of Biennial Transparency Reports (BTR) in order to track progress on mitigation and adaptation. However, despite the progress made in accounting for conventional greenhouse gases, PCCDV are not sufficiently integrated into the main national climate planning instruments. This report forms part of the project supporting the Government of Madagascar in strengthening the integration of non-CO₂ pollutants into the revision of the NDC 3.0 and into future BTRs. Through the updating of emission inventories, the spatial analysis of pollution sources and the modelling of mitigation scenarios, this report highlights the considerable potential offered by the integration of PCCDV into the national climate strategy. Beyond compliance with international commitments, this approach represents an opportunity to strengthen Madagascar's climate ambition, mobilise new financing, support more sustainable development and bring concrete improvements to the living conditions of the Malagasy people. The integration of PCCDV into the NDC 3.0 and the BTR therefore appears not only as a climate transparency requirement, but also as a strategic lever for building a more resilient, healthier and more prosperous future for Madagascar.

Context

Greenhouse gases (GHG) comprise all gaseous compounds capable of absorbing and re-emitting infrared radiation in the atmosphere, thereby contributing to the positive radiative forcing responsible for global warming. Carbon dioxide (CO₂) is the main greenhouse gas of anthropogenic origin, followed by methane (CH₄) and nitrous oxide (N₂O), together with fluorinated gases such as hydrofluorocarbons (HFC), perfluorocarbons (PFC) and sulphur hexafluoride (SF₆) (Ciais et al., 2013; IPCC, 2021). The successive reports of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) show that the global mean temperature has already risen by about 1,1 °C compared with the pre-industrial era and could reach 1,5 °C between 2030 and 2052 if current emission trends continue (IPCC, 2021). In this context, reducing long-lived GHG emissions remains essential to stabilise the climate over the long term. However, PCCDV now hold a strategic place in climate policies because of their strong potential to rapidly reduce global warming. The Climate and Clean Air Coalition (CCAC) recognises mainly four categories of PCCDV: methane (CH₄), hydrofluorocarbons (HFC), black carbon (BC) and tropospheric ozone. Their relatively short atmospheric residence time, from a few days for black carbon to about twelve years for methane, makes it possible to achieve almost immediate climate benefits when emissions are reduced. According to the United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) and the CCAC, an ambitious reduction of PCCDV could limit global warming by about 0,4 to 0,5 °C by 2050, while generating substantial co-benefits for air quality, public health, food security and ecosystems (UNEP, 2020; CCAC, 2023). These pollutants are distinguished by their specific sectoral sources and by emission dynamics that are particular to each national context. Madagascar contributes little to global greenhouse gas emissions, with a share estimated at less than 0,2 % of global emissions (UNFCCC, 2015; INDC Madagascar, 2015). Despite this low contribution, the country is among the States most vulnerable to the effects of climate change. This vulnerability is heightened by the strong dependence of the national economy on natural resources, the fragility of ecosystems, energy insecurity and structural poverty.

The country faces an intensification of extreme climate events, in particular tropical cyclones, recurrent droughts in the Grand Sud, floods and disturbances in rainfall patterns directly affecting agriculture, water resources and food security. These climate pressures are aggravated by the strong dependence of households on biomass fuels for domestic cooking, affecting more than 90 % of the population. Under its revised Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC 3.0), Madagascar aims to strengthen its climate commitment through better consideration of PCCDV, in particular methane, black carbon and hydrofluorocarbons. This orientation is intended not only to contribute to climate change mitigation, but also to improve air quality, health conditions and the livelihoods of the population. Several national greenhouse gas emission inventories and various sectoral analyses have already been carried out in Madagascar. However, existing approaches remain largely aggregated at the national scale and provide little information on the geographical distribution of emissions. The absence of detailed spatialisation thus limits the precise identification of priority emission areas and reduces the effectiveness of targeting public mitigation policies. In this context, the spatialisation of PCCDV emissions appears as a strategic issue for strengthening national climate planning and improving decision support. The present study therefore focuses on the spatial modelling of emissions of methane (CH₄), black carbon (BC) and hydrofluorocarbons (HFC) in Madagascar. Unlike tropospheric ozone, which is a secondary pollutant formed through atmospheric chemical reactions and not directly emitted by human activities, methane, black carbon and HFC are subject to direct emissions that can be quantified at the sectoral and geographical scale, which makes them amenable to spatial modelling of emission sources. The objectives of this study are in particular to:

  • analyse the spatial distribution of PCCDV emissions in Madagascar;

  • develop emission projections under different evolution scenarios;

  • compare the results obtained with existing inventories and studies;

  • assess mitigation options as well as their climate, environmental and health co-benefits.

Short-Lived Climate Pollutants (PCCDV)

PCCDV are atmospheric compounds whose residence time ranges from a few days for black carbon to about twelve years for methane, well below that of CO₂, whose effects persist for several centuries (CCAC, 2023). Despite this short lifetime, their short-term warming power is considerable, which makes them priority mitigation levers for the period 2020–2030. According to UNEP and the CCAC, an ambitious reduction of PCCDV could limit global warming by nearly 0,5°C by 2050, while generating significant co-benefits for air quality, public health and food security (UNEP, 2020). Methane (CH₄) Methane is the second most important greenhouse gas of anthropogenic origin after CO₂, with a 100-year global warming potential (GWP₁₀₀) estimated at 27–30 times that of CO₂, and which can exceed 80 times over a 20-year horizon (IPCC, 2021). It is responsible for about 30% of the warming observed since the pre-industrial era (Saunois et al., 2020). Its main anthropogenic sources are agriculture, in particular enteric fermentation in ruminants and irrigated rice cultivation, the energy sector and waste management. In Madagascar, emissions are mainly linked to rice cultivation, extensive cattle rearing, wetlands and uncontrolled urban landfills.

Black carbon (BC - Black Carbon)

Black carbon is a carbonaceous aerosol and not a gas, resulting from the incomplete combustion of biomass and fossil fuels. This distinction is important because, unlike gaseous GHGs, it acts directly on the radiative balance by absorbing solar radiation in particulate form, with a positive radiative forcing estimated at +1,1 W/m² at the global scale (Bond et al., 2013; IPCC, 2021). In addition to its climate impact, it is associated with serious health effects, in particular respiratory and cardiovascular diseases. In Madagascar, its main sources are agricultural burning (tavy), savannah fires, the combustion of fuelwood and charcoal for domestic cooking, and urban transport using poor-quality fuels (Ramanathan and Carmichael, 2008).

Hydrofluorocarbons (HFC)

HFCs are synthetic fluorinated gases introduced as substitutes for CFCs and HCFCs under the Montreal Protocol. Although they do not destroy the ozone layer, their GWP₁₀₀ is extremely high, ranging from 138 for HFC-152a to more than 14 000 for HFC-23 (IPCC, 2021). Their emissions are growing rapidly with the development of the refrigeration, air-conditioning and urban trade sectors. In Madagascar, population growth and urbanisation are accelerating demand for refrigeration equipment, making HFCs an emerging source of emissions. The Kigali Amendment to the Montreal Protocol, ratified by Madagascar, governs their progressive reduction and opens up prospects for a transition towards refrigerants with a low climate impact (UNEP, 2016; 2017).

Objectives

As part of the revision of its Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC 3.0), Madagascar aims to strengthen the integration of PCCDV into its national climate policies. This orientation responds to the need for more detailed analyses making it possible to better understand the spatial dynamics of emissions, to identify priority intervention areas and to assess short-term mitigation potential. Despite the existence of national greenhouse gas inventories, the available information remains largely aggregated at the national scale and does not allow the geographical distribution of emissions of methane (CH₄), black carbon (BC) and hydrofluorocarbons (HFC) to be captured precisely. In this context, the development of spatial and prospective modelling approaches appears essential to support climate planning, the tracking of NDC commitments and the identification of the environmental and health co-benefits associated with reducing PCCDV.

Objective 1: Spatio-temporal modelling

The first objective is to develop three spatio-temporal models making it possible to represent and map the spatial distribution of the main PCCDV in Madagascar, namely methane (CH₄), black carbon (BC) and hydrofluorocarbons (HFC). This modelling aims to identify the highest-emitting areas as well as the dynamics of emission trends over time, in order to provide a sound scientific basis for the integration of non-CO₂ pollutants into Madagascar's NDC 3.0.

Objective 2: Construction of emission scenarios

The second objective is to develop several prospective emission scenarios based on the models developed. A "Business As Usual" (BAU) reference scenario will first be constructed in order to project the evolution of emissions without additional intervention. On this basis, reduction scenarios aligned with national climate ambitions will be developed, including an intermediate target for 2030 corresponding to an emission reduction of 10 % (unconditional) to 19 % (conditional) relative to the BAU, as well as a final target for 2035 aiming at a reduction of 16 % (unconditional) to 30 % (conditional). Two additional scenarios will also be constructed in order to enrich the prospective analysis. The first incorporates the Land Use, Land-Use Change and Forestry (UTCATF) dimension, which takes into account the interactions between land-cover dynamics, in particular deforestation, agricultural burning and ecosystem degradation, and emissions of methane and black carbon. This scenario makes it possible to assess the extent to which forest conservation and restoration policies can contribute to reducing PCCDV at the national scale. The second is an accelerated urbanisation scenario, proposed by the International Institute for Environment and Development (IIED), whose work underlines the need to explicitly integrate urbanisation trajectories into emission projections for African countries, where urban growth is one of the main drivers of the future evolution of PCCDV emissions (IIED, 2023). This scenario models the evolution of emissions in a context of population growth and rapid urban expansion, taking into account the transformation of energy consumption patterns, the increase in demand for refrigeration and air conditioning, and the intensification of urban waste generation.

Methodology

The modelling of PCCDV emissions is based on a hybrid methodological architecture combining satellite remote sensing, machine learning and scenario projections. Methane (CH₄) modelling Satellite data Four annual environmental variables were used as predictors, covering the main determinants of CH₄ emissions in tropical ecosystems (Poulter et al., 2017; Saunois et al., 2020).

Brown et al. (2022)

The atmospheric concentration of CH₄ (ppb), used as the target variable of the model, is derived from the TROPOMI sensor on board Sentinel-5P (Hu et al., 2018; Lorente et al., 2021). It should be specified that this concentration constitutes an integrative signal of surface emissions and not a direct flux. Emission fluxes are estimated independently at pixel scale using sectoral emission factors from the IPCC guidelines, with the modelled concentration serving as a spatial validation variable to check the consistency between areas of high atmospheric concentration and areas of high estimated flux. Data pre-processing Sentinel-2 images are used at surface reflectance level (Level-2A), with masking of clouds and their shadows by the scene classification layer provided with the product (ESA, 2021). Each variable is aggregated at annual scale using a statistical reducer suited to its nature: median for reflectance data, mean for surface temperature, annual total for precipitation and mode for land cover.

Machine learning modelling The relationship between the environmental predictors and the atmospheric concentration of CH₄ is modelled using the Random Forest algorithm in regression mode (Breiman, 2001), recognised for its robustness to non-linear relationships and to multi-source remote sensing data (Pandey et al., 2019). The model is trained randomly over the entire territory, on the 2019 reference year, and then applied to each year of the 2019–2025 period. Model performance is assessed by ten-fold cross-validation (10-fold cross-validation), using three metrics: the coefficient of determination (R²), the root mean square error (RMSE, in ppb) and the mean absolute error (MAE). A complementary temporal validation is carried out by comparing the predicted concentrations with the TROPOMI observations available for the years 2020–2025 (Kohavi, 1995). Calculation of emission fluxes

CH₄ fluxes are estimated by assigning to each land cover class an emission factor (EF, in kg CH₄ ha⁻¹ j⁻¹) derived from the IPCC guidelines and from the specialised literature on tropical ecosystems (IPCC, 2006):

The annual CH₄ flux per pixel is calculated according to the Tier 1 methodology of the IPCC guidelines (IPCC, 2006, Vol. 4, Ch. 2): CH𝟒 (t an−𝟏) = 𝑬𝑭𝒊× 𝑺ha × 𝟑𝟔𝟓÷ 𝟏𝟎𝟎𝟎 Where: 𝐸𝐹𝑖: emission factor of class 𝑖(kg ha⁻¹ j⁻¹) 𝑆ha: pixel area in hectares. The emission factors used are drawn from the international literature and from the Tier 1 IPCC guidelines, which constitute the methodological reference in the absence of direct local measurements. Their applicability to the Malagasy context rests on the assumption that biogeochemical processes are comparable to those documented in analogous tropical ecosystems. Local specificities, such as tanety rice-growing practices or the characteristics of coastal wetlands, could generate deviations from the reference values, a limitation to be addressed in later phases through the collection of field data.

Black Carbon modelling

Satellite data Six satellite products are used, covering different dimensions of the combustion signal, burned area detection and air quality (Giglio et al., 2016; Lyapustin et al., 2018; van der Werf et al., 2010):

Filipponi (2018)

The approach is based on the complementarity of two types of satellite signal. MODIS FRP detects active fires at the time of the satellite overpass and provides information on the instantaneous energy intensity of combustion. NBR and BAIS-2, derived from Sentinel-2 images, make it possible to map burn scars after the fire has passed, including low-intensity fires not detected by MODIS. This combination thus offers more complete coverage of BC emissions linked to vegetation fires, agricultural burning and deforestation in Madagascar. It should be noted, however, that this approach orients the modelling towards fire-related emission sources. Diffuse emissions from domestic biomass combustion and urban transport, although significant in Madagascar, are not directly captured by these satellite signals and constitute a methodological limitation to be explicitly acknowledged. CO and NO₂, gaseous tracers co-emitted with BC during biomass combustion, strengthen the estimation of black carbon concentration (van der Werf et al., 2010). The absorbing aerosol index (AAI), derived from S5P/TROPOMI, is used as a qualitative indicator of the presence of absorbing carbonaceous aerosols in the atmospheric column, retaining only positive values (Torres et al., 1998). Data pre-processing FRP data are extracted from the MaxFRP band and converted into MW by applying a factor of 0,1. The MODIS Terra and Aqua collections are merged by monthly maximum aggregation in order to maximise fire detection. Since AOD values are masked, the scale factor of 0,001 is used. Physical quality filters are also applied to the TROPOMI data (CO: 0–0,5 mol/m²; NO₂: 0–0,001 mol/m²). For Sentinel-2 images, burned areas are identified by the simultaneous application of the following two spectral criteria: NBR < 0 and BAIS-2 > 0,87. Only pixels satisfying both conditions are retained as confirmed burned areas. Each variable is then aggregated at monthly scale by the mean of valid pixels, with the exception of FRP, for which a maximum composite is retained.

Spectral indices for burned area detection The NBR is calculated from the NIR (B8, 842 nm) and SWIR (B12, 2 190 nm) bands of Sentinel-2: NBR = B8 −B12

Where: Bn: satellite image band B4: Red (0,665 µm) B6: Red Edge Vegetation 1 (0,744 µm) B7: Red Edge Vegetation 2 (0,783 µm) B8: Near Infrared 1 (0,842 µm) B8A: Near Infrared 2 (0,865 µm) B12: Mid Infrared (2,190 µm) Negative values (NBR < 0) indicate moderate to high burn severity (Key and Benson, 2006). Values above 0,87 indicate the presence of recently burned areas (Filipponi, 2018). Machine learning modelling As in the CH₄ modelling, the relationship between the satellite predictors and the black carbon concentration is used by the Random Forest algorithm in regression mode (Breiman, 2001). The model is trained on the 2019 reference year and applied to the 2019–2025 period. Performance is assessed by ten-fold cross-validation, using the R², RMSE and MAE metrics (Kohavi, 1995). Calculation of black carbon emissions Monthly BC emissions are estimated from fire radiative power (FRP) and from the area of burned zones detected by NBR and BAIS-2, following the approach of Vermote et al. (2009) and Ichoku and Kaufman (2005): BCmasse = FRP × 𝑬𝑭𝑩𝑪× 𝚫𝒕 Where:

𝐸𝐹𝐵𝐶= 0,28 g BC MJ−1: black carbon emission factor for African vegetation (Andreae and Merlet, 2001; Akagi et al., 2011), Δ𝑡: monthly duration in seconds and FRP is expressed in MW (= MJ s⁻¹). The burned areas identified by Sentinel-2 serve as a spatial mask to constrain emission estimates to pixels confirmed as affected by fires, thereby reducing the risk of overestimation in areas of MODIS false positives. The monthly values are summed to obtain the annual flux per pixel, then integrated over the national area to produce an annual national estimate.

HFC modelling

Satellite data The modelling of HFCs is based on an approach different from that adopted for CH₄ and BC. HFCs do not benefit from dedicated satellite sensors offering operational spatial coverage at national scale, because of their low concentrations

in the atmosphere and the complexity of their spectral signature (Rigby et al., 2014; Velders et al., 2015). The spatial modelling of HFC emissions therefore relies on an approach based on the spatial disaggregation of sectoral activity data, combined with satellite proxy variables representative of demand for refrigeration and air conditioning.

These variables are indirect proxies for demand for refrigeration and air-conditioning equipment, the main drivers of HFC emissions in Madagascar. Night-time lights and urban density make it possible to identify areas where commercial and residential activities are concentrated, while land surface temperature indicates the climatic pressure driving cooling demand. Data pre-processing All variables are reprojected and resampled to a common resolution of 10 km, consistent with the uncertainties inherent in the spatial disaggregation approach for HFCs. Land surface temperature is aggregated as an annual mean. Night-time lights are aggregated as a monthly median and then summed annually in order to capture seasonal variations in economic activity. Urban density and land cover are used as static variables, updated according to the availability of the annual vintages of the products. Spatial disaggregation approach In the absence of direct atmospheric measurements of HFCs at the national scale, total emissions are first estimated at the national level from sectoral activity data, then disaggregated spatially using a weighting that combines the satellite proxy variables. This approach is consistent with the IPCC methodological guidance for national inventories of fluorinated gases (IPCC, 2006, Vol. 3, Ch. 7). Spatial disaggregation is carried out by weighting the pixels according to a composite index 𝑊𝑖 calculated for each 10 km pixel 𝑖, corresponding to the spatial resolution of the disaggregation grid adopted, compatible with the resolution of GHSL: 𝑾𝒊= 𝜶× LST𝒊+ 𝜷× Urban𝒊+ 𝜸× NTL𝒊 Where: LST𝑖, Urban𝑖 and NTL𝑖 are the normalized values of land surface temperature, urban density and night-time lights respectively, 𝛼, 𝛽, 𝛾: the weighting coefficients determined by optimization on the available activity data, with 𝛼+ 𝛽+ 𝛾= 1. The spatialized emission per pixel is then:
