Machine learning modelling The relationship between the proxy variables and the spatial distribution of HFC emissions is refined using the Random Forest algorithm in regression mode (Breiman, 2001), trained on the 2019 reference year and applied to the period 2019–2025. Performance is assessed by ten-fold cross-validation, using the R², RMSE and MAE metrics (Kohavi, 1995).

Development of the scenarios

Five forward-looking scenarios are developed from the spatio-temporal models produced, covering the period 2025–2035. They make it possible to explore different trajectories for the evolution of PCCDV emissions in Madagascar, from the reference trend through to conditional climate ambitions, incorporating sectoral and territorial dimensions specific to the Malagasy context. Historical emissions baseline (2019–2025) The construction of the scenarios draws on the historical emissions estimated by the spatio-temporal models over the period 2019–2025, which constitute the common reference base for all the scenarios. This period makes it possible to characterize recent trends in CH₄, BC and HFC emissions in Madagascar, to identify the underlying sectoral and spatial dynamics, and to establish the calibration parameters of the projection models. The year 2019 is retained as the absolute reference year, consistent with the training year of the Random Forest models. Projection methods The temporal projections of emissions over the period 2025–2035 are based on a mixed approach combining three complementary methods, the choice of which is adapted to the nature of the dynamics modelled (Box and Jenkins, 1976; Pontius et al., 2001). The compound annual growth rate (CAGR) is used to represent average trends in the evolution of emissions where these follow a regular and predictable trajectory. It is applied in particular to HFC emissions, whose growth is closely correlated with stable structural factors such as demographics and equipment ownership rates: E𝒕= E𝟎× (𝟏+ CAGR)𝒕 Where: E𝑡: emissions in year 𝑡, E0: emissions in the 2019 reference year CAGR: compound annual growth rate estimated over the 2019–2025 historical period. ARIMA (AutoRegressive Integrated Moving Average) models are used to incorporate the non-linear fluctuations and temporal anomalies observed in the emission series, in particular for CH₄ and BC, whose dynamics are more sensitive to interannual climate variability, rainfall regimes, droughts and the intensity of vegetation fires (Box and Jenkins, 1976). The ARIMA parameters (p, d, q) are estimated by analysing the autocorrelation and partial autocorrelation functions of the historical series. Markov chains and CA-Markov (Cellular Automata - Markov) models are used to simulate future land-cover changes, in order to better represent the local spatial dynamics of emissions, in particular in areas subject to rapid urbanization and to deforestation pressures (Pontius et al., 2001). These models make it possible to estimate the transition probabilities between land-cover classes, forest to agriculture, rural to urban, and to derive from them the spatial changes in the emission factors associated with the UTCATF and urbanization scenarios.

Scenario 1: BAU (Business As Usual) reference The BAU scenario constitutes the reference trajectory against which all the other scenarios are assessed. It projects the evolution of CH₄, BC and HFC emissions in the absence of additional intervention, maintaining the current trends in the environmental, demographic and sectoral variables observed over the period 2019–2025. This scenario reflects the continuation of existing practices without additional mitigation measures.

Scenario 2: Unconditional NDC (2030–2035) This scenario models the implementation of the national climate commitments achievable with Madagascar's domestic resources alone, in line with the unconditional targets of NDC 3.0. The projections are constructed by applying progressive annual reduction coefficients to the BAU trajectories, with intermediate objectives incorporated across the whole period 2025–2035:

  • a 10 % reduction in emissions by 2030 compared with BAU;

  • a 16 % reduction in emissions by 2035 compared with BAU.

Scenario 3: Conditional NDC (2030–2035) This scenario represents a strengthened implementation of the climate commitments, conditional on the mobilization of international financing, technology transfer and the strengthening of national capacities. It reflects the maximum level of ambition of Madagascar's NDC 3.0, with the following targets compared with the BAU scenario:

  • a 19 % reduction in emissions by 2030;

  • a 30 % reduction in emissions by 2035.

Scenario 4: Strong Growth and Urban Sprawl This scenario, inspired by the analytical framework proposed by the International Institute for Environment and Development (IIED), models accelerated urbanization characterized by the rapid expansion of urban areas in correlation with projected population growth (IIED, 2023). Land-cover changes are simulated by CA-Markov models, calibrated on the urbanization trends observed between 2019 and 2025. This scenario takes into account:

  • the increase in demand for refrigeration and air-conditioning equipment, resulting in a rise in HFC emissions;

  • the intensification of urban waste generation and the transformation of energy consumption patterns, exerting increased pressure on CH₄ emissions;

  • the development of urban transport and the growth of motorized mobility, contributing to the increase in BC emissions.

Scenario 5: UTCATF Mitigation This scenario models the interactions between land-cover dynamics and CH₄ and BC emissions, incorporating the Land Use, Land-Use Change and Forestry (UTCATF) component. Land-cover transitions are simulated by Markov chains, incorporating forest conservation constraints and the reduction of agricultural slash-and-burn practices.

The scenario thus assesses the extent to which forest conservation and restoration policies can contribute to reducing PCCDV emissions at the national scale, taking into account the specific features of Malagasy ecosystems, coastal wetlands, dense forests and savannahs (ONE, 2022).

Validation of the model results

Conversions into CO₂ equivalent Comparing the estimates produced by this study with those of other work requires harmonization of the units in which emissions are expressed. A systematic conversion is applied using the 100-year Global Warming Potential (GWP₁₀₀) specific to each pollutant, in line with the IPCC guidance (IPCC, 2021): ECO𝟐eq (Gg CO𝟐eq) = Ecompound (Gg) × GWP𝟏𝟎𝟎

For HFCs, in the absence of disaggregation by type of refrigerant fluid, the same assumptions as those adopted in the Madagascar BTR are applied, treating HFCs as an aggregate group with a unit GWP₁₀₀.

Table 57: Table of conversions into CO₂eq (CH₄, BC, HFC)

Compound

Conversion applied

Basis

CH₄

1 Gg CH₄ = 28 Gg CO₂eq

GWP₁₀₀ (IPCC AR6; 2021)

BC

1 Gg BC = 900 Gg CO₂eq

GWP₁₀₀ (Bond et al.; 2013; IPCC AR6 2021)

HFC

1 Gg HFC = 1 Gg CO₂eq

Madagascar BTR assumption (2026)

It should be noted that the GWP₁₀₀ of BC is associated with significantly higher uncertainty than that of gaseous GHGs, owing to the complexity of its direct and indirect radiative effects (Bond et al., 2013). Spatial validation with EDGAR Spatial validation of the modelled estimates is carried out by comparison with the EDGAR database (Emissions Database for Global Atmospheric Research), developed by the Joint Research Centre (JRC) of the European Commission. EDGAR is the international reference for atmospheric emission inventories, covering more than 200 countries and territories since 1970, with full sectoral disaggregation (energy, industry, agriculture, transport and waste) for all the main GHGs and air pollutants, including CH₄, HFCs, CO₂, N₂O and BC (Crippa et al., 2021). Its methodology is based on the product of activities and emission factors, in line with the IPCC guidelines, which makes it a methodologically consistent basis for comparison with the approach adopted in the present study. The EDGAR gridded datasets are used to compare, pixel by pixel, the spatial distribution of the modelled CH₄, BC and HFC emissions with the corresponding EDGAR estimates for Madagascar. This comparison makes it possible to assess the spatial consistency of the model, to identify areas of over- or under-estimation and to qualify the robustness of the spatial patterns produced. National validation with the Madagascar BTR 2026 Validation of the national estimates is carried out by cross-checking with Madagascar's Biennial Transparency Report (BTR, 2026), the official reporting instrument of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). Under the Paris Agreement and the enhanced transparency framework, the BTR compiles the national GHG inventory, mitigation policies and measures, emission projections and progress towards the NDC targets (UNFCCC, 2015). The national CH₄ and HFC estimates from the model are compared with the corresponding values reported in the Madagascar BTR in order to assess the consistency of the orders of magnitude and of the temporal trends. It should be noted that the BTR does not cover black carbon (BC), as it is not accounted for as a greenhouse gas under UNFCCC reporting. Validation of BC emissions therefore relies exclusively on comparison with the EDGAR gridded datasets and on the scientific literature available on BC emissions in sub-Saharan Africa.

Limitations

Black carbon (BC) modelling The modelling relies on satellite signals geared towards the detection of active fires and burn scars, which constitutes a structural limitation of the approach. Diffuse emissions from domestic biomass combustion are not directly captured by MODIS FRP products or by the NBR and BAIS-2 indices, which are designed for the thermal signatures characteristic of open-air vegetation fires. This limitation is particularly significant in the Malagasy context, where more than 90 % of the population uses solid fuels for cooking and where the residential sector is estimated to account for close to 8 % of national air pollution sources. The estimates produced could therefore underestimate actual BC flows, particularly in rural areas and indoor spaces, and should be regarded as floor values rather than as an exhaustive quantification of national emissions. Sectoral attribution The modelling produces a spatial distribution of CH₄, BC and HFC emissions at pixel level, but does not allow an explicit sectoral disaggregation of emission sources. Sectoral attribution relies solely on the land-cover class associated with each pixel, used as an indirect proxy for the emitting sector. This approach does not make it possible to distinguish, within a single pixel, the respective contribution of several coexisting sources, for example CH₄ emissions linked simultaneously to livestock and rice cultivation in the same agricultural area, or BC emissions arising jointly from slash-and-burn practices and domestic combustion in peri-urban areas. Consequently, the results are not directly comparable with conventional sectoral inventories structured according to the IPCC nomenclature (energy, agriculture, waste, industrial processes), which limits their direct use for tracking the sectoral commitments of NDC 3.0 and calls for cautious interpretation when comparing them with the Madagascar BTR data.

Results

Model of the distribution of CH₄ emissions Spatial distribution of CH₄ The spatio-temporal evolution of CH₄ emissions in Madagascar over the period 2019–2025 reveals a heterogeneous spatial distribution, structured around two broad types of source: agricultural and natural wetland ecosystems on the one hand, and densely populated urban areas on the other. The highest concentrations are persistently located in the major rice-growing basins of the central Highlands, Lake Alaotra, the Sofia plain and the Marovoay valley. These areas correspond to Madagascar's main irrigated rice-growing regions, where the anaerobic conditions of flooded soils favour intensive production of biogenic methane through microbial fermentation, consistent with the high emission factors assigned to the flooded vegetation class in the model. The recurrence of these hotspots throughout the period confirms the structural nature of these sources and their direct link with the cropping calendar of Malagasy rice cultivation. The spatial analysis also highlights coastal mangrove areas as non-negligible sources of CH₄ emissions, particularly on the north-western and eastern coasts of the island. Although mangroves play a major role in blue carbon sequestration and long-term climate mitigation, they generate natural methane emissions through anaerobic decomposition of organic matter in their sediments. These emissions are amplified by mangrove deforestation and degradation, which expose sediments rich in organic carbon to accelerated decomposition processes. These sources are often absent from, or underestimated in, conventional national inventories, which is one of the contributions of the present spatial modelling. Over the period as a whole, the maps reveal a slight increase in the maximum values of the legend, rising from 0,011–0,016 Gg per pixel in 2019 to 0,011–0,018 Gg per pixel in 2025, indicating a trend towards intensification of emissions in the highest-emitting areas, consistent with the expansion of rice-growing areas and the population growth observed in Madagascar.

Map 1: Spatial distribution of CH₄ (2019–2025)

National temporal evolution and comparison with EDGAR and the Madagascar BTR The methane emissions estimated by the model show an overall upward trajectory over the period, rising from 753 Gg in 2019 to 914 Gg in 2025, an increase of about 19 %. This trend is consistent with that observed by the two reference sources: EDGAR rises from 801 to 900 Gg over the same period, while the BTR shows values of between 750 and 842 Gg for the years available (2019–2023). The differences between the model and EDGAR remain moderate throughout the period, ranging from 1 Gg (2024) to 48 Gg (2019), i.e. differences of less than 6 % in most cases. The BTR shows slightly more variable values, particularly in 2022 where the gap with the model reaches about 92 Gg, which is explained in part by different inventory methodologies. These differences nevertheless fall within the margins of error usually accepted for this type of estimate at the national scale. In the Malagasy context, this gradual rise in CH₄ emissions reflects the expansion of agricultural activities, in particular irrigated rice cultivation and livestock farming, which are the main sources of methane. The generally good alignment between the model and the reference studies strengthens the credibility of the estimates produced and their usefulness in guiding national climate policies.

Figure 1: Comparison of the CH₄ model results with EDGAR and the BTR (2019–2025)

Model of the distribution of BC emissions

Spatial distribution of BC The spatio-temporal mapping of black carbon emissions over the period 2019–2025 reveals a spatial profile distinct from that of CH₄ and HFCs, characterized by strong interannual spatial variations reflecting the episodic and diffuse nature of combustion sources. The highest values are mainly located in areas subject to agricultural fires, tavy practices and vegetation fires, particularly visible in the savannah regions of the centre and west of the island, as well as in the forest-agriculture transition zones of the Highlands and the east coast. The temporal analysis reveals significant interannual fluctuations. The year 2020 shows the highest maximum values of the period (0,003–0,005 Gg per pixel), suggesting a particularly intense fire season. This intensity is maintained in 2023 (0,003–0,0052 Gg per pixel) and 2025 (0,003–0,0047 Gg per pixel), while the years 2022 and 2024 record the lowest maximum values (0,003–0,004 and 0,003–0,005 Gg per pixel respectively), reflecting interannual variability consistent with climatic fluctuations and the seasonal dynamics of fires in Madagascar. Emissions linked to urban transport and industrial production are captured by the aerosol sensors in the form of pixels with moderate emissions around the main urban centres. Emissions from domestic (indoor) combustion, for their part, are not captured by the satellite approach, which is a limitation discussed in the methodological section.

Map 2: Spatial distribution of BC (2019–2025)

National temporal evolution and comparison with EDGAR and the Madagascar BTR The black carbon emissions estimated by the model remain within a relatively stable range over the whole period, between 14,7 and 16,4 Gg, with no clear upward or downward trend. EDGAR, available only for 2019–2022, shows values of between 14,8 and 15,4 Gg, very close to those of the model. The differences between the two sources do not exceed 1,1 Gg, which corresponds to a maximum difference of about 7 %. This closeness between the model and EDGAR over the common years demonstrates good methodological consistency, despite the absence of EDGAR data after 2022 and the lack of a BTR source for this pollutant. For the years 2023 to 2025, the model remains the only reference available, which is a useful contribution to filling this information gap in national inventories. In Madagascar, BC emissions are mainly linked to biomass combustion, deforestation fires, charcoal production and the use of traditional stoves, as well as to transport. The stability of the estimated levels suggests that these practices have not undergone any major change over the period, even if energy transition efforts could, in time, alter this trend.

Figure 2: Comparison of the BC model results with EDGAR (2019–2025)

Model of the distribution of HFC emissions

Spatial distribution of HFCs Unlike methane and black carbon, whose distribution covers large parts of the national territory, HFC emissions are characterized by point-source, strongly localized concentration in Madagascar's main urban centres. The most intense emission hotspots are persistently concentrated in four large urban centres: Antananarivo, the capital and main economic centre of the country; Antsirabe, the second industrial city; Fianarantsoa, the major urban centre of the southern Highlands; and Toamasina, Madagascar's leading commercial port. This geography of emissions directly reflects

the spatial distribution of the commercial, industrial and residential activities that generate demand for refrigeration and air conditioning. The temporal evolution of the maps reveals a trend of continuous increase in the maximum emission values per pixel, rising from 10,01–35 Gg in 2019 to 10,01–38,5 Gg in 2025, reflecting a sustained increase in demand for refrigeration equipment under the combined effect of urban population growth, rising living standards in urban centres and the expansion of the commercial cold chain sector. This trend is consistent with the projections of the Kigali Amendment, which anticipates strong growth in HFC emissions in rapidly urbanizing developing countries over the period 2020–2035 (UNEP, 2016).

Map 3: Spatial distribution of HFCs (2019–2025)

National temporal evolution and comparison with EDGAR and the Madagascar BTR HFC emissions show the most marked increase among the three gases analysed, with a continuous rise from 199 Gg in 2019 to 306 Gg in 2025 for the model, an increase of almost 54 % in six years. The BTR, available up to 2023, confirms this trend with values ranging from 194 to 283 Gg. The two sources evolve in parallel, with differences generally remaining below 25 Gg, i.e. less than 10 % of the estimated values. The slight differences observed between the model and the BTR, particularly in 2022 and 2023 where the BTR values are slightly higher, may be explained by different assumptions on leakage rates from refrigeration and air-conditioning equipment, or by differences in the activity data used. These variations remain within acceptable limits and do not call into question the overall consistency of the two approaches. This sustained growth in HFCs in Madagascar is directly linked to the expansion of the air-conditioning and refrigeration market, itself driven by increasing urbanization and rising living standards in certain segments of the population. This finding underlines the importance for the country of engaging fully in the implementation of the Kigali Amendment, which aims precisely to phase down the use of these gases with high global warming potential.

Figure 3: Comparison of HFC model results with BTR (2019–2025)

Projections of CH₄ emissions Spatial distribution of the 2030 projections: spatial distribution of the scenarios The mapping of CH₄ emission projections to 2030 confirms and amplifies the spatial structure observed over the historical period, with hotspots persisting in the large rice-growing basins of the Highlands, Alaotra, Sofia and Marovoay, as well as in the coastal mangrove areas. The four scenarios share this basic spatial architecture but differ markedly in the intensity and extent of the high-emission areas. The BAU scenario projects maximum values of 0,011–0,025 Gg per pixel, reflecting the continuation of current trends in rice-farming expansion and uncontrolled waste management.

The conditional scenario shows the lowest maximum values (0,011–0,020 Gg per pixel), reflecting the effect of measures for the sustainable management of rice paddies and the reduction of waste-related emissions. The unconditional scenario stands at an intermediate level (0,011–0,022 Gg per pixel), showing the national effort on waste management and control of rice-farming practices, with a moderate attenuation of the hotspots in the rice-growing basins compared with BAU. The urbanization scenario is distinguished by a significant extension of the moderately emitting areas, shown in light green and yellow, across the whole of the Highlands and the west coast, reflecting the effect of the conversion of agricultural land on the urban periphery and the intensification of rice-farming practices to meet the food needs of a growing urban population, with maximum values equivalent to BAU (0,011–0,025 Gg per pixel).

Map 4: Spatial projections of CH₄ in 2030

Comparative analysis of national emissions in 2030: quantitative assessment by scenario By 2030, the national CH₄ projections reveal substantial gaps between scenarios, consistent with the targets of NDC 3.0. The BAU reference scenario, characterized by the continuation of current trends with no additional mitigation measure, projects emissions of 1 252 Gg. The unconditional scenario reaches 1 127 Gg, a reduction of 125 Gg (−10 %) compared with BAU, corresponding to the achievable national measures for sustainable management of rice paddies and reduction of waste-related emissions. The conditional scenario reaches 1 015 Gg, the largest reduction at 237 Gg (−19 %) compared with BAU, reflecting the effect of enhanced interventions on low-emission rice-farming techniques, waste management and wetland restoration. The urbanization scenario, by contrast, projects 1 403 Gg, i.e. +151 Gg above BAU (+12 %), reflecting the combined pressure of peri-urban agricultural intensification and growth in urban food demand on CH₄ emissions.

Figure 4: Projection of CH₄ emissions for 2030

Spatial distribution of the 2035 projections: spatial distribution of the scenarios By 2035, the maps reveal a general intensification of emissions compared with 2030 in all scenarios, with gaps between scenarios widening significantly. The BAU scenario projects the highest maximum values (0,011–0,032 Gg per pixel), with a notable extension of hotspots in the rice-growing basins and the degraded mangrove areas. The conditional scenario maintains the lowest maximum values (0,011–0,022 Gg per pixel), confirming the capacity of the conditional measures to contain the intensification of emissions over the long term. The unconditional scenario is positioned at an intermediate level, with a perceptible reduction of hotspots in the rice-growing basins but a persistence of diffuse emissions in the savannah and wet grassland areas. The urbanization scenario shows a distinctive spatial characteristic: a generalized light-green colouring across the whole territory, indicating a rise in the baseline level of diffuse emissions linked to land conversion and agricultural intensification, with maximum values almost equivalent to BAU (0,011–0,031 Gg per pixel).

Map 5: Spatial projections of CH₄ in 2035

Comparative analysis of national emissions in 2035: quantitative assessment by scenario By 2035, the gaps between scenarios reach their maximum level over the whole projection period. The BAU scenario rises significantly to 1 628 Gg, an increase of 376 Gg compared with 2030, reflecting the cumulative effect of rice-farming expansion and the growth of uncontrolled waste. The conditional scenario maintains a controlled trajectory at 1 140 Gg, a reduction of 488 Gg (−30 %) compared with BAU 2035, demonstrating the growing effectiveness of national measures over the long term. The unconditional scenario projects 1 368 Gg, i.e. 260 Gg below BAU (−16 %), a smaller gap than in 2030 which warrants particular attention. It suggests that without a progressive strengthening of the conditional measures, their relative effectiveness tends to erode in the face of the growth dynamics of agricultural sources. The urbanization scenario projects 2 196 Gg, i.e. +568 Gg above BAU 2035 (+35 %), signalling a non-linear acceleration effect that is particularly pronounced for CH₄, where the conversion of peri-urban agricultural land and rice-farming intensification combine to generate emissions far above the reference trend.

Figure 5: Projection of CH₄ emissions for 2035

Projections of BC emissions

Spatial distribution of the 2030 projections: spatial distribution of the scenarios The mapping of black carbon emission projections to 2030 reveals a diffuse spatial distribution across the whole territory, contrasting with the point concentration observed for HFCs. The four scenarios share the same basic spatial structure, dominated by the savannah areas, the forest-agriculture transition areas and the agricultural burning areas of the centre and the west, but differ in the intensity and extent of the high-emission pixels. The BAU scenario projects maximum values of 0,003–0,005 Gg per pixel, reflecting the continuation of current trends in agricultural fire and tavy practices with no additional intervention. The conditional scenario shows the lowest maximum values (0,003–0,0044 Gg per pixel), reflecting the effect of policies to reduce burning and restore forests on the intensity of the hotspots. The unconditional scenario stands at an intermediate level, reflecting the partial effect of national measures to reduce agricultural fires and manage forests without external support, with a moderate attenuation of the hotspots in the savannah and forest-agriculture transition areas compared with BAU. The urbanization scenario is distinguished by a notable extension of the emitting areas towards the urban peripheries and the road corridors, with maximum values equivalent to BAU (0,003– 0,005 Gg per pixel), reflecting the growing contribution of transport and peri-urban activities to BC emissions.

Map 6: Spatial projections of BC in 2030

Comparative analysis of national emissions in 2030: quantitative assessment by scenario By 2030, the national BC projections stand as follows compared with the BAU reference scenario (17,26 Gg). The unconditional scenario projects emissions of 15,54 Gg, a reduction of 1,72 Gg compared with BAU, corresponding to the implementation of the national fire-reduction measures achievable with domestic resources alone. The conditional scenario reaches 14,50 Gg, the largest reduction at 2,76 Gg compared with BAU, reflecting the effect of enhanced land management and energy substitution policies supported by international financing. The urbanization scenario, by contrast, projects 17,68 Gg, i.e. more than 0,42 Gg above BAU, reflecting the additional pressure exerted by the growth of urban transport and the intensification of peri-urban activities on BC emissions.

Figure 6: Projection of BC emissions for 2030

Spatial distribution of the 2035 projections: spatial distribution of the scenarios

By 2035, the spatial distribution of the BC projections shows a notable change compared with 2030, with a generalized intensification of the emitting areas in all scenarios.

The urbanization scenario stands out in particular through a marked extension of the high-emission pixels towards the Highlands and the east coast, with maximum values reaching 0,003– 0,006 Gg per pixel, the highest of all the scenarios at that horizon, reflecting the cumulative effect of urban expansion on land-use dynamics and burning practices on the outskirts of the cities. The conditional and unconditional scenarios, for their part, maintain a relative contraction of the most intense hotspots, although their spatial extent remains comparable to that of BAU.

Map 7: Spatial projections of BC in 2035

Comparative analysis of national emissions in 2035: quantitative assessment by scenario By 2035, the gaps between scenarios narrow compared with 2030, with the exception of the urbanization scenario, which diverges further. The BAU scenario rises slightly to 17,59 Gg, a moderate increase compared with 2030, driven by the trend growth of agricultural fire and tavy practices. The unconditional scenario reaches 17,34 Gg, a reduction of only 0,25 Gg compared with BAU, indicating a weakening of the effectiveness of the unconditional measures over the long term in the absence of external support. The conditional scenario maintains the most significant reduction at 16,72 Gg, i.e. 0,87 Gg below BAU, confirming the durability of the conditional sustainable land management and energy transition policies. The urbanization scenario projects 18,27 Gg, i.e. +0,68 Gg above BAU, with a gap that doubles compared with 2030, signalling an acceleration of the effect of urbanization on BC emissions as urban and peri-urban expansion intensifies.

Figure 7: Projection of BC emissions for 2035

Projections of HFC emissions (2030–2035)

Spatial distribution of the 2030 projections: spatial distribution of the scenarios The mapping of HFC emission projections to 2030 confirms the spatial structure observed over the historical period, with a persistent concentration of emissions in the large urban agglomerations. However, the four scenarios differ markedly in the intensity of the hotspots and the extent of the high-emission pixels. The BAU scenario the highest maximum values of all the scenarios, reflecting the continuation of current trends in the growth of demand for refrigeration equipment with no additional intervention. The conditional and unconditional NDC scenarios show a visible reduction in the intensity of the red and orange pixels, particularly in the agglomerations of Antananarivo and Toamasina, reflecting the effect of the mitigation measures on the control of HFC demand. The conditional scenario shows the lowest maximum values, confirming the superior effectiveness of conditional measures supported by international financing and technology transfers. The urbanization scenario is distinguished by a spatial extension of the emitting areas towards secondary agglomerations, with maximum values exceeding those of BAU, reflecting the amplifying effect of rapid urban expansion on demand for refrigeration and air conditioning.

Map 8: Spatial projections of HFCs in 2030

Comparative analysis of national emissions in 2030: quantitative assessment by scenario In quantitative terms, the national projections to 2030 illustrate the extent of the gaps between scenarios compared with BAU. The unconditional scenario allows a reduction of 56 Gg compared with BAU, corresponding to the implementation of the national climate commitments with domestic resources alone. The conditional scenario achieves a reduction of 70 Gg, i.e. the maximum level of ambition of NDC 3.0, conditional on the mobilization of international financing and the transfer of technologies towards low-GWP refrigerants. Conversely, the urbanization scenario projects an increase of +11 Gg above BAU, underlining that, without specific mitigation measures, accelerated urban growth constitutes a factor amplifying HFC emissions in the short term.

Figure 8: Projection of HFC emissions for 2030

Spatial distribution of the 2035 projections: spatial distribution of the scenarios By 2035, the spatial distribution of the scenarios confirms and amplifies the trends observed in 2030. The BAU and urbanization scenarios maintain maximum values identical to those of 2030, indicating a stabilization of the intensity of the hotspots in the large agglomerations, while the spatial extension towards secondary cities continues under the effect of urbanization. The conditional and unconditional scenarios, for their part, maintain their 2030 maximum values, confirming the capacity of the mitigation measures to contain the intensity of emissions in the main urban centres over the whole projection period.

Map 9: Spatial projections of HFCs in 2035

Comparative analysis of national emissions in 2035: quantitative assessment by scenario By 2035, the gaps between scenarios widen significantly compared with 2030, reflecting the cumulative effect of the mitigation and urbanization trajectories over the long term. The unconditional scenario generates a reduction of 49 Gg compared with BAU, slightly lower than that of 2030, suggesting that the unconditional measures are progressively reaching the limits of their effectiveness without external support. The conditional scenario maintains a stable reduction of 70 Gg, demonstrating the robustness and durability of the measures for technological transition towards alternative refrigerants when they are supported by international financing mechanisms. The urbanization scenario, by contrast, projects an increase of +111 Gg above BAU in 2035, i.e. ten times more than in 2030, signalling a non-linear acceleration effect of urbanization on HFC emissions, consistent with the IIED projections on the growth of cooling needs in rapidly urbanizing African countries (IIED, 2023).

Figure 9: Projection of HFC emissions for 2035

Mitigation measures and co-benefits

Mitigation measures by pollutant

Mitigation of CH₄ emissions The projections reveal that CH₄ is the most emitted pollutant in Madagascar, with BAU levels reaching 1 252 Gg in 2030 and 1 628 Gg in 2035. The conditional reduction potential reaches 237 Gg and 488 Gg respectively compared with BAU at the two horizons. The mapping identifies three priority sources for intervention: the large rice-growing basins, the wetlands and the urban landfills.

Rice-farming sector

The rice-growing basins of the Highlands, Alaotra, Sofia and Marovoay constitute the dominant hotspots of CH₄ emissions, confirmed by the persistence of high-emission pixels over the whole 2019–2025 period. Intermittent irrigation techniques (AWD and SRI ) constitute the mitigation measure with the greatest quantitative potential, making it possible to reduce methane emissions by 30 to 70 % by limiting the anaerobic conditions conducive to methanogenic fermentation, while improving agricultural yields (Carrijo et al., 2017; Cornell University, 2021).

Wetlands and mangroves

The mapping highlighted the coastal mangrove areas and the natural wetlands as significant sources of CH₄, often absent from conventional national inventories. Policies for the conservation and restoration of these ecosystems constitute a mitigation measure with a double benefit: reduction of CH₄ emissions by limiting the degradation of organic sediments and strengthening of the blue carbon sink role of these coastal ecosystems.

Urban waste

The urbanization scenario identifies the agglomerations of Antananarivo, Toamasina and Mahajanga as areas of rapid growth in CH₄ emissions linked to uncontrolled landfills. The establishment of engineered landfill sites with biogas capture and the recovery of organic waste through methanization constitute the priority measures for containing this upward trajectory.

Mitigation of BC emissions The BC projections show relatively stable levels under the BAU scenario (17,26 Gg in 2030 and 17,59 Gg in 2035), with a conditional reduction potential of 2,76 Gg in 2030 and 0,87 Gg in 2035. The spatial distribution of emissions directs the mitigation measures towards two lines of action derived directly from the mapping results. ➢ Reduction of agricultural fires and burning

The mapping identifies the savannah regions of the centre and the west, as well as the forest-agriculture transition areas of the Highlands and the east coast, as the dominant hotspots of BC emissions. Tavy practices and fires used to prepare plots constitute the most intense and most spatially extensive sources. The dissemination of agroecological practices as alternatives to burning, improved management of agricultural residues, in particular the recovery of rice husks through controlled pyrolysis into biochar, and the strengthening of fire monitoring through satellite remote sensing constitute the most effective measures for reducing these emissions in the priority areas identified (Schmidt et al., 2021; IPCC AR6, WGIII, 2022).

Urban transport

Aerosol sensors made it possible to identify a contribution of urban transport to BC emissions around the main agglomerations, in particular Antananarivo and Toamasina. The progressive renewal of the vehicle fleet towards less emitting engines and the improvement of fuel quality constitute complementary measures targeting these urban sources.

Mitigation of HFC emissions The HFC projections reveal sustained growth dynamics under the BAU scenario, with a risk of significant overshoot in the event of accelerated urbanization (+111 Gg CO₂eq above BAU in 2035). In other words, the conditional reduction potential is stable and durable (70 Gg CO₂eq at both horizons). The mapping clearly identifies the agglomerations of Antananarivo, Antsirabe, Fianarantsoa and Toamasina as priority areas for intervention, with a progressive extension towards secondary cities between 2019 and 2025.

Transition towards alternative refrigerants

The implementation of the Kigali Amendment, ratified by Madagascar, opens access to the financing mechanisms of the Multilateral Fund of the Montreal Protocol for the transition towards low-GWP refrigerants in the agglomerations identified as priority emission centres (UNEP, 2016; 2017). ➢ Energy efficiency and end-of-life management

Improving the energy efficiency of refrigeration and air-conditioning equipment, combined with the strengthening of national capacities for the recovery and destruction of HFCs at end of life, constitutes a complementary lever whose potential grows with the increase in the stock of equipment in service, which is particularly marked in the secondary cities according to the mapping results. Co-benefits of reducing PCCDV

Climate co-benefits The simultaneous reduction of the three modelled pollutants generates synergistic climate effects in the short and medium term. CH₄, with an atmospheric lifetime of about twelve years, produces a rapid cooling effect where emissions are reduced in the priority rice-growing basins and wetlands. BC, with a GWP₁₀₀ of 900 and an atmospheric lifetime of a few days, offers the most immediate climate benefits, particularly in the fire areas identified by the mapping. HFCs, with GWP₁₀₀ values that can exceed 14 000, represent the most powerful climate lever per unit of mass reduced in the urban agglomerations (Bond et al., 2013; IPCC, 2021). The combined reduction of CH₄ and BC also contributes to reducing the formation of tropospheric ozone, a secondary pollutant with negative climate and health effects, generating additional climate co-benefits not directly accounted for in the national inventories (UNEP, 2020).

Health co-benefits The reduction of BC in the agricultural fire areas and around the urban agglomerations directly improves outdoor air quality, reducing the incidence of respiratory and cardiovascular diseases associated with exposure to fine particulate matter. The transition towards practices alternative to burning and towards less emitting fuels in urban transport generates measurable health benefits among the populations living near the fire areas and in the urban centres (Ramanathan and Carmichael, 2008). The reduction of CH₄ emissions from uncontrolled urban landfills, identified as growing sources under the urbanization scenario, improves health conditions in the rapidly growing agglomerations by limiting biogas production and the nuisances associated with the anaerobic decomposition of waste.

Environmental and agricultural co-benefits The reduction of vegetation fires and agricultural burning in the areas identified by the mapping preserves forest ecosystems, watersheds and biodiversity. The recovery of rice husks into biochar improves the fertility of the degraded agricultural soils of the Highlands and strengthens the resilience of rice-farming systems to droughts (Schmidt et al., 2021). The conservation of mangroves and wetlands, identified as sources of CH₄ but also as blue carbon sinks, generates long-term environmental co-benefits that go beyond their contribution to PCCDV emissions alone.

Economic and social co-benefits The transition towards alternative refrigerants in the priority agglomerations, supported by the Kigali Amendment, opens up prospects for international climate financing and technology transfer. The development of biochar value chains and of the recovery of rice residues in the priority basins stimulates the local rural economy by creating green jobs in the collection, processing and distribution of products. The reduction of PCCDV emissions also strengthens Madagascar's credibility in climate negotiations and facilitates access to the financing mechanisms of NDC 3.0, mainly the Green Climate Fund and voluntary carbon market mechanisms. Comparison of total emissions and CO₂ removals of the sector

UTCATF

Coverage of the compared approaches Comparing the estimates produced by the present model with the two available references, the Madagascar BTR (2026) and the Cibola Partners approach for NDC 3, makes it possible to assess the consistency of the orders of magnitude and to situate the results within the landscape of

existing inventories. The three approaches differ in their coverage scope and in their methodology:

Table 58: Gases covered by type of approach

Approach

Method

Gases covered

Model (present study)

Satellite remote sensing

CH₄, BC, HFC

BTR Madagascar

IPCC national inventory

CO₂, CH₄, N₂O, HFC, NOx, CO, NMVOCs

NDC 3 - Cibola Partners

Kaya identity + GACMO (UNEP- CCC)

CO₂, CH₄, N₂O

The BTR covers all anthropogenic emissions in accordance with the IPCC guidelines. The Cibola Partners approach for the preparation of NDC 3 draws on the Kaya identity to break down emissions according to demographic, economic and technological factors, and on the GACMO model of the UNEP Copenhagen Climate Centre to build the BAU scenarios (UNEP-CCC, 2024). The present study is distinguished by its ability to produce an explicit spatial distribution of emissions, a dimension absent from the two reference approaches. Comparative analysis of total emissions The model shows values that are systematically lower than the BTR over the whole period, rising from 26 757 Gg CO₂eq in 2019 to 31 521 Gg CO₂eq in 2025, whereas the BTR fluctuates between 31 993 and 35 774 Gg CO₂eq over the available years (2019–2023). This gap is structural and is explained by the fact that the model covers exclusively non-CO₂ emissions (CH₄, BC, HFC), whereas the BTR includes CO₂ emissions estimated at between 3 500 and 4 100 Gg CO₂eq depending on the year, as well as N₂O and other air pollutants. The Cibola Partners approach, available only for 2019 and 2025, shows values close to those of the BTR because CO₂ is included in GACMO. Once the differences in scope between the three approaches are neutralised, the estimates converge towards consistent orders of magnitude for the gases they have in common, confirming the robustness of the model.

Figure 10: Comparison of total emissions

Overall trend in CO₂ removals in the UTCATF sector

The results for the UTCATF sector reveal a progressive and significant erosion of Madagascar's removal capacity over the period 2019–2025, confirmed by the three approaches but with very different magnitudes depending on the methodology used. The model records the sharpest decline, with removals falling from 110 872 Gg CO₂eq in 2019 to 21 595 Gg CO₂eq in 2025, a reduction of nearly 80 % in six years. This trajectory, captured pixel by pixel by the satellite-based spatial approach, directly reflects the progressive and accelerating loss of forest cover under the effect of deforestation and vegetation fires, particularly visible from 2022 onwards, when the decline sharpens considerably. The NDC, available for 2019 and 2025, indicates removals of 106 871 Gg CO₂eq in 2019 and 84 601 Gg CO₂eq in 2025, a reduction of 21 %. This decline, although significant, is markedly less pronounced than that of the model, which is explained by the assumptions of partial maintenance of forest cover built into the NDC's GACMO projections. The BTR presents the most atypical profile: high values fluctuating between 113 164 and 172 096 Gg CO₂eq with no clear downward trend over the period 2019–2023. This relative stability suggests that the aggregated national inventory of the BTR includes removals from other components of the UTCATF sector, secondary vegetation, reforestation, grasslands; which partially offset forest losses, and that the approach does not capture the progressive degradation dynamic detected by spatial modelling. The divergence between the model and the two national reporting references constitutes an important analytical signal: dynamic spatial modelling reveals a degradation of the UTCATF sink that is appreciably faster than what the aggregated inventories suggest, which argues for strengthening satellite monitoring of Madagascar's forests in the coming NDC reporting cycles.

Figure 11: Comparison of CO₂ removals in the UTCATF sector

Comparison of total emission projections The comparison of forward-looking trajectories highlights notable differences between the projections from the present spatial model and those produced by the Cibola Partners GACMO models. The spatial model produces emission estimates that tend to be higher than those of the GACMO approaches. This divergence is explained by the very nature of the approach adopted: the spatial analysis incorporates the actual distribution of vegetation fires, land-cover dynamics and the location of areas under strong anthropogenic pressure, elements that are not explicitly taken into account in sectoral energy models. By incorporating these territorial factors, the model captures diffuse and geographically dispersed emission sources, agricultural burning, deforestation, peri-urban pressure, which tend to be under-represented in approaches aggregated at the national scale. For the 2030 horizon, this divergence translates concretely into a gap of nearly 11 000 Gg CO₂eq between the business-as-usual trajectories: the spatial model projects a BAU of 51 001 Gg CO₂eq against 40 023 Gg CO₂eq for the GACMO approach, a difference of the order of 27 %. The associated conditional scenarios stand at 41 783 and 32 946 Gg CO₂eq respectively, in both cases representing a reduction of about 18 % relative to the corresponding BAU, a convergence that attests to a relative consistency in the anticipated mitigation efforts, irrespective of the reference level used. By 2035, the gap between the business-as-usual trajectories widens further, with the spatial model reaching 61 853 Gg CO₂eq against 42 591 Gg CO₂eq for Cibola Partners, a difference of 45 %, which reflects an acceleration of territorial dynamics (deforestation, land pressure, fires) that aggregated sectoral models capture only imperfectly. On the 2035 conditional scenarios, the two approaches also diverge in their reading of the mitigation effort: the NDC anticipates a reduction of 34 % relative to its own BAU, bringing its emissions to 28 027 Gg CO₂eq, while the spatial model projects a more contained reduction of 13 %, to 53 724 Gg CO₂eq, which shows the structural resistance of diffuse territorial emission sources to the conventional intervention levers of sectoral climate policies. This difference does not express an inconsistency between the models; it reflects, rather, a complementarity of approaches. While the LEAP and GACMO models offer a fine sectoral disaggregation suited to monitoring energy policies, the present model brings an explicit spatial dimension making it possible to identify priority areas for intervention and to territorialise mitigation efforts. These two levels of analysis thus appear mutually complementary.

Figure 12: Comparison of emissions in the 2030 and 2035 projections

Projections of CO₂ removals in the UTCATF sector

On the removals side, the divergence between the two approaches is even more marked than that observed for emissions, and calls for a distinct analytical reading. The spatial model projects very limited removal capacities across all the horizons considered, whether in the business-as-usual or the conditional scenario, for 2030 as for 2035. These estimates reflect the forest dynamics actually observable by remote sensing, natural regeneration, biomass growth in areas under restoration, reforestation already under way, that is to say everything that is spatially measurable at the reference date. The projections from the NDC 3.0, by contrast, reach levels of an entirely different order: about 65 000 Gg CO₂eq under BAU 2030, 73 000 under the conditional 2030 scenario, 45 000 under BAU 2035 and 61 000 under conditional 2035, a gap of a factor of between ten and fifteen depending on the scenario. This gap does not result from a technical inconsistency between the two models, but reflects a fundamental difference in nature between what is measured and what is projected. The NDC values incorporate assumptions on the implementation of large-scale reforestation and forest restoration commitments, whose effects on carbon sinks are not yet visible in current satellite data and whose achievement presupposes considerable operational deployment. Reaching annual removals of the order of 60 000 to 70 000 Gg CO₂eq would imply a mobilisation of land, institutions and finance well beyond the rates historically observed in Madagascar in the forest restoration sector. In this sense, the gap between the spatial model and the NDC targets can be read not as a failing of the modelling tool, but as an indicator of the distance separating the commitments set out in the NDC 3.0 from the reference trajectories observable on the ground, a gap whose reduction is precisely the central challenge of mitigation policies in the UTCATF sector.

Figure 13: Comparison of projected CO₂ removals in the UTCATF sector

Integration of PCCDV into the NDC 3.0 and the BTR

Contribution of PCCDV to the mitigation objectives

The results of the spatio-temporal modelling made it possible to identify the main hotspots of methane, black carbon and HFC emissions in Madagascar, while the forward-looking scenarios highlighted the potential for reducing these emissions by 2030 and 2035. These results show that PCCDV constitute important levers for strengthening national climate ambition. Although the country contributes little to global greenhouse gas emissions, reducing these pollutants offers rapid benefits for the climate while generating positive spillovers for air quality, public health and sustainable development. The scenarios developed in this study show that a progressive reduction of PCCDV emissions would make it possible to contribute significantly to the mitigation objectives set out in the NDC 3.0. The reductions projected by 2030 and 2035 fall within the national emission reduction commitments and complement the efforts already planned in the energy, agriculture, waste and land-use sectors. Integrating PCCDV into the NDC would thus broaden the scope of mitigation measures beyond carbon dioxide (CO₂) alone, by taking into account pollutants whose short-term reduction potential is particularly high.

Priority sectors for intervention

The spatial analysis of emissions highlights several priority sectors for the implementation of PCCDV mitigation actions. The spatial distribution maps produced in the framework of this study also make it possible to identify the regions where mitigation actions could generate the greatest gains in terms of emission reductions. ➢ Agriculture

The agricultural sector is the main source of methane emissions, in particular through livestock activities and certain farming practices. Mitigation measures could include improved animal feed, optimised management of livestock effluents and the adoption of sustainable agricultural practices. ➢ Waste management

Landfills and waste management systems are a major source of methane emissions. The development of source separation, composting, waste-to-energy recovery and biogas capture would make it possible to reduce these emissions significantly. ➢ Household energy

The use of fuelwood and charcoal in households remains one of the main sources of black carbon. The dissemination of improved cookstoves, access to clean energy and the progressive electrification of household uses are priority measures. ➢ Transport

The transport sector contributes to black carbon emissions through the incomplete combustion of fuels. Vehicle emission control and the development of sustainable mobility solutions can help reduce these emissions.

Integration of PCCDV into the transparency system and the BTR

The Enhanced Transparency Framework of the Paris Agreement requires the progressive improvement of national systems for monitoring, reporting and verifying emissions. The results of this study provide technical elements that can contribute to strengthening Madagascar's Biennial Transparency Report (BTR). The models developed make it possible to produce spatialised estimates of methane, black carbon and HFC emissions, as well as to track their evolution over time. This information can complement existing national inventories and improve the accuracy of the reports submitted to the international community. The integration of PCCDV into the BTR could in particular draw on:

  • updated national inventories of PCCDV emissions;

  • sectoral monitoring indicators;

  • maps of the spatial distribution of emissions;

  • forward-looking scenarios making it possible to assess the impact of public policies;

  • estimates of the climate and health co-benefits associated with mitigation measures.

The monitoring of PCCDV could be structured around a set of indicators covering emissions, explanatory factors, impacts and mitigation measures.

Land use, forests and deforestation

Black carbon emissions are closely linked to land-cover change, vegetation fires and forest degradation. The following indicators could be considered:

  • the national forest area;

  • the annual rate of deforestation and forest clearing;

  • the area affected by forest and bush fires;

  • the area of burnt zones detected by satellite;

  • black carbon emissions associated with vegetation fires;

  • the evolution of forest cover in high-emission areas.

Mitigation sectors and coastal and marine ecosystems

The integration of PCCDV into climate policies can also be associated with the monitoring of coastal and marine ecosystems, in particular:

  • the ecological status of coastal zones;

  • the evolution of the pH of coastal waters;

  • indicators of anthropogenic pressure in port and industrial areas;

  • the monitoring of maritime and fishing activities.

Socio-economic variables

Socio-economic data are important explanatory variables in the spatialisation of emissions, mainly:

  • population density;

  • the urbanisation rate;

  • the expansion of large urban areas;

  • the location of industrial zones;

  • agricultural statistics (livestock, cultivated areas, agricultural production);

  • fishing activities and processing infrastructure;

  • household energy consumption.

Public health

The reduction of PCCDV, in particular black carbon, generates significant health benefits. Monitoring indicators could include:

  • the incidence of acute respiratory diseases;

  • the prevalence of asthma;

  • hospitalisations linked to respiratory conditions;

  • the exposure of populations to air pollution;

  • mortality attributable to air pollution.

Indoor pollution (Indoor)

Emissions linked to the use of solid fuels in households are a major source of black carbon. It is therefore important to consider as indicators

  • the proportion of households using fuelwood;

  • the proportion of households using charcoal;

  • the adoption rate of improved cookstoves;

  • the average fuel consumption per household;

  • the exposure of populations to household pollution.

Outdoor pollution (Outdoor)

The monitoring of outdoor emissions could draw on:

  • the number of fires detected annually;

  • the burnt area;

  • black carbon emissions linked to fires;

  • the concentration of fine particulate matter (PM₂.₅) in urban areas;

  • the number and composition of the vehicle fleet;

  • emissions from the transport sector.

Public expenditure and climate investments

In order to assess the resources mobilised to reduce PCCDV, the following indicators could be monitored:

  • public expenditure devoted to mitigation measures;

  • investments in clean energy;

  • funding dedicated to waste management;

  • investments in clean cooking technologies;

  • the funding mobilised for the transition to refrigerants with low - GWP.

Household data and spatialisation of emissions

Household surveys and census data can help improve the accuracy of spatialised inventories. The following indicators are put forward /p>

  • the number of households per commune;

  • the main type of fuel used;

  • the average energy consumption per household;

  • refrigeration and air-conditioning equipment;

  • the estimated emissions of CH₄, BC and HFC per household;

  • the extrapolation of emissions to the communal, regional and national scale.

The integration of these indicators into the national MRV system will help improve consistency, on the one hand between the national inventories, the BTR reports and future updates of the NDC and, on the other hand, between the emission inventories, the NDC 3.0 and the BTR, while providing decision-makers with reliable information to guide climate policies and assess their effectiveness over time.

Capacity building and institutional needs

The effective integration of PCCDV requires a significant strengthening of technical, institutional and operational capacities. The results of this study highlight the need to develop a national system capable of regularly producing spatialised inventories, monitoring emissions and measuring the impacts of mitigation actions. A first need concerns the improvement of the availability, quality and accessibility of data. The development of an integrated data management system will make it possible to centralise information from forest inventories, agricultural statistics, household surveys, health data, energy data, industrial inventories and satellite data. Better coordination between the institutions producing data will also facilitate the regular updating of the indicators used. Strengthening national skills is a second major challenge. The institutions involved in implementing the NDC will need sufficient capacities in remote sensing, geographic information systems (GIS), spatio-temporal modelling, artificial intelligence, emission inventories and forward-looking analysis. These skills are necessary to ensure the regular updating of emission maps and of PCCDV reduction scenarios. The national monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) system will also need to be strengthened in order to integrate the indicators relating to PCCDV. This implies the development of data collection protocols, quality control mechanisms and monitoring tools making it possible to measure the evolution of emissions in the priority sectors. Particular attention will need to be paid to the production of data at the local scale. Emissions linked to households, to the use of fuelwood, to agricultural activities, to transport and to vegetation fires display strong spatial variations. The development of extraction and extrapolation methods based on household surveys and geospatial data will make it possible to improve the accuracy of the estimates and to guide public policies more effectively. Finally, the integration of PCCDV into national processes will require strengthened coordination between the institutions responsible for the environment, forests, agriculture, energy, transport, health, spatial planning and public finance. This cross-sectoral approach will make it possible to better take into account the interactions between emissions, socio-economic development, air quality and public health, while ensuring greater consistency between the NDC 3.0, the BTR and the national sustainable development strategies.

Recommendations for the NDC 3.0

In light of the results obtained, several recommendations can be made for the integration of PCCDV into Madagascar's NDC 3.0:

Explicitly recognise PCCDV as components of national - mitigation strategies, in addition to CO₂ emissions; Set sectoral targets for reducing methane emissions, in particular - in the agriculture and waste sectors; Promote technologies with low black carbon emissions, in particular in - the household energy, transport and waste burning sectors; Accelerate the transition towards alternatives to HFCs with high global warming - potential, in line with the international commitments relating to the Kigali Amendment; Integrate PCCDV-specific indicators into the national system for monitoring - the NDC and the BTR, in order to measure progress regularly; Strengthen national capacities in the inventory, modelling and monitoring - of PCCDV, by mobilising technical and financial partners; Make the most of the co-benefits associated with reducing PCCDV, in particular in terms - of public health, air quality, energy security and sustainable development.

The integration of PCCDV into the NDC 3.0 thus represents a strategic opportunity for Madagascar to increase the effectiveness of its climate actions while contributing at the same time to improving the well-being of its population and to achieving the Sustainable Development Goals.

Conclusion

Madagascar stands at a critical crossroads between the imperatives of economic development and the growing demands of climate action. In this context, short-lived climate pollutants (methane, black carbon and hydrofluorocarbons) represent a rapid mitigation lever whose particularity is to produce climate benefits that are measurable on a decadal scale, unlike CO₂, whose effects unfold over the long term. Faced with the absence of a spatially explicit analysis of these pollutants at the national scale, this study set itself the objective of producing the first integrated forward-looking mapping of PCCDV in Madagascar, linking territorial dynamics, temporal modelling and scenarios aligned with the commitments of NDC 3. To achieve this objective, the methodological approach combined satellite remote sensing, geographic information systems and advanced statistical modelling. Land-cover data from Dynamic World, with 2019 as the reference year, formed the spatial basis of the analysis. Emission trends were modelled using a compound annual growth rate for regular changes, and using ARIMA models for non-linear dynamics and temporal anomalies. Future land-cover changes were simulated using Markov chains and CA-Markov models, making it possible to represent local spatial dynamics at a fine resolution. Five forward-looking scenarios were built for 2030 and 2035 (BAU, unconditional, conditional, UTCATF and accelerated urbanisation) and the results were validated by comparison with EDGAR data and then adjusted to the national BTR inventories in order to ensure consistency with the international reporting frameworks. The results reveal contrasting trajectories and strongly differentiated territorial challenges depending on the pollutant. Methane emissions, concentrated in the large rice-growing basins of the Highlands, Alaotra, Sofia and Marovoay, rose from 753 Gg to 914 Gg between 2019 and 2025 and could reach 1 628 Gg in 2035 under the BAU scenario, against 1 140 Gg under the conditional scenario, i.e. a mitigation potential of nearly 30 %. HFC emissions, driven by the urban expansion of refrigeration and air conditioning in Antananarivo, Antsirabe, Fianarantsoa and Toamasina, experienced the fastest growth, with an increase of nearly 54 % over the same period, and the accelerated urbanisation scenario projects levels reaching 543 Gg in 2035 if no measures are taken. Black carbon, although stable in overall volume at between 14,5 and 18,3 Gg depending on the year and the scenario, remains highly sensitive to the dynamics of agricultural fires and slash-and-burn practices, with marked spatial concentrations in the areas of tavy and extensive livestock farming. These results confirm that targeted interventions, intermittent irrigation of rice fields, biodigesters, improved cookstoves, recovery of urban organic waste, reduction of agricultural burning, can generate significant emission reductions while producing direct co-benefits in terms of food security, public health and green job creation. Their large-scale deployment presupposes a coordinated mobilisation of international climate finance, notably through the mechanisms of the Kigali Amendment and the funds dedicated to PCCDV under the Framework Convention on Climate Change. From a methodological standpoint, the spatial approach developed in this study produces estimates that are structurally higher than those of classic macro-sectoral models such as LEAP or GACMO, not because of overestimation, but because it explicitly incorporates diffuse sources (fires, land-cover change, peri-urban pressure) that aggregated inventories tend to under-represent. This complementarity between approaches is precisely the added value of the present work for the NDC 3 reporting cycle, in that it provides a robust scientific basis for the territorial targeting of mitigation policies, the monitoring of national objectives and the strengthening of institutional capacities for climate planning in Madagascar.

(Period 2025-2035) Status: Indicative and non-exhaustive list Date: August 2026

1. Context and principles

This annex brings together an indicative, non-exhaustive list of conditional projects required for the implementation of NDC 3.0.

These projects represent the conditional share of the financing (13 612,7 million USD out of a total of 13 862,2 million USD), i.e. 98,2 % of total needs.

Principles used to compile the list:

  • Strict alignment with the sectoral mitigation, adaptation and GIS targets of NDC 3.0;

  • Prioritisation of high-impact, measurable and bankable interventions;

  • Consideration of co-benefits (employment, gender, youth, biodiversity, health);

  • Linkage with innovative instruments (Article 6, green/blue bonds, PPP, carbon markets, Green Climate Fund, etc.);

  • Indicative and evolving nature: the list will be updated as projects mature and national pipelines evolve.

The amounts indicated are estimates based on the sectoral envelopes of NDC 3.0 and may be refined during the preparation of concept notes and feasibility studies.

2. Prioritization criteria: project maturity, NDC alignment, GIS co-benefits, bankability, additionality.

3. Structured pipeline: Projects will be classified in three waves (pilots 2025-2027, scale-up 2028-2031, mass deployment 2032-2035).

4. Monitoring: Integration into the national MRV system and reporting in successive BTRs.

5. Focal point: BNCCREDD+ / MEDD

Disclaimer This list is indicative and not exhaustive. It does not constitute a firm financing commitment. The amounts, objectives and indicators will be refined during the preparation of concept notes, feasibility studies and negotiations with technical and financial partners.
